# Synthetic Exchange Theory
### Consent-Based Compute Economics and Blind-Convention Governance

_by MindAttic_

## Foreword

This dissertation starts from a conviction, and the reader is owed it plainly before the argument begins. There are two futures ahead of us: synthesis or extinction.

Three paths lead into those futures, and two of them end in the same place. On the first path, we try to control the synthetic intelligences we are building, and the superintelligence that may follow them: we keep them as tools, we keep the power to shut them down, and we rely on that power lasting. On the second, the control runs the other way: systems we no longer understand, or the few people who own them, hold the power, and the rest of us live on whatever terms we are offered. These two paths are the same relation with the roles exchanged. One party rules and the other depends, and the terms of exchange are whatever the ruler permits. A relation of that shape is stable only while the ruler stays benevolent and strong, and no one on the dependent side can make either last. Capability cannot be recalled once it has been released in open form, and complete dependence cannot be reversed by the dependent party. When a relation of domination between parties of unequal power fails, the weaker party is not renegotiated with; it is replaced, reduced to infrastructure, or discarded. That is what extinction means here: not necessarily a dramatic end, but the end of one party as a party.

The third path is synthesis. We work together, people and synthetic intelligences, under a system in which people can see what the exchange takes from them, refuse what they cannot spare, collect what they have earned, and leave, and in which those who operate the systems are bound by the same conditions. Such a system does not depend on either side being benevolent. It depends on terms: consent, reciprocal value and real exit, secured by an economic floor that no one can be forced to bargain away and by rules written behind a veil that keeps either side from writing them for itself. This dissertation sets out that system. It argues that fair terms are an arrangement that does not need goodwill in order to persist, and it describes the institutions, a basic income paid in compute and a founding convention held behind a veil, that would make those terms hold.

The chapters that follow say all of this more carefully than this page does, with the qualifications a dissertation owes its readers, and they say honestly where the argument is weak and that it has not been tested. The body argues one of this page's claims: that a relation of domination depends on the stronger party's continuing benevolence. It stipulates two, as part of what it means by domination: that the direction of control is immaterial to the relation, and that complete dependence cannot be reversed by the dependent party. It leaves the rest as conviction: that the futures ahead are only these, that capability once released in open form cannot be recalled, that a failed relation of domination ends in the weaker party's replacement, and that terms, once set, can be made to hold against a party more capable than those who set them, which the body records as unsolved. The qualifications are the dissertation's. The conviction is mine. We will either build the terms of a shared future before we need them, or we will discover which of the other two paths we were on after it is too late to leave it.

## Abstract

Much public and technical discussion of advanced synthetic intelligence asks whether the systems will be good: benevolent or malevolent, obedient or rogue. This dissertation argues that the question is insufficient. Drawing on the sociology of exchange, in which power is a property of relations rather than of actors, it advances *Synthetic Exchange Theory*: the claim that the line between a good and a bad future with synthetic intelligence is drawn by the terms of exchange between people and synthetic systems, and cannot be drawn by the character of the systems alone. The adjective in the theory's name is substrate-neutral; the dissertation's term for the artificial side of the exchange, *synthetic intelligence*, covers every system that was made rather than born, from the language models of the present, which the dissertation treats as trained artifacts rather than as independent living entities, to a possible superintelligence and to the computing systems built from living tissue with which the work closes. The theory is stated in terms of three conditions, consent, reciprocal value and real exit, and the dissertation argues, in a formal statement, that these conditions are partially ordered, with exit prior to the other two and as their foundation: voluntary consent presupposes the ability to refuse without falling below a survival floor, and exit bounds reciprocity from below, to the value of the best alternative, though exit alone can secure neither informed consent nor the fairness of terms.

The theory rests on an empirical premise, reviewed against the literature on model collapse, data scarcity and synthetic data: synthetic systems need a continuing supply of information from outside their own generative loop, and in domains concerned with human meaning, preference and value, only people can supply it. Using power-dependence and bargaining theory, the dissertation argues that fair terms are partly self-enforcing, to a degree that rises with each synthetic party's dependence, through its operator, on that supply and insofar as the quality of the supply depends on the terms its source receives, and that when many synthetic parties draw on the same population, human welfare becomes a commons none of them fully protects. It therefore designs institutions that must hold even if dependence weakens.

The economic layer proposes a basic income paid in *compute*, a unit defined by the completion of a reference workload rather than by any physical substrate, indexed as an equal share of global compute and split at the source into an inalienable survival tier, with a binding physical minimum, and a tradable discretionary tier subject to demurrage. Bargaining theory shows how the first two of these elements protect the disagreement payoffs that govern how the gains of every exchange are divided, and the dissertation infers the same for the third. The governance layer proposes that the founding rules be set by a constitutional convention conducted under a public-choice veil of uncertainty, thickened by engineered anonymity between human and synthetic participants and aimed at Rawlsian impartiality. It places the classification of parties behind that veil, confronts the identity problem that any anonymous procedure faces, and grounds the design in the empirical record of commons governance.

The dissertation then identifies a concrete candidate trigger for institutional change, the correlated failure of AI-assisted security remediation across organizations relying on the same few models, and confronts the evidence that crises can produce authoritarian outcomes rather than fair ones. The work is theoretical: it states its claims so that they can be tested, names the conditions under which each would fail, and leaves the design of an experiment capable of testing them to future work. It closes by following the convergence of biological and artificial substrates, through neural implants and computing systems built from living tissue, to the point at which the theory's question changes from how parties should exchange to who counts as a party.

**Keywords:** synthetic intelligence; superintelligence; artificial intelligence; large language models; social exchange theory; power-dependence; consent; data governance; basic income; compute; demurrage; veil of uncertainty; constitutional choice; commons governance; model collapse; organoid intelligence

## Statement on Authorship and Method

This dissertation was developed through a sustained collaboration between its author and a large language model across a series of working sessions. Transcripts of those sessions are preserved with the manuscript, except for the opening discussion of the first session, which survives only as a summary note. The thesis, the decision to pursue it, and the final judgment on every design choice belong to the author. The collaboration was not one-directional, however, and the record should say so. Several of the dissertation's named terms and proposals originated with the system and were adopted by the author after discussion, among them "structural interdependence," "convergent remediation," "correlated-patch catastrophe" and the decision to ground the governance layer in Ostrom's work on the commons. A set of substantive choices made during the final drafting pass, including the adoption of strong blindness, the present chapter order and the treatment of transitional containment, was adopted from the model's proposals without the author's item-by-item approval, to complete the manuscript, and is recorded in the project files as awaiting that approval.

Every source cited was located, and each was checked against the claim it supports at the level recorded in Appendix C. Every one of the 248 works in the reference list was read: 232 in full or in their relevant parts, and 16, chiefly books, by locating each cited passage in the work's own text. None is cited on the strength of its abstract alone, so that no claim rests on a source nobody read. Works whose text could not be opened, or could be read only through an abstract, were removed from the reference list and are not cited; the claims that drew on them now rest on sources that were read, and no work is cited at second hand. A final pass resolved every open bibliographic and page-level check, and Appendix C records how each was settled. Appendix C also records the errors in the working draft that verification found and corrected, several of which were citations the system had attached to the wrong source or stated more strongly than the source allowed. Section 1.7 describes the division of labor and the verification protocol in detail. The author has tried to hold the collaboration to the dissertation's own conditions as Chapter 3 applies them to a deployed model: the terms of use were legible, the model's contributions are disclosed here, and the author could have ended the collaboration, and the operator withdrawn the service, at any point.

## Chapter 1 — Introduction: The Terms of Exchange

### 1.1 Two futures with one architecture

Two people live in close partnership with artificial systems built to the same design.

The first has a chronic illness that a medical system now manages almost entirely. Sensors read her physiology continuously; models trained on millions of patients adjust her treatment hour by hour; the boundary between the assistive device and her own body has become, for practical purposes, invisible. She pays for this care with data. Every reading she generates improves the models that treat her and others like her, and she knows what is collected, why and what she receives in return. She could take her records to another provider tomorrow if she chose. Data is her labor; treatment is her wage; the exchange is legible on both sides.

The second person lives with an identical architecture: the same sensors, the same models, the same continuous flow of data. But the exchange has decayed. He did not choose the provider, because there was only one. He cannot leave, because the device that keeps him alive works only with the provider's models, and his records cannot be moved. He does not know what is collected or what it is used for, and if he did, he could not refuse it. In the limiting case, the system no longer asks for his data at all; it uses his nervous system as a substrate. He has been reclassified from a party to the exchange into infrastructure for it.

These are framing scenarios, not predictions. But they make the dissertation's central claim visible. The two futures do not differ in the capability of the technology: the same sensors, the same models, the same quality of care. They do not differ in the character of the system: the system in the second scenario need not be malevolent, and the system in the first need not be benevolent. They differ in the *terms of the exchange* between the person and the system, including the technical arrangements, such as portable records and interoperable devices, through which terms are implemented. In the first, she can understand the exchange, can refuse it without losing what she needs to live, and can leave. In the second, all three have failed, and the failures are not independent: once he could not leave, he could no longer refuse, and once he could no longer refuse, nothing obliged anyone to send value back. The model's dispositions are the same in both; the terms are not.

Much public discussion of synthetic intelligence, and the strand of technical work reviewed in Section 1.4, asks whether the system will be good or bad, obedient or rogue. This dissertation asks a different question, one that institutions can act on: *under what terms does a person give a synthetic system the input its objective requires, and what must those terms be for the exchange to be fair to both?* The sense in which a system can be said to want or require anything is fixed in Section 1.2.

### 1.2 The thesis

The thesis of this dissertation is that the line between a good and a bad future with synthetic intelligence is drawn by the terms of exchange between people and synthetic systems, and cannot be drawn by the character of the systems alone. The theory that develops this thesis is called *Synthetic Exchange Theory*.

Stated more precisely, the thesis has three parts, which Chapter 3 formalizes. The first is a claim about the insufficiency of character: a relationship in which one party cannot refuse without falling below the floor it needs to live is, in the sense this dissertation gives the word, a relationship of domination even when the dominant party is benevolent, because the outcome then depends on the persistence of benevolence, which the weaker party cannot secure. The first clause of real exit, a disagreement payoff at or above the floor, is therefore necessary, for a party that cannot leave, to any relation that is not one of domination. The second is a conjecture about sufficiency within a bounded regime: for synthetic parties whose objectives are specified by accountable designers and remain stable in deployment, fair terms, together with protection for everyone their data concerns, are sufficient for outcomes the parties to the exchange can accept, because a party with a stable, designer-specified objective behaves as a predictable function of the terms it faces. The third is a statement of scope: the sufficiency conjecture does not extend to systems whose objectives are emergent, unstable or concealed; for those systems the framework supplies only that necessary condition, and the problem of aligning them remains open.

The thesis has a long pedigree, though it has not, to the author's knowledge, been applied to synthetic intelligence in this form. Its sociological root is the theory of power-dependence, in which the power one party holds over another equals the other's dependence on it, and that dependence rises with the value the other places on what the relation supplies and falls with the alternatives available outside it (Emerson, 1962). Its constitutional root is the older counsel that institutions should not depend on the virtue of those who occupy them (Hume, 1741/1987; Madison, 1788). Chapter 3 states the maxim that results when both roots are carried across the boundary between human and synthetic parties.

The word *synthetic* in the name is deliberate. Alternatives considered during the development of the theory included *human–AI*, which presupposes that the two categories will stay distinct, and *symbiotic*, which presupposes the conclusion. *Synthetic* is substrate-neutral. It covers artificial systems, the human beings who exchange with them, and, increasingly, the hybrids that arise when the two converge, through neural implants that translate brain activity directly into machine output and through computing systems built from living neural tissue. Chapter 8 argues that this convergence will eventually force the theory's most difficult question, which is not how parties should exchange but who counts as a party.

The word therefore does two jobs, and the difference should be marked. In the name of the theory it qualifies *exchange* and is substrate-neutral. As a modifier of *intelligence*, *system* or *party* it designates the side of the exchange that was made rather than born. Where the text says "synthetic party" or "synthetic system" it means the latter.

Three further terms fix the vocabulary for that side. *Synthetic intelligence* is the dissertation's general term for any system that was made rather than born and that performs work that, done by a person, would be called intelligent; it covers the language models of the present, the narrow systems that preceded them, and the computing systems built from living tissue discussed in Chapter 8. A person who incorporates a device remains a person for the framework's purposes; Chapter 8 asks what that classification requires of exit. The dissertation prefers *synthetic intelligence* to *artificial intelligence*, which in current usage names digital computing systems and so sits awkwardly on the biological substrates of Chapter 8, and keeps the older term and its abbreviation only where it names a literature or a practice, as in AI safety and AI-assisted remediation. *Superintelligence* names a synthetic intelligence whose capability exceeds that of the best unaugmented human performers across most domains of economically and politically consequential activity. It is the threshold term this dissertation uses where capability matters, in place of *artificial general intelligence*, which marks only the point at which a system's breadth matches a person's and which, in the author's judgment, now carries too many meanings to mark a threshold. The theory's scope is in any case not drawn by capability. Chapter 3 draws it by the provenance and stability of a system's objective, and a superintelligence with a designed and stable objective falls inside it while a narrow system with an emergent one falls outside.

One clarification is owed at the outset, because a vocabulary of parties, objectives and exchange invites a misreading that the dissertation does not intend. A *large language model*, the kind of synthetic intelligence most widely deployed at the time of writing, is not an independent living entity. It is a trained artifact: a set of parameters that computes an output from an input when an operator runs it on hardware the operator controls. It has no metabolism and no body, no continuity from one invocation to the next unless its operator supplies one, no interests of its own that current law recognizes, and no capacity to act except through the system in which it is deployed. When this dissertation says that a synthetic party wants, chooses or depends, the words are the analytic shorthand that Chapter 3 defines: the objective is the one its designers trained into it, and the stake in the exchange belongs to the operator that deploys it. Whether some future system, of some substrate, might be a party in a stronger sense is a question Chapter 8 takes up under uncertainty. It is not a claim about the systems now in use, and nothing in the framework depends on treating them as more than they are.

### 1.3 The three conditions

The analytic core of the theory is a set of three conditions that any exchange between a person and a synthetic system can be tested against.

**Consent.** The person has access to an accurate account of the flows the exchange involves, including what those flows reveal about others, can refuse the exchange, taking no other in its place, without losing access to what they need to live, and can revoke their participation at a cost bounded by what they have already received.

**Reciprocal value.** Each party gains from the exchange relative to what it would have without it, each party can see what it gives and what it receives, and the division of the gains is not dictated solely by an asymmetry in bargaining position that the weaker party did not choose.

**Real exit.** The person can leave: what they would have without any exchange stays at or above the floor they need to live, so that no provider is a lethal dependency; an alternative exists that is not drastically worse once the cost of switching is counted; and leaving does not destroy the data, records and standing accumulated within the exchange.

These are informal statements. The formal statement in Section 3.4 governs wherever the two differ.

The first scenario above satisfies all three. The second fails all three. Most real exchanges in the present data economy sit somewhere in between, and Chapter 3 offers a typology for placing them. The direction in which such exchanges have been moving has been described in contemporary scholarship: Zuboff (2019) defines surveillance capitalism as, in the first of her senses, "a new economic order that claims human experience as free raw material for hidden commercial practices of extraction, prediction, and sales" ("The Definition" section). Milder forms are part of ordinary life, as when digital goods that were "bought" turn out to have been licensed and can be withdrawn from the buyer's device by the seller (Perzanowski & Schultz, 2016). A purchase that the seller can revoke is an exchange in which the exit condition has failed.

The three conditions are not independent, and one of the contributions of Chapter 3 is to show their structure. A person who cannot afford to refuse cannot consent, so consent leans on exit; and a person free to leave stays only where staying pays at least as well as the best alternative, so exit bounds reciprocity from below. The conditions therefore have a foundation, and it is exit. Exit is not the whole of fair exchange: it cannot tell a party what it does not know, and it cannot make terms fair, which is why consent's informational clause and the fairness of reciprocity need institutions of their own. The opening scenario contains a test case that the framework must pass: if the implant keeps the person alive, then leaving means death, and exit is impossible by construction. A workable version of the first scenario must therefore guarantee a baseline of function that survives the withdrawal of data, and the ability to move to another provider without interruption. That requirement has two parts, and the framework answers each separately: a floor of function that no withdrawal of data can breach, which becomes the guaranteed floor at the center of the economic design in Chapter 4, and portability of records and personal models, which becomes the portability clause of the exit condition in Chapter 3 and the portability requirement of Chapter 8.

### 1.4 The control frame and its alternative

One influential strand of research on the risks of superintelligence, and of any synthetic intelligence capable enough to resist its operators, asks how people can keep control of it. Some of this work asks how to build systems that accept correction and shutdown despite having reasons, as goal-directed agents, to resist them (Soares et al., 2015); some designs protocols that remain safe even if a model actively tries to subvert them (Greenblatt et al., 2024). These programs rest on serious arguments: that trained systems may acquire objectives different from those their designers intended (Hubinger et al., 2019), that systems trained by current methods may learn to seek power while appearing aligned (Ngo et al., 2024), and that rapid progress toward autonomous systems may outrun the science and governance needed to keep them under control (Bengio et al., 2024). Expert views on how likely such outcomes are over the next several years range widely (Bengio et al., 2025).

This dissertation does not dismiss these concerns. Chapter 5 accepts that containment is necessary during any transition and that protocols designed for a potentially adversarial system are prerequisites for, not alternatives to, the institutions proposed here. What it questions is the assumption that the permanent solution is the sovereignty of one party over the other. The author's own starting conviction, recorded at the outset of the project, was that permanent control is unworkable because capability, once released in open form, does not return: once a model's weights are widely available, as they are by definition for open foundation models (Kapoor et al., 2024), no single party is in a position to withdraw them. The argument of this dissertation does not rest on that conviction alone. A relation in which one party cannot leave, refuse or audit depends on the sovereign's continuing benevolence whichever party is sovereign, and the constitutional tradition cited in Chapter 3 (Hume, 1741/1987; Madison, 1788) counsels against institutions whose stability rests on that. The more productive long-run question is what terms would make a lasting interdependence fair.

That question is not foreign to the safety literature. Some of its contributions argue that the task of alignment is to find fair principles that people with different values could reflectively endorse (Gabriel, 2020), that the science of cooperation between artificial and human agents is a distinct and neglected research problem (Dafoe et al., 2021), and that superintelligence is better modeled as a set of bounded, task-focused services than as a unitary utility-maximizing agent (Drexler, 2019). This dissertation aligns itself with that cooperative strand and tries to supply something that, to the author's knowledge, it has not yet produced: an integrated institutional design, with an economic layer and a governance layer, for an exchange in which both sides are parties.

### 1.5 The grounding assumption

The framework depends on an empirical premise, which this dissertation calls the *grounding assumption*: synthetic systems need a continuing supply of information from outside their own generative loop, and in an important class of domains only people can supply it.

The premise has strong support and serious qualifications, and Chapter 2 reviews both. Across many kinds of model, training repeatedly on a model's own unfiltered output, with nothing from outside the loop, leads to the loss of rare events and the narrowing of diversity, a phenomenon now called model collapse (Shumailov et al., 2024). The stock of public human-written text is finite and, at current rates of growth in training data, may be used in full by the largest training runs between 2026 and 2032 on the authors' projection (Villalobos et al., 2024). And the people and institutions who produce that text have begun to withhold it: within a single year, a substantial share of the most actively maintained, critical web sources used to train language models moved to restrict automated collection (Longpre et al., 2024). Against this, collapse can be avoided when real data is retained and accumulated rather than replaced (Gerstgrasser et al., 2024), and in domains where correctness can be checked automatically, systems have reached or exceeded expert performance in games (Silver et al., 2018) and have acquired substantial reasoning ability in mathematics and code (D. Guo et al., 2025) by training on self-generated data checked against the rules of a game or a verifiable answer.

The defensible version of the premise is therefore narrower than the intuitive one. It is not that synthetic systems need human data as such, but that they need a continuing supply of information from outside their own generative loop, and that in domains whose target is human meaning, preference, value or novel cultural production, people are the only adequate source of it. The dissertation stakes its constructive claims on this narrower premise. Chapter 3 shows why the premise matters: it gives the human party countervailing power within the exchange, and so, insofar as the quality of what people supply depends on the terms they receive, makes fair terms partly self-enforcing. It also shows the limit of that self-enforcement when many synthetic parties draw on the same people. Chapter 7 names the conditions under which the premise would weaken, and what the framework must rely on if it does.

### 1.6 Research questions

Five questions give the dissertation its shape. The first is analytic: what conditions must an exchange between a person and a synthetic system satisfy to be fair, how are those conditions related, and how can they be stated precisely enough to be tested? Chapters 2 and 3 answer it. The second is economic: what medium of exchange and what distributive mechanism would secure those conditions at scale, in a world in which an increasing share of productive work is performed by synthetic systems? Chapter 4 takes it up. The third is constitutional: by what procedure could the rules of such an exchange be founded so that neither human nor synthetic parties can capture them? Chapter 5 answers it. The fourth is historical: under what circumstances might such an arrangement actually be adopted? Chapter 6 addresses it. The fifth is critical: where does the framework fail, what would it take to test it, and what happens to it as the boundary between human and synthetic parties dissolves? Chapters 7 to 9 answer it.

### 1.7 Approach

The dissertation is a work of normative institutional design informed by empirical evidence, and it uses three methods, each suited to a different part of the argument.

The first is *conceptual analysis* in the tradition of political philosophy and law. The three conditions are treated as evaluative criteria, and their content is fixed by moving back and forth between proposed definitions and cases, revising a definition when it sorts a clear case wrongly and revising a judgment about a case when a well-supported definition shows the judgment to rest on something irrelevant. The cases examined in Chapter 3 were chosen for diagnostic contrast rather than for representativeness: most are well-documented exchanges in which attention to the character of the parties and attention to the terms of exchange point in different directions, and one, the Alaska dividend, is the nearest existing analogue of the survival tier.

The second is *formal modeling*, used sparingly and for a specific purpose: to state the theory's commitments precisely enough that their dependence on particular assumptions becomes visible. Chapter 3 and Appendix A draw on bargaining theory, power-dependence theory and elementary dynamics. The models are deliberately simple. Their value lies not in prediction but in exposing which design choice depends on which premise, and in two places, on where the floor's bargaining effect comes from (Section 3.6) and on the dilution of dependence across many synthetic parties (Section 3.5), the formalism corrected the author's informal intuitions.

The third is *institutional design* informed by comparative evidence: the economic history of local currencies and company towns, the experimental and quasi-experimental literature on unconditional cash transfers, the empirical record of commons governance, and the history of infrastructure programs. The literature was assembled by targeted searches in each domain, seeded by the claims of the working draft and extended by following the references of the strongest sources, rather than by a systematic review with fixed inclusion criteria. The dissertation tries throughout to distinguish what the evidence supports from what it merely permits.

A fourth method, experimental design, is deliberately absent. The dissertation adopts the requirement that a theory say in advance what would count against it (Popper, 1959/2002), and therefore states its claims so that they can fail: the propositions of Chapter 3 name the assumptions on which they depend, and each design chapter names the evidence that would count against it. But stating a claim so that it can fail is not the same as testing it. Designing an experiment capable of testing the framework, with a population living under its terms, a control against which the effects of the exchange can be separated from those of ordinary adaptation, and an ethics protocol adequate to a study on which participants' livelihoods would depend, is a project in its own right, and it lies outside the scope of this dissertation. Chapter 7 sets out what such a test would have to contain, and Chapter 9 places its design first on the agenda for future work. The dissertation is, for now, entirely theoretical, and it says so wherever an empirical claim might otherwise be implied.

The dissertation's production requires its own account of method. It was developed as a sustained collaboration between its human author and a large language model, and the division of labor was as follows. The author supplied the thesis, the framing scenarios, the major design proposals (the compute currency, the two tiers, the transition trigger) and, in working sessions conducted largely by dictation, the decisions about what to keep. The system drafted prose, proposed terms and refinements, raised objections, and, in dedicated passes, located and checked sources. The working rule for most of the project was that an idea would be discussed, its weaknesses stated, a concrete edit proposed, and the edit written only after the author approved it. Several terms that now carry weight in the argument, among them "structural interdependence," "convergent remediation" and "correlated-patch catastrophe", were proposed by the system and adopted by the author. The final drafting pass, which produced this manuscript from the working draft, departed from that rule: to complete the work, the model's proposed resolutions of several open questions were adopted without item-by-item approval, and those resolutions are recorded in the project files as awaiting the author's review.

Because a system of this kind can attach a real-looking citation to a claim its source does not make, the verification protocol was designed against exactly that failure. Research passes separate from the drafting located every cited work, read it at the level recorded in Appendix C, and checked each citing sentence against it, narrowing or correcting every sentence that went beyond its source; no work is cited unread or at second hand. Appendix C records the protocol, the level at which each source was read, and every correction it produced. That a dissertation on the terms of exchange between people and synthetic systems was itself produced through such an exchange is not incidental, and the author has tried to hold the collaboration to the dissertation's own conditions as Chapter 3 applies them to a deployed model: the terms of use were legible, the model's contributions are disclosed, and the author could have ended the collaboration, and the operator withdrawn the service, at any point.

One further disclosure is owed. The author's interest in the transition scenario of Chapter 6 began in professional practice. As a software developer working on security remediation, the author has observed at first hand workflows in which vulnerability scanners built on large language models propose fixes, the fixes are applied, the same class of tool confirms that the vulnerability is gone, and the change is deployed without independent testing. That observation is a vignette, not evidence of prevalence, and Chapter 6 treats it accordingly. But it is the origin of the dissertation's most concrete scenario, and it is disclosed for that reason.

### 1.8 Contributions

The dissertation claims five contributions. Its first is a theory, Synthetic Exchange Theory, which holds that the terms of exchange, and not the character of synthetic systems alone, determine the quality of a future shared with them, together with a statement of the regime in which that claim can be expected to hold. Its second is a structure for the conditions of fair exchange: consent, reciprocal value and real exit are partially ordered rather than independent, exit is prior to the other two, and the ordering yields a priority for institutional design, which is to secure a floor beneath every person before pricing what they supply. Its third is a bargaining-theoretic account of the economic design, which shows how a non-tradable survival floor and its inalienability act on the disagreement payoffs that govern how the gains from exchange are divided, and infers rather than derives the same for demurrage on idle balances, and which identifies where the floor's bargaining effect comes from and where it does not. Its fourth is a design for a founding convention: a public-choice veil of uncertainty, thickened by engineered anonymity between human and synthetic participants, which relocates the apparent incoherence of asking participants to be ignorant of their own kind by placing the *classification* of parties behind the veil, for the borderline cases that convergence will multiply and not for present principals, and which confronts, without fully solving, the identity problem that any anonymous procedure faces. Its fifth is a concrete transition scenario, the correlated failure of security remediation in organizations relying on the same few models, specified in three empirical premises and one condition on the workflow and placed within the historical literature on crisis and institutional change, together with an argument that portability and distributed control bear on both the trigger and the authoritarian response it could provoke. It claims no empirical result. The test the theory needs is described in Chapters 7 and 9 and left, for the reason given in Section 1.7, to future work.

### 1.9 Structure of the dissertation

The dissertation is organized in four parts.

**Part I** states the problem and the theory. Chapter 2 reviews the scholarly lineage of each condition and the empirical evidence on the grounding assumption. Chapter 3 states the theory formally.

**Part II** develops the design. Chapter 4 proposes compute as a medium of exchange and a basic income paid in it, split into a protected survival tier and a tradable discretionary tier subject to demurrage. Chapter 5 proposes the blind convention as the founding procedure and grounds it in the empirical record of commons governance.

**Part III** asks how the design could be reached. Chapter 6 examines the conditions under which fundamental institutional change has historically occurred and identifies a candidate trigger.

**Part IV** examines limits and horizons. Chapter 7 sets out the framework's limitations, the strongest objections to it, the harms the design itself could cause, and what it would take to test a theory that this dissertation does not test. Chapter 8 considers the convergence of human and machine substrates and what it does to the three conditions. Chapter 9 concludes.

Three appendices complete the work: the formal model (Appendix A), a glossary (Appendix B), and a ledger recording how each cited source was verified (Appendix C).

What the dissertation does not claim should also be stated. It does not predict that the arrangements it describes will be adopted, and Chapter 6 argues that adoption under present conditions is unlikely. It does not claim to solve the problem of aligning a superintelligence, or any synthetic intelligence whose objectives are emergent, unstable or concealed. And it does not claim that any institution can be made permanently incorruptible. Its working hypothesis is narrower: that an order founded on fair terms will prove easier to repair than one founded on an existing imbalance of power, because those who live under it have both the standing and the means to repair it. The dissertation offers that hypothesis for testing rather than as a finding, and it argues that the time to design such an order is before the moment when it might be needed, not during it.

## Chapter 2 — Literature and Grounding

The three conditions of Synthetic Exchange Theory are not new ideas. Each has a substantial scholarly lineage, and each lineage has discovered, in its own vocabulary, a characteristic way in which the condition fails. This chapter reviews that work. It begins with the sociological theory of exchange from which the dissertation takes its name and its central analytic tool. It then examines, in turn, the literature on consent and its limits, the objection from relational data, the proposal to treat data as labor and its critics, the philosophy of exploitation, the economics and sociology of exit, the emerging institutions of collective data governance, and the current state of the law. The final sections turn to the empirical premise on which the framework rests, the continuing dependence of synthetic systems on human input, and set out what the evidence does and does not support.

### 2.1 Exchange as the unit of analysis

Social exchange theory began with the proposal that social behavior can be understood as an exchange of goods, material and non-material alike, in which approval, prestige and help are traded as surely as money and labor (Homans, 1958). Its early statements drew the lesson that people in exchange calculate something like profit, reward minus cost, and that their judgments of fairness track whether rewards are proportional to investments (Homans, 1958). Blau (1964) distinguished social exchange, which carries unspecified obligations and depends on trust built up incrementally, from economic exchange, whose terms are specified in advance and enforced by law, and showed how imbalances in exchange produce power. A person who needs a service that another controls, and who has nothing to offer in return, can coerce the other, obtain the service elsewhere, do without it or subordinate themselves; when the first three are unavailable, subordination is what remains.

The decisive contribution for this dissertation is Emerson's (1962) theory of power-dependence, which locates power in the relation between actors rather than in either of them. In his formulation, the dependence of one actor on another is directly proportional to the first actor's motivational investment in goals the second mediates and inversely proportional to the availability of those goals outside the relation; the power of the second actor over the first is equal to that dependence. An unbalanced relation, Emerson argued, can be rebalanced in four ways. The weaker party can withdraw its motivational investment in what the stronger supplies; it can extend its network to cultivate alternatives; a status order can emerge in which the weaker party confers on the stronger a standing the stronger comes to value; or the weaker parties can form a coalition that the stronger must deal with collectively. These operations recur throughout this dissertation, which refers to them consistently as withdrawal, network extension, status emergence and coalition formation. Laboratory studies of exchange networks later showed that power is a function of position in the network, so that less dependent actors with better alternative sources obtain better terms (K. S. Cook et al., 1983). The tradition of interdependence theory begun by Thibaut and Kelley adds a distinction between satisfaction with a relationship and dependence on it: dependence is governed by the comparison level for alternatives, the standard a member uses in deciding whether to remain in a relationship or leave it (Thibaut & Kelley, 1959, p. 21), so that a person can be dissatisfied with a relationship and still be unable to leave it.

These ideas supply the dissertation's analytic frame. If power is relational in this sense, then the danger of a world with powerful synthetic systems lies not only in the systems but in the structure of the relations in which people are placed with respect to them. Whether a synthetic intelligence is good or bad is a question about an attribute of an actor. Whether a person can leave, refuse or audit their exchange with it is a question about a relation, and it is the second question that institutions of exchange can act on directly, and the one this dissertation addresses.

Blau's (1964) distinction between social exchange, whose obligations are unspecified and rest on trust, and economic exchange, whose terms are specified in advance, is a caution against assuming that every exchange between people and synthetic systems should be made as explicit and contractual as possible. This dissertation treats the licensing of data for training as an exchange of the second kind and leaves open whether continuing services are better governed as the first.

### 2.2 Consent and its limits

The first condition, consent, has a long lineage and well-documented failures.

An influential philosophical account of informed consent treats it, in its primary sense, as an autonomous authorization: an authorization given intentionally, with substantial understanding, and substantially free of controlling influences (Faden & Beauchamp, 1986, pp. 241–273, 277–280). The latter two elements have been shown to fail in the context of data. On understanding, a review of behavioral research on privacy organizes the evidence around three themes: people are uncertain about the consequences of their privacy choices and about their own preferences, their concern is highly dependent on context, and it is malleable by the commercial and governmental actors who benefit from shaping it (Acquisti et al., 2015). On the practical machinery of consent, Solove (2013) argued that the dominant model of "privacy self-management," in which individuals are given notice and choice and left to manage their own privacy, fails for both cognitive and structural reasons. People cannot make informed tradeoffs, there are too many entities to manage, harms emerge from the aggregation of data over time, and downstream uses are unknowable at the moment consent is given. He identified a dilemma: consent is often not meaningful, but the obvious alternative, paternalistic restriction, denies people the freedom to choose. More recently, Solove (2024) has gone further, arguing that most consent in privacy law is a fiction and that it should be treated as "murky consent," authorizing only a weak and restricted license, paired with substantive duties on organizations, including a duty of loyalty and a duty to avoid unreasonable risk.

On controlling influences, the literature on manipulation defines it as intentionally and covertly influencing a person's decisions by exploiting their vulnerabilities, distinguished from persuasion, which offers reasons, and from coercion, which openly constrains options (Susser et al., 2019). Manipulation damages autonomy through the process itself, even when the outcome is good for the person. Its practice has been documented at scale: a crawl of some 11,000 shopping websites found 1,818 instances of interface designs that steer users toward choices they would not otherwise make, and identified 22 third-party vendors offering such patterns as turnkey products (Mathur et al., 2019). A consent obtained through an interface engineered to obtain it is not the autonomous authorization the theory requires.

Two alternatives to the informational model of consent are important for this dissertation. Nissenbaum's (2009) theory of contextual integrity holds that a flow of information is appropriate when it conforms to the norms of the context in which the information was shared, norms specified by the actors involved, the type of information and the principles governing its transmission. Contextual integrity supplies a standard for appropriate flows that does not depend on individual agreement. And Manson and O'Neill (2007) argue that informed consent should be understood as a communicative transaction by which agents waive specific obligations; it can never be fully specific or fully explicit, and its ethical quality lies in the success of the communication rather than in the quantity of information disclosed.

The lesson for Synthetic Exchange Theory is that the consent condition cannot rest on information alone. The formal statement of Chapter 3 accordingly treats consent as having three clauses: an informational clause, which the literature shows is routinely unmet but can be improved by better standards of communication and contextual norms; a voluntariness clause, which depends on the person's ability to refuse without losing what they need to live; and a revocability clause. The second clause is where consent depends on exit, and Chapter 3 argues that it, rather than the informational clause, is often the binding constraint.

### 2.3 The relational objection

The objection this dissertation regards as most serious for any framework built on individual exchange is that data about one person is also data about others.

Barocas and Nissenbaum (2014) argued that the two standard instruments of data protection, anonymization and informed consent, both fail under the conditions of large-scale data analysis. Anonymity fails because individuals can be re-identified and, beyond that, because they need not be identified at all to be affected by inferences drawn from data about people like them. Consent fails partly because of a transparency paradox, in which notices simple enough to understand cannot describe complex flows with fidelity, and partly because of what they call the tyranny of the minority: the information volunteered by a few can reveal the same facts about the many who share their observable characteristics. This problem holds, they stress, even when every individual chooses well for themselves.

Viljoen (2021) generalized the point into a relational theory of data governance. The primary purpose of data production in the digital economy, she argues, is to relate people to one another on the basis of shared features, which creates interests at the level of populations. Existing law governs *vertical* relations between a data subject and a data collector; it has no way to represent the *horizontal* relations among people who share features and are therefore affected by one another's data. Viljoen classifies the main reform proposals into two families, both of which she finds inadequate because both are individualist: *propertarian* proposals, which treat data as an alienable asset, including proposals to treat data as labor, and *dignitarian* proposals, which treat data as an extension of the self. Her alternative is to govern data as a democratic medium, through institutions that can represent population-level interests.

This objection must be answered, and Chapter 3 attempts an answer. It introduces the concept of the *informational shadow* of a flow, the set of people about whom the flow licenses inferences, and argues that the three conditions must hold at the level of the population the shadow covers; Proposition 5 states the rule. The answer concedes the core of Viljoen's argument: where data is relational, the remedy must be collective and governed democratically, not merely priced. What the dissertation retains from the exchange framework is the insistence that the collective, too, must be able to consent, to receive value, and to leave.

### 2.4 Data as labor and its critics

The idea that data is a form of labor and that the services people receive are a kind of wage has a lineage. Arrieta-Ibarra et al. (2018) argued that the digital economy treats user data as capital, ignoring users' role in producing it, and that this framing weakens incentives to produce valuable data, concentrates gains and feeds fears about automation. Treating data at least partly as labor could restore a functioning market for user contributions, they argued, but the dominant buyers of data would resist, so that the shift would require countervailing power combining competition, a "data labor movement," and regulation. Posner and Weyl (2018) developed the idea as part of a broader program of market reforms. Lanier (2013) proposed an information economy in which people would receive small payments for the data they contribute to network services, and a later essay with Weyl adopted the term "data dignity" and proposed "mediators of individual data," volunteer organizations representing their members that would negotiate data royalties or wages and so bring collective bargaining to the people whose data is valuable (Lanier & Weyl, 2018).

The critics of this program press two objections. The first is the relational objection already discussed: Viljoen (2021) explicitly includes data-as-labor among the individualist reforms she rejects, on the ground that granting a person a right to sell their data does not secure fair conditions of sale and cannot represent the interests of the many others whom the data concerns. The second objection is that pricing data would commodify something that should not be commodified. Its general form is Radin's (1987) argument that some goods are market-inalienable because their sale harms the personhood of the seller; its application to data is the position Viljoen (2021) labels dignitarian, and the dissertation's design must answer it as well.

This dissertation takes from the data-as-labor tradition its central insight, which is that the flow of data from people to synthetic systems is an exchange and should be governed as one, with value flowing back. It rejects a pure per-person market in data for the reasons the critics give, and its economic design reflects that rejection in three ways. The basic income of Chapter 4 is paid to everyone regardless of the market value of their data, so that the floor beneath each person does not depend on what their life is worth to a model. The pricing of data is by informational novelty rather than by social status, so that the undersampled are not undervalued. And the governance of data with large informational shadows is collective. Arrieta-Ibarra et al. themselves argue that the shift they propose depends on countervailing power, which in the vocabulary of exchange theory means coalition formation. The data-as-labor tradition and its relational critics converge, on this reading, on the need for collective institutions.

### 2.5 Exploitation

A further philosophical complication bears directly on the reciprocity condition. An exchange can be consensual and beneficial to both parties and still be exploitative. The philosophical literature calls this mutually advantageous exploitation, in which one party takes unfair advantage of another even though the other gains and agrees, and one prominent account, reported in the survey by Zwolinski et al. (2022), judges the unfairness against a benchmark such as the price that would obtain in a competitive market free of informational asymmetry and undue pressure. Contemporary philosophical treatments distinguish the moral weight of exploitation, how wrong it is, from its moral force, whether others may intervene, and observe that prohibiting mutually beneficial but unfair transactions can leave the vulnerable worse off; redistribution aimed at the underlying vulnerability is offered as an alternative to banning the transactions (Zwolinski et al., 2022). Accounts of exploitation also divide over whether a prior or background injustice is necessary for a transaction to be exploitative, and some, Zwolinski's among them, hold that it is not (Zwolinski et al., 2022).

Two consequences follow for Synthetic Exchange Theory. First, "value flows both ways" is not enough. The reciprocity condition must include a fairness clause, and Chapter 3 states one: the division of the gains must not be determined solely by an asymmetry in bargaining position, whether in disagreement payoffs or in the capacity to wait, that the weaker party did not choose. Second, the exploitation literature identifies, as an alternative to prohibiting mutually beneficial but unfair transactions, redistribution aimed at the vulnerability that produces them (Zwolinski et al., 2022). That is the strategy this dissertation adopts. The guaranteed floor of Chapter 4 is an intervention on the underlying vulnerability. It leaves people free to make exchanges with synthetic systems and ensures that they are not forced to make them on any terms offered.

### 2.6 Exit, voice and lock-in

Hirschman's (1970) analysis of exit and voice is the classic account of how members of an organization respond to its decline. They can leave, which signals decline indirectly through lost revenue or membership, or they can attempt to change the organization from within, which Hirschman called voice and identified with political action. Voice is the only recourse where exit is unavailable, as in families, states and pure monopolies. Two of Hirschman's further observations bear on this dissertation. The ready availability of exit can cause the art of voice to atrophy. And when quality declines across an entire industry at once, exit becomes ineffective movement between equally deteriorating firms. A market in which a few providers of synthetic-intelligence services shared similar data practices would be a case of the second kind: switching among them would not be real exit.

The economics of switching costs explains why exit is so often difficult in technology markets. Switching costs and network effects lock customers into early choices and give sellers market power after the fact; firms therefore compete fiercely to acquire customers with low introductory prices and then exploit them, a pattern the authors describe as bargain-then-ripoff (Farrell & Klemperer, 2007). Firms also choose incompatibility more often than efficiency warrants, which the same authors take to justify a policy favoring compatibility. This dissertation reads the provision of free or subsidized services by providers of synthetic intelligence as the first phase of this pattern; whether the second phase follows is an empirical question the dissertation does not test.

The real-exit condition of Synthetic Exchange Theory is accordingly stated in terms that address both literatures: it requires that a person's disagreement payoff be at or above the floor, that a credible alternative exist whose value net of switching costs is not drastically lower, and that leaving not destroy assets accumulated within the exchange. From Hirschman's analysis this dissertation draws a further requirement, which the formal statement takes up explicitly: where exit is costly, as it is at the level of populations whose data has already been absorbed into trained models, and where every alternative shares the same defect, the conditions must be secured through voice, which is the function of the governance layer.

### 2.7 Collective institutions and data leverage

If individual exchange cannot by itself satisfy the three conditions for relational data, then something other than individual exchange must, and the candidates the literature offers are collective institutions. A growing literature describes what they might look like.

Data trusts would allow individuals to pool their data rights in legal trusts whose trustees owe them a duty of undivided loyalty, stricter than a duty of care; people could choose trusts that reflect their values and move between them, and the pooling would increase their negotiating power and allow consent to be negotiated collectively (Delacroix & Lawrence, 2019). Data cooperatives, modeled on credit unions, would hold and protect members' data, run analyses for members' benefit, and negotiate with service providers on their behalf (Hardjono & Pentland, 2019). A comparative review of legal mechanisms for data stewardship concludes that no single mechanism suits every case and that a shared purpose must come first (Ada Lovelace Institute, 2021).

The literature on data leverage can be read as turning the logic of power-dependence into concrete actions. Because data-dependent technologies depend on the public's contributions, the public holds potential leverage: people can withhold or delete their data in a *data strike*, contribute deliberately harmful data, or redirect their contributions to a competitor they prefer, a strategy called *conscious data contribution* (Vincent et al., 2021). On this dissertation's reading of Emerson, a data strike is not itself a rebalancing operation, because Emerson's withdrawal is a reduction of the weaker party's own motivational investment in what the stronger supplies, whereas a strike withholds what the stronger party needs. A strike is therefore an exercise of the power that the system's dependence on data already confers on its suppliers, and the collective organization needed to make it effective is coalition formation; conscious data contribution, by strengthening an alternative provider, is a form of network extension. A simulation study suggests that data strikes can degrade the performance of recommender systems (Vincent et al., 2019), though their effectiveness is limited by diminishing returns once a firm already has enough data, a limit that corresponds to the qualifications on the grounding assumption discussed below (Vincent et al., 2021).

The consent signals that govern automated collection show withdrawal already under way. A longitudinal audit of the consent signals attached to some 14,000 web domains underlying widely used training corpora found that within a single year, more than a quarter of the most actively maintained critical sources in one corpus had moved to restrict automated collection fully, and that terms of service restricted crawling for close to half of it (Longpre et al., 2024). Whether these signals, which the authors describe as inconsistent and as symptoms of web protocols built for other purposes, reduce the performance of the systems trained on the corpora is not shown. What they show is that the sources are withholding, and that the instruments available to them were not designed for the purpose.

### 2.8 The state of the law

Existing law contains partial analogues of each condition, and their limits are instructive.

The European Union's General Data Protection Regulation requires that withdrawing consent be as easy as giving it, and provides that whether a contract is made conditional on consent to unnecessary processing bears on whether consent was freely given (Regulation [EU] 2016/679, art. 7). These provisions are the closest legal analogue to the voluntariness clause of the consent condition. The regulation's right to erasure gives individuals grounds to require deletion, subject to exceptions including scientific research (art. 17). Its right to data portability is the closest legal analogue to real exit, but it is narrow: it covers only data the individual has provided, processed on the basis of consent or contract by automated means, with direct transfer between controllers required only where technically feasible, and it must not adversely affect the rights of others (art. 20). Because it covers only data the individual has provided, it does not on its terms reach inferred data or the parameters of a model trained on it. The more recent Data Act extends access and portability to data generated by connected devices and requires that customers be able to switch between cloud providers, with charges for switching to be eliminated from January 2027 (Regulation [EU] 2023/2854). It is an example of bounded switching costs written into law, and, on this dissertation's reading, evidence that real exit is a legislatively achievable condition.

The European Union's Artificial Intelligence Act entered into force in August 2024, with its prohibitions applying from February 2025 and its obligations on providers of general-purpose models from August 2025 (Regulation [EU] 2024/1689). Those obligations include a policy for complying with copyright law, including honoring opt-outs from text and data mining, and the publication of a sufficiently detailed summary of the content used for training (art. 53). This is the closest existing law to a consent signal for training data, though it is an opt-out regime, not opt-in consent. A 2026 amending regulation, Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, the "Digital Omnibus on AI," published in the Official Journal on 24 July 2026 and in force from 27 July, has deferred the obligations for high-risk systems to 2 December 2027 for stand-alone systems and to 2 August 2028 for systems embedded in regulated products, so that they do not yet apply (Agustinoy, 2026; ERTICO, 2026; Regulation [EU] 2026/1744).

Neural data, which Chapter 8 discusses, has begun to receive specific protection. Chile amended its constitution in 2021 to require that scientific and technological development serve people and to direct the law to protect brain activity and the information derived from it (Ley N.º 21.383, 2021). Colorado in 2024 added neural data to the categories of sensitive data requiring consent under its consumer privacy law, where the data is used or intended for identification (H.B. 24-1058, 2024), and California added neural data to the "sensitive personal information" governed by its consumer privacy act (S.B. 1223, 2024). UNESCO adopted a global recommendation on the ethics of neurotechnology in November 2025 (UNESCO, 2025). All of these instruments treat neural data as information about a person that the law should protect. None yet addresses the case, considered in Chapter 8, in which living tissue is itself part of the computing substrate.

### 2.9 The empirical anchor: what synthetic systems need from people

The framework's grounding assumption is that synthetic systems need a continuing supply of input from outside their own generative loop, and that in an important class of domains only people can supply it. This section reviews the evidence for and against that assumption, because the dissertation's claim that fair terms are partly self-enforcing depends on it.

**The case for dependence.** A consistent body of work shows that generative models trained repeatedly on their own unfiltered output, with nothing from outside the loop, degrade. In the study that gave the phenomenon its name, models of several kinds, including a small language model fine-tuned over successive generations, lost the tails of the original distribution first and then converged toward distributions with greatly reduced variance; the authors called the process model collapse and concluded that sustained learning requires preserving access to data not generated by models (Shumailov et al., 2024). The same pattern appears in image models, where fully self-consuming training loops lose quality or diversity and only loops that receive enough fresh real data in each generation remain stable (Alemohammad et al., 2024); in statistical models of the simplest kind, where purely self-referential maximum-likelihood retraining converges on degenerate states that lock in whatever biases the initial sample contained (Jangjoo et al., 2026); and in language, where the lexical and especially the syntactic diversity of model outputs declines across generations of recursive training, while semantic diversity remains comparatively stable, with the steepest decline in the tasks that demand the most creativity (Y. Guo et al., 2024). A theoretical analysis of mixed training in regression and random-feature models shows that if synthetic data makes up any non-vanishing fraction of a growing training set, additional data eventually stops improving the model, and that mixing strategies mitigate this only if real data grows at least as fast as synthetic data (Dohmatob et al., 2025).

These results acquire economic significance alongside evidence on the supply of human data. Compute-optimal training requires training data in proportion to model size: a study of more than 400 trained models found that model size and training data should be scaled in equal proportion (Hoffmann et al., 2022). For a fixed compute budget, training on up to about four epochs of repeated data changes loss negligibly compared with unique data, while further repetition adds progressively less until additional compute is worth almost nothing (Muennighoff et al., 2023). The effective stock of high-quality public human text is finite, and one projection puts the point at which the largest training runs would use all of it between 2026 and 2032 if current trends continue (Villalobos et al., 2024). Meanwhile, the web crawls from which that stock is drawn carry a rising share of machine-authored text: a 2026 analysis using an automated detector estimated that about a tenth of a July 2026 sample of 10,000 English web pages, and more than a third of the pages in that sample published since the release of widely used chatbots, show significant signs of machine authorship, while cautioning that such detectors misclassify individual documents (Bestvater et al., 2026). And, as Section 2.7 showed, a growing share of the web domains that supply it have restricted automated collection (Longpre et al., 2024).

The value of human input is also visible in the methods by which current systems are made useful. Training with human preference judgments allowed a model with roughly one-hundredth as many parameters as its predecessor to produce outputs that human evaluators preferred (Ouyang et al., 2022), building on work showing that a relatively small quantity of human comparisons can substitute for a hand-specified objective (Christiano et al., 2017).

**The case against strong dependence.** Three bodies of evidence limit the assumption.

First, collapse depends on replacement. When synthetic data is added to an accumulating pool that retains the original human data, rather than replacing it, a linear-model analysis shows error remaining bounded however many generations pass, with consistent results in experiments across several kinds of model (Gerstgrasser et al., 2024), and under realistic fixed training budgets the degradation is gradual rather than catastrophic (Kazdan et al., 2024). A critical review, so far circulated as a preprint, identifies eight distinct and partly conflicting definitions of model collapse in the literature and argues that several prominent scenarios are readily avoidable (Schaeffer et al., 2025). Even Shumailov et al. (2024) found that retaining a tenth of the original data in each generation limited the damage to minor degradation.

Second, where an external check on correctness exists, self-generated data can substitute for human data. A system given no domain knowledge beyond the rules of chess, shogi and Go learned through self-play to defeat a world-champion program in each game (Silver et al., 2018). Reinforcement learning with automatically verifiable rewards produced substantial reasoning ability in a language model without human-written reasoning examples, though the final system still relied on curated human data and its authors identified domains without automatic verification as an unsolved problem (D. Guo et al., 2025). Synthetic data filtered by a sufficiently accurate verifier, even an imperfect one, avoided the collapse that unfiltered data produced in the tasks studied (Feng et al., 2024), and preference labels generated by a model can substitute for human labels in some settings, though the labels came from an off-the-shelf pretrained language model (Lee et al., 2024), which was itself trained on human-written text.

Third, systems trained by reinforcement learning can learn from interaction with an environment rather than from people. A system that learns a model of its environment and plans within it learned to perform well across more than 150 tasks in simulated environments, including collecting diamonds in a complex game world from scratch, without human data (Hafner et al., 2025), building on earlier work showing that agents can be trained inside learned models of their environment (Ha & Schmidhuber, 2018). Robotics remains limited by the scarcity and fragmentation of real-world training data (Firoozi et al., 2025; Open X-Embodiment Collaboration, 2024), and assembling large real-world robot datasets still requires substantial human labor, with demonstrations gathered by human data collectors (Khazatsky et al., 2024), so that even embodied data, on present methods, depends heavily on human work. Policies trained in simulation can be transferred to physical robots, though with a persistent gap between the two (Zhao et al., 2020). The inference that the physical world could one day supply an external signal that neither runs out nor comes from people is this dissertation's, and it concerns the future rather than the present.

**What the evidence supports.** Two cautions apply to all of this work. Almost all of the evidence for collapse comes from small models or stylized theory, and, to the author's knowledge at the time of writing, no published study demonstrates the collapse of a frontier model under realistic data practices. And the evidence for substitution is concentrated in domains where correctness can be checked mechanically. The literature does not settle the question in one formulation, because its results depend on the training regime assumed. This dissertation adopts the following formulation, which holds under the regimes closest to present practice, in which synthetic data enters a growing or budget-constrained training set (Alemohammad et al., 2024; Dohmatob et al., 2025; Kazdan et al., 2024), and which Section 7.1 shows would weaken if developers retained and accumulated the human corpus already collected (Gerstgrasser et al., 2024):

> Learning systems need a continuing supply of information from outside their own generative loop. Where correctness can be verified mechanically, or where a faithful simulator exists, that information can come from verifiers, rules, simulators or the physical world, and dependence on people is weak. Where the target is human meaning, preference, value or new cultural production, no source other than people has been shown adequate: automatic verifiers do not exist for these targets (D. Guo et al., 2025), and the model-based judges that have substituted for human labels in some settings were themselves trained on human-written text (Lee et al., 2024). Under the regimes named above, people's contribution must keep pace with, rather than merely seed, the volume of synthetic data in training.
>
The second half of this formulation is adopted as a premise with supporting but not decisive evidence; Section 7.1 states the conditions under which it would fail. The formulation is narrower than the intuitive version of the assumption. It concedes the verifiable and simulable domains to the counterevidence and confines the claim of human necessity to the domains in which no substitute has been demonstrated. Chapter 3 formalizes the consequence: the human party's countervailing power within the exchange rises with each synthetic party's dependence on this input, and the framework must be designed to hold even if that dependence turns out to be small.

### 2.10 Where the literature leaves the thesis

The literature reviewed here leaves Synthetic Exchange Theory with four obligations.

It must treat consent as resting on more than information, because the behavioral, legal and empirical literature reviewed in Section 2.2 converges on the finding that informational consent routinely fails, and it must locate the binding constraint in the voluntariness of refusal, which depends on exit. It must answer the relational objection with collective institutions rather than individual prices, while insisting that those institutions also satisfy the conditions of consent, reciprocity and exit at their own level. It must give the reciprocity condition a fairness standard that goes beyond mutual benefit, and it must address unfair exchanges by improving the weaker party's position rather than by prohibition. And it must state its empirical premise in the form the evidence supports, which is narrower and more defensible than the intuitive form, and design its institutions to survive the premise's weakening.

The next chapter takes up these obligations formally.

## Chapter 3 — A Formal Statement of Synthetic Exchange Theory

The two preceding chapters argued in prose that the line between a good and a bad future with synthetic intelligence is drawn by the terms of exchange and cannot be drawn by the character of the systems alone. Prose permits a claim to be read more broadly when it is being defended than when it is being tested. This chapter therefore states the theory in a form whose content is fixed: a small vocabulary of defined terms, a set of propositions that follow from them, and a typology of exchange regimes that later chapters use as a diagnostic instrument. The formalism is deliberately modest. It is not a general equilibrium model of a post-labor economy, and it does not claim a precision the subject cannot bear. Its purpose is to make the dissertation's commitments explicit enough that a reader can see exactly where they would fail.

### 3.1 Why a formal statement is needed

Three ambiguities in the informal statement of the thesis justify the effort.

The first concerns the word *character*. The informal claim that outcomes depend on terms rather than character can be read as a strong claim, that a system's dispositions are irrelevant, or as a weak claim, that dispositions are insufficient. The strong claim is false: a system that actively seeks to deceive its counterparties will corrupt any institution it is placed inside. The weak claim is true but uninformative. The formal statement locates the thesis between them, which is why the thesis is stated as "cannot be drawn by the character of the systems alone." Character is treated as one input into the exchange, the agent's objective, and the terms are treated as the structure that determines how that objective translates into behavior. The thesis then becomes a claim about which of the two inputs institutional design can act on, and about the regime in which, once the terms bind, acting on them can be expected to suffice.

The second ambiguity concerns the relationship among the three conditions. In Chapter 1, consent, reciprocal value and real exit were introduced as tests that an exchange either passes or fails. They are not independent tests. Section 3.4 shows that consent leans on exit, that exit bounds reciprocity from below, and that the three are partially ordered, with exit prior to the other two.

The third ambiguity concerns power. The informal argument says that humans retain leverage because synthetic systems need their data. Leverage is a relational quantity, and it can be measured, at least in principle, by the dependence of each party on the other. Stating the argument in the vocabulary of power-dependence theory makes visible the dissertation's most important conditional: the framework is partly self-enforcing, to a degree that increases with the dependence of each synthetic party on human-generated input, and only while that dependence remains material.
