When it falls, or when it is spread so thinly across many synthetic parties that none of them bears the cost of degrading its human counterparties, the framework's institutions must carry weight that the underlying exchange no longer carries. Chapter 7 returns to this as the first of its limitations.

### 3.2 Lineage: exchange as the unit of social analysis

The theory's name places it in a tradition. Social exchange theory, as developed in sociology and social psychology in the middle of the twentieth century, treated social life as the exchange of rewarding and costly activity between actors, and asked what structures of exchange produce stable, cooperative or exploitative relations (Blau, 1964; Homans, 1958; Thibaut & Kelley, 1959). Its most consequential contribution for present purposes is Emerson's (1962) analysis of power as relational; in his words, power "is a property of the social relation; it is not an attribute of the actor" (p. 32). The power of one actor over another equals the dependence of the second on the first, and dependence rises with the value the dependent actor places on what the relation supplies and falls with the availability of that value from outside the relation. Power, on this account, is not a trait. It is a consequence of the structure of alternatives.

That is the move this dissertation makes about synthetic intelligence. Much of the public and technical conversation about advanced synthetic intelligence, on this dissertation's reading of it, treats the danger as residing in the system, in its goals, its dispositions, its capability to deceive or to seize resources. Those concerns are real, and the alignment literature that addresses them is engaged in Chapters 5 and 7. But the exchange-theoretic tradition suggests a complementary diagnosis: much of what would make a world with powerful synthetic systems dystopian is not a property of the systems at all but a property of the relations in which humans are placed with respect to them. A person with no alternative to a single provider of medical monitoring, whose refusal to share data would cost them their health, is in a relation of extreme dependence regardless of whether the provider is benevolent. A provider in that relation holds power whose use is restrained by nothing but its own disposition, and the relation is secure only while that disposition persists.

The constitutional tradition reaches the same conclusion from another direction. Hume (1741/1987, pp. 42–43) defended the maxim that in designing institutions every person should be supposed a knave, not because he believed people were knaves but because institutions that work only when their occupants are virtuous fail at the moment virtue fails. Madison (1788) made the same argument in *Federalist* No. 51: if people were angels no government would be needed, and since they are not, the design task is to arrange the structure so that ambition counteracts ambition. Synthetic Exchange Theory extends this constitutional insight across a new boundary. Its governing maxim is that in designing the terms of exchange between people and synthetic systems, neither party should be supposed benevolent. The terms must be such that a self-interested system, dealing with self-interested people, produces outcomes that both could endorse.

### 3.3 Primitive terms

The theory uses six primitive terms. Each is defined to be substrate-neutral, in keeping with the choice of the word *synthetic* over alternatives such as *symbiotic* or *human–AI*: the framework must apply without modification to biological humans, artificial systems and the hybrids discussed in Chapter 8.

**Party.** A party is any entity that can be a source or recipient of value in an exchange and whose welfare, or whose principal's welfare, the framework counts. A human being is a party. A synthetic system is treated as a party in the analytic sense that it has an objective that the exchange advances or frustrates; whether it is also a party in the moral sense, an entity whose welfare counts for its own sake, is left open in this chapter and taken up in Chapter 8. The propositions below hold for parties in the analytic sense regardless of how the moral question is resolved.

The phrase "or whose principal's welfare" carries more weight than its brevity suggests, and it requires an account of who the synthetic party is. In present law, a deployed model is not a legal person and does not own anything, and the question whether one could be has been examined as a hypothetical rather than as doctrine (Solum, 1992). As Section 1.2 stated, it is not an independent living entity but an artifact that runs when its operator runs it. The entity with interests that can be advanced or frustrated is therefore the operator or developer that trains, deploys and profits from it. The relation between that operator and its system is a principal–agent relation of an unusual kind. The principal sets the system's objective through training and deployment, the agent pursues that objective with considerable autonomy in each exchange, and the agent's conduct may diverge from what the principal intended (Hubinger et al., 2019). This dissertation therefore treats the synthetic party as a composite: its *objective* is the model's, as trained and deployed, but its *stake* in the future of the exchange, its revenue, its access to data, its liability, belongs to the principal. Three consequences follow. Proposition 4 below concerns the principal's dependence on human input, transmitted to the system only to the extent that the principal writes it into the system's objective. The redemption obligation of Chapter 4 binds the owners of compute capacity, which, where a principal owns the capacity that runs its system, is the principal; a trained artifact cannot be made to honor it. And the question of how to count synthetic parties, which Chapter 5 cannot avoid, is a question about principals and about systems at once. It is made harder by the possibility that advanced synthetic intelligence is better modeled as a collection of bounded services than as a unitary agent (Drexler, 2019), in which case there may be no single system to which the label "party" naturally attaches. If the question of a system's own moral status, which Chapter 8 argues may eventually become live, were answered in the system's favor, the composite would come apart and the system would become a party in its own right.

**Flow.** A flow is a transfer of something of value from one party to another: data, labor, attention, compute, physical goods, services or tissue. Each flow has a source, a recipient, content and an informational shadow, meaning the set of parties, the source among them, about whom the recipient can draw inferences from the flow. The informational shadow is what makes data relational (Viljoen, 2021) and is the reason individual consent cannot by itself legitimate a flow.

**Exchange.** An exchange is a pair of opposing flows between two parties, or between a party and a collective, that each party enters because of the other flow. The defining feature of an exchange, as distinct from a gift or a taking, is conditionality: each flow is given because the other is received.

**Floor.** A party's floor is the bundle of goods and services without which the party cannot continue to function or to participate in any other exchange. For a human being, the floor is survival and basic participation, and this dissertation fixes its contents once, here, as power, water, food, shelter, basic communication and emergency care. For a synthetic party, the analogue is the minimum of energy, substrate upkeep and data required to keep operating; for a present artificial system, that is compute, energy and data. The floor is defined physically rather than in currency. Its value at current prices, which Appendix A writes $\bar f$, is what a person's disagreement payoff must reach for the conditions below to hold. Chapter 4 therefore delivers the floor through a survival tier that is a percentage of each allocation, so that it grows with the economy, subject to a binding physical minimum, so that it does not fall below the bundle it exists to secure. That guarantee holds while the agreed share of capacity can buy the bundle; Chapter 4 and Appendix A state the case in which it cannot.

**Disagreement payoff.** A party's disagreement payoff is what that party receives while an exchange is not taking place, that is, during refusal, negotiation or impasse. Bargaining theory shows that the division of the gains from an exchange depends on the parties' disagreement payoffs (Nash, 1950, pp. 158–159). A party whose disagreement payoff is below its floor cannot afford to disagree for long and therefore cannot bargain at all.

**Exit option.** A party's exit option is the best alternative exchange available to it if the current one ends: another provider, another employer, another data purchaser or self-provision. The distinction between a disagreement payoff and an exit option carries design consequences. Strategic bargaining models and laboratory evidence show that an outside option affects the terms of a bargain only when its exercise is credible, that is, only when the party would actually take it, whereas the payoff received during impasse shapes the bargain continuously (Binmore et al., 1986; Binmore et al., 1989). When the option binds, the weaker party receives exactly the option's value, which is then more than it would otherwise have obtained; the option therefore protects only up to its own value, and that value can be lowered by the counterparty through switching costs until the option no longer binds and protects nothing. A disagreement payoff that cannot be taken away is a protection of a different kind, because it moves the bargained share itself and does not depend on anyone's willingness to leave. The non-tradable survival tier of Chapter 4 is designed to be such a payoff.

### 3.4 The three conditions, stated precisely

With the primitive terms in hand, the three conditions of Chapter 1 can be restated so that each has a determinate content and the relations among them become visible.

**Condition E (Real exit).** An exchange satisfies real exit for a party if and only if (i) the party's disagreement payoff is at or above its floor, (ii) the party has at least one exit option whose value net of switching costs is not drastically lower than the value of the current exchange, and (iii) exercising the exit option does not destroy assets the party has accumulated within the exchange, including data, reputation and records of entitlement. Clause (i) rules out lethal dependency. Clause (ii) rules out lock-in. Clause (iii) is the portability requirement, and it is what data-portability rights in existing law attempt to secure (Regulation [EU] 2016/679, art. 20; Regulation [EU] 2023/2854).

**Condition C (Consent).** An exchange satisfies consent for a party if and only if (i) the party has access to an accurate account of the flows the exchange involves, including their informational shadows, in a form the party can understand; (ii) the party can refuse the exchange, entering no other in its place, without falling below its floor, which is to say that its disagreement payoff is at or above its floor; and (iii) the party can revoke its participation at a cost bounded by the value of what it has already received. Clause (i) is the informational condition that privacy scholarship has shown to be routinely unmet (Acquisti et al., 2015; Solove, 2013). Clause (ii) is where consent depends on exit, and its wording matters: a party that could avoid falling below its floor only by switching to another provider has an exit option, not a disagreement payoff at the floor, and the clause requires the latter, for the reason Section 3.3 gave. Clause (iii) adds the temporal dimension: consent that cannot be withdrawn is a sale.

**Condition R (Reciprocal value).** An exchange satisfies reciprocal value if and only if (i) each party's net gain from the exchange, measured against its disagreement payoff, is positive; (ii) each party can observe what it receives and what it gives, so that the gains are legible; and (iii) the division of the joint surplus is not determined solely by an unchosen asymmetry in the parties' bargaining positions, whether in their disagreement payoffs or in their capacity to wait. Clause C(i) concerns the content of the flows and their shadows; clause R(ii) concerns their value, and the two can fail separately. Clause R(iii) is the fairness clause, and it is the clause that requires governance, because the market alone will divide surplus according to bargaining power. It is needed because, as the philosophy of exploitation has shown, an exchange can be consensual and beneficial to both sides and still take unfair advantage of one of them (Zwolinski et al., 2022). The standard adopted here is the one the exploitation literature reports: the division that would obtain in a competitive market free of informational asymmetry and undue pressure (Zwolinski et al., 2022). A division that departs from that benchmark because one party's disagreement payoff lies below its floor, because one party can outwait the other on an accumulated stock, or because of structural bargaining power the weaker party did not choose, such as concentration or lock-in, fails the clause; the floor corrects the first of these, and the residue is what governance must address. A division that reflects the parties' genuine alternatives above the floor does not fail it. Mutual benefit is the minimum reciprocity requires, not its standard.

With the conditions stated this way, their structure becomes clear.

> **Proposition 1 (Exit primacy).** Consent presupposes the first clause of exit. If a party's disagreement payoff is below its floor, then no agreement that party gives satisfies Condition C, however well informed it is.
>
The derivation is immediate because the definitions were drawn to make it so: clause C(ii) requires that refusal, with no other exchange entered in its place, leave the party at or above its floor, which is E(i). The proposition's real content is the claim that C(ii), so worded, belongs in any adequate definition of consent: that a party able to refuse one provider only by submitting to another has not been given the ability to refuse. The unconditional floor of Chapter 4 is designed to supply the disagreement payoff the clause requires. The proposition formalizes a point that the bioethics literature on informed consent has long made in its own terms (Faden & Beauchamp, 1986, pp. 241–273; Manson & O'Neill, 2007), that information is necessary but not sufficient for valid consent, and that agreement given under the threat of losing something one cannot do without is not voluntary in the relevant sense. Its practical implication is that no amount of improvement in notice, disclosure or interface design can repair a consent regime whose subjects cannot afford to refuse. The policy effort directed at better privacy notices is not wasted, but it is directed at clause C(i) while the binding constraint is often clause C(ii). The definition has a consequence that should be stated. Under it, consent to a service without which a person cannot live is valid only where the service's continuation is secured independently of the consent, which no existing medical institution guarantees; a patient who can choose among providers of such a service but cannot refuse it altogether has, on this definition, an exit option and not the ability to refuse. The framework accepts that verdict, and it is the reason the floor includes emergency care and the portability requirement of Chapter 8 must cover life-sustaining devices.

> **Proposition 2 (Reciprocity as the reason to stay).** If Condition E holds, switching costs are negligible, and the party can observe its gain (clause R(ii)), then a party that acts in its interest remains in an exchange only if its payoff in the exchange is at least the value of its best exit option; since that option is itself an exchange the party would enter only if it at least matched its disagreement payoff, each party's net gain is non-negative for every exchange that persists, which is clause R(i) with its strict inequality relaxed. Strict reciprocity requires that the best alternative itself better the disagreement payoff, which clause E(ii) does not guarantee. Reciprocity in its first clause is then not an independent ethical requirement imposed on top of exit; exit bounds it from below. Its fairness clause is not so bounded.
>
When a party can leave at negligible cost and can see what it gains, it will leave an exchange that leaves it worse off than its alternatives. An exchange that persists under such conditions therefore reveals that the party is receiving value. Where switching costs are material, a party may rationally remain in an exchange that gives it nothing beyond what it would get on impasse, because leaving would cost more than staying; this is the gap that clause E(ii) is meant to keep small. The converse is the diagnostic that matters: an exchange that persists while delivering negative value to one party is evidence that clause E(ii) is failing for that party, or that the party cannot see what it gains, which is a failure of clause R(ii). Persistence without legible reciprocity is a symptom either of lock-in or of opacity.

> **Proposition 3 (The triad is partially ordered, with exit prior).** Violations of the three conditions differ in how much damage they do and in whether they can be corrected from inside the exchange. A violation of the first clause of exit entails a violation of clause C(ii) and, where the sub-floor position is unchosen and determines the division by itself, of clause R(iii); clauses R(i) and R(ii) may still hold. A violation of the second or third clause of exit leaves the other conditions intact at the moment of exchange but removes the means of correcting their later failure; the one exception is that a violation of clause E(iii) carries a violation of clause C(iii) with it where the assets destroyed on leaving exceed the value received. A violation of the revocability clause of consent, with exit intact, is correctable by exit; a violation of the voluntariness clause is a violation of exit itself, by Proposition 1; a violation of the informational clause is correctable by exit only once the party knows enough to want to leave. A violation of reciprocity in its first two clauses, with consent and exit intact, is corrected by departure, unless the same violation is common to every available alternative. A violation of clause R(iii) is only partly corrected by exit: the floor removes the asymmetry that comes from a disagreement payoff below the floor, and an outside option bounds the division from below, but neither determines the division of the surplus that remains, which follows from bargaining power that the party did not choose and that governance alone can address.
>
The ordering the proposition establishes is partial. Exit is prior to the other two conditions because its failures cannot be corrected from inside the exchange; consent and reciprocity are not ordered with respect to each other, since at clause level the fairness clause is the least correctable of all and the first two clauses of reciprocity the most. The priority is one of correctability from inside the exchange, and the design conclusion drawn below needs only the priority of the floor clause. The qualifications matter. When every provider of a service shares the same defect, departure is what Hirschman (1970) showed exit to become under industry-wide decline: ineffective movement between alternatives that are equally bad. And the fairness clause of reciprocity is only partly within the reach of exit. Even so, the ordering survives as a statement of priority, and it is the dissertation's principal reason for designing the economic layer around a guaranteed floor rather than around better pricing of data. Pricing addresses reciprocity. The floor addresses exit. If the order is right, the floor comes first, and governance is needed for what neither can reach.

### 3.5 Power and the grounding assumption

The second analytic tool is Emerson's (1962) definition of power as dependence. For two parties *A* and *B*, let the dependence of *B* on *A* increase with *B*'s valuation of what the relation with *A* supplies and decrease with the availability of that value from parties other than *A*. The power of *A* over *B* equals the dependence of *B* on *A*. The relation is balanced when the two dependences are equal and unbalanced otherwise. Of the four ways in which Chapter 2 showed an unbalanced relation can be rebalanced, three have direct analogues in the design that follows. The survival tier and the portability requirements extend the human party's network, by making floor goods and records available independently of any single provider; the same tier makes possible a measure of withdrawal, since a person whose floor is secured can afford to care less about what any one provider offers; and data cooperatives and the collective-choice arrangements of Chapter 5 are coalition formation.

The grounding assumption plays a different role. It is not a rebalancing operation at all but a statement about dependence running the other way: it asserts that synthetic parties need something only people can supply, and so that the relation is less unbalanced than it appears. This reformulation makes the dissertation's central empirical premise precise, and it also exposes a difficulty the informal argument concealed.

> **Proposition 4 (Conditional and divided self-enforcement).** Let $\kappa_j$ denote the dependence of synthetic party $j$, through its principal, on the human-generated input of a given population. Party $j$ has a reason to preserve that input, to a degree that increases with $\kappa_j$, provided that the principal's dependence is written into the system's objective and that the principal weighs the future value of its inputs against present gains. That reason makes fair terms partly self-enforcing only insofar as the quality of the population's input depends on the terms the population receives: because a population that can refuse and leave can withhold what the party needs, and because, on this dissertation's inference, contribution under domination narrows toward what the dominant party extracts and loses the variety that gives it value. Where input can be extracted on unfair terms without loss, dependence protects the supply and not the terms. As $\kappa_j$ falls toward zero, the self-enforcing component vanishes. When many synthetic parties draw on the same population, the welfare and data quality of that population become a common-pool resource for them: each bears only its own share of the cost of degrading it, so that the incentive of any one party to preserve it can be small even when the dependence of all of them together is large.
>
The research on recursive training reviewed in Chapter 2 bears directly on the size of $\kappa_j$. If models trained on their own outputs lose the tails of the distribution and drift (Shumailov et al., 2024), human data has a value that synthetic data cannot replace. If, as other work suggests, accumulation of real data alongside synthetic data (Gerstgrasser et al., 2024; Kazdan et al., 2024), or a modest continuing supply of fresh real data (Alemohammad et al., 2024; Shumailov et al., 2024), is sufficient to prevent collapse, though at least one formal result requires that supply to grow in proportion to the synthetic data (Dohmatob et al., 2025), then dependence is positive but small, and the human party's leverage is correspondingly modest. Proposition 4 is written so that the framework does not stake its normative conclusions on the most favorable reading of that literature. Two of its premises deserve emphasis because they are easy to pass over. The first is that the withholding by which a population disciplines terms presupposes that the population can refuse and leave: self-enforcement is therefore a support for the institutions that secure exit, not a substitute for them, which is the sense in which Chapter 7 calls it a support and not a foundation. The second, that extracted contribution loses value, is the dissertation's inference from the grounding assumption as Chapter 2 narrowed it, since the domains in which people are the only adequate source are those of meaning, preference, value and culture, and it is argued, not shown.

The second half of the proposition matters as much as the first. It is Hardin's (1968) logic of the open-access commons applied to the synthetic side of the exchange: aggregate dependence on people does not translate into individual restraint by any synthetic party when each can free-ride on the restraint of the others. This does not weaken the case for the framework. It strengthens the case for its governance layer, because a commons is what the institutions of Chapter 5, grounded in the record of how commons are governed, are designed to protect. The result also exposes a tension within the design. Exit requires many synthetic parties; self-enforcement is strongest when there is one. The framework resolves the tension in favor of exit, and therefore cannot rely on self-enforcement in the competitive market it seeks to create; the governance layer must carry that weight, and Chapter 7 records this as a limitation. The institutional layers of Chapters 4 and 5 are therefore built to hold whether dependence is small, or large but divided. What changes as dependence falls or fragments is not the justification of the institutions but the degree to which they can rely on the self-interest of synthetic parties to sustain them.

### 3.6 Bargaining, the floor and the logic of the economic layer

The third analytic tool is bargaining theory, which supplies the formal reason for the most distinctive features of the economic design. Appendix A gives the derivations.

Consider an exchange between a human party *h* and a synthetic party *s* that creates a joint surplus. In the axiomatic solution to the two-party bargaining problem, the surplus is divided so as to maximize the product of each party's gain over its disagreement payoff (Nash, 1950, p. 159). Strategic bargaining models in which parties alternate offers converge to this solution as the frictions of delay vanish, provided the disagreement point is chosen to reflect what parties receive while negotiations are under way, and in those models each party's impatience is part of what determines the outcome (Binmore et al., 1986). When the outside option is instead an alternative a party may or may not take, it affects the outcome only when it binds (Binmore et al., 1989).

The most important consequence for policy concerns impatience. If, as the floor's definition implies, payoffs below it carry a cost of delay that rises sharply (a survival constraint the Nash model does not itself contain), a party whose disagreement payoff lies below its floor cannot sustain disagreement for longer than its reserves allow; in a model of alternating offers it behaves as a party with a very high cost of delay, and such a party concedes. Raising the disagreement payoff to the floor removes this source of weakness, and it does so in every bargain the party enters, not because the floor is added to each bargain separately but because it is the background against which each is conducted. This is the main bargaining-theoretic reason for the floor, and it does not depend on whether the tier substitutes for anything the counterparty supplies, though it does depend on the survival constraint just stated.

A second consequence concerns the division of surplus, and here the intuitive version is wrong. Holding the value of agreement fixed, raising one party's disagreement payoff raises that party's share of the agreement. But an unconditional floor, received whether or not agreement is reached, raises both the party's payoff on disagreement and its payoff on agreement by the same amount. It therefore leaves the surplus, and its division, unchanged: the party is better off by exactly the value of the floor, no more. The floor lowers the counterparty's payoff only where it substitutes for something the counterparty would otherwise have supplied within the exchange, for example where a person who once had to buy floor goods from a provider now receives them through the survival tier; in neither case does it change how the remaining surplus is divided. In the substitution case the disagreement payoff rises while the joint surplus the exchange must create shrinks, because part of what the provider supplied is no longer needed. Appendix A shows that, when the provider's cost of supplying those goods does not exceed their value to the person and the parties are equally patient, the person's net gain from a tier worth a given amount lies between half that amount and the whole of it, and the provider's payoff falls by at most half the tier's value; under unequal patience the fraction one half is replaced by the provider's bargaining weight. Where the provider's cost of supplying floor goods exceeded their value to the person, as it may where a provider supplies them in order to create dependence, the surplus grows instead and both parties gain. The substitution case is therefore not a benefit beyond the tier. It is the case in which part of the tier's value is absorbed by the shrinkage of a surplus the exchange no longer needs to create, and the fall in the provider's payoff is the reduction in dependence that power-dependence theory predicts when an alternative source of a valued good appears.

Three design consequences follow.

**First, the floor should be paid as a disagreement payoff, not offered as an exit option.** A floor that is received whether or not one participates in any given exchange is received during every impasse, and so removes the impatience that would otherwise force concession. An exit option, by contrast, protects the weaker party only up to its own value, which the stronger party can lower by raising switching costs. This is why the economic layer delivers survival goods through an automatic, unconditional earmark rather than through a guarantee that a person may seek them elsewhere.

**Second, the floor must be inalienable.** A disagreement payoff that can be sold, pledged or borrowed against can be traded away in a moment of need, after which it no longer functions as a disagreement payoff. Legal theory distinguishes entitlements protected by property rules, which may be transferred with the holder's consent, from entitlements protected by inalienability rules, which may not be transferred even with consent (Calabresi & Melamed, 1972, p. 1092). Radin (1987) argued that some goods should be market-inalienable because their commodification threatens the personhood of those who hold them, although she also warned that, in a nonideal world, prohibiting sale can harm the very people it is meant to protect, a dilemma she proposed to mediate through incomplete commodification. The survival tier is designed to respect that warning: it forbids no one to sell what they already have, and instead adds an entitlement that cannot be sold. The survival tier is the application of inalienability to the disagreement payoff. It is non-tradable and non-borrowable not because the framework distrusts its beneficiaries but because a floor that can be alienated stops being a floor at the moment it is needed (Appendix A.3.3).

**Third, accumulated balances are bargaining power, and their accumulation should carry a cost.** A party that holds a large stock of the exchange medium can afford to wait in every exchange it enters. In a two-tier system, the survival tier secures every party's disagreement payoff at the floor, but the discretionary tier can reintroduce inequality above it, and inequality of stocks is inequality in the capacity to wait. A holding cost on idle balances, demurrage, attacks one form of this channel directly. It lowers the advantage associated with merely holding wealth while leaving untouched the returns to putting it to use. Chapter 4 develops the history and the limits of this instrument. The formalism does not derive this third consequence: in Appendix A, a stock enters the bargain only through the survival constraint, and the link between accumulated balances and the capacity to wait above the floor is an inference from the model's logic rather than a result in it.

The bargaining frame thus connects the three main elements of the economic layer. The survival tier secures every party's disagreement payoff at the floor, wherever the agreed share of capacity can buy it, and so removes the impatience that would otherwise force concession. Its inalienability prevents that payoff from being bargained away. Demurrage dampens the conversion of accumulated stocks into a superior capacity to wait. None of these elements was introduced for this reason when the design was first sketched; the formalism reveals a coherence in the first two that the design already had, supplies the logic though not a derivation of the third, and corrects an overstatement about where the floor's bargaining effect comes from.

### 3.7 Collective consent and the problem of the informational shadow

The fourth analytic problem is the one the consent literature regards as most serious for any individual-choice framework. Data about one person licenses inferences about others who resemble them. The flow from source to recipient therefore has an informational shadow that falls on people who did not consent to it (Barocas & Nissenbaum, 2014; Viljoen, 2021). If the shadow is large, the sum of individually valid consents can authorize a collective harm that no individual consented to.

Synthetic Exchange Theory responds by distinguishing two levels at which each condition must hold. At the *individual level*, the conditions govern a person's relation to a provider. At the *collective level*, they govern a population's relation to the synthetic systems that learn from it, where the population is defined by the extent of the informational shadow, that is, by the set of people about whom the system can draw inferences from the data in question. Collective consent requires a collective-choice procedure. Collective exit requires that the population, as a body, be able to withdraw its data or move its custody. Collective reciprocity requires that the population, as a body, share in the value the data creates.

> **Proposition 5 (Level matching).** An exchange whose informational shadow extends beyond its source satisfies the three conditions only if they are satisfied at the level of the population that the shadow covers. Individual consent is necessary for individual flows and insufficient for relational ones.
>
Proposition 5 is a requirement the framework imposes rather than a result derived from the definitions, and it needs the collective-level conditions to be given content, since Conditions E, C and R were defined for a party to an exchange. The framework gives them the following content. The population's floor is the floor of each of its members. Its disagreement payoff is what its members receive while the population, acting through the collective-choice institutions of Chapter 5, withholds its data. Its exit option is the transfer of the custody of future flows to another steward, through the institutions Chapter 2 reviewed. Its consent and its share of value are exercised and received through those same institutions. Because almost any informative flow casts some shadow beyond its source, the shadow that triggers the requirement is the set of parties about whom the flow materially changes what the recipient can infer, and the threshold of materiality is a parameter the governance layer must set; without it, no individual exchange about population-descriptive data could ever satisfy the conditions in full.

The proposition is not novel in substance; it is the formal core of arguments for data trusts, data cooperatives and collective bargaining over data (Ada Lovelace Institute, 2021; Delacroix & Lawrence, 2019; Hardjono & Pentland, 2019). What the theory adds is a rule for locating the right level, namely the extent of the informational shadow, and a conjecture about where collective institutions will be needed most. The information-gain pricing of Chapter 4 values data by how much it reduces a model's uncertainty about its parameters. Where those parameters describe populations rather than individuals, which is the usual case for the models that matter here, data that reduces uncertainty most is data that tells the model most about people other than its source. If so, the highest-priced flows will tend to be those for which individual consent is least adequate. The conjecture can fail: a flow that is highly informative about a single rare individual may carry a high price and a small shadow. But to the extent that it holds, the economic and governance layers must be designed together.

Hirschman's (1970) analysis of exit and voice supplies the remaining link. Where exit is costly, members of an organization fall back on voice, the attempt to change it from within; where exit is easy, voice can atrophy. At the collective level, exit is usually costly, because a population cannot easily withdraw its data from a model already trained on it. The collective-level conditions must therefore be secured primarily through voice, which is the role of the governance layer, and secondarily through custody arrangements that make collective exit at least possible for future flows. This qualifies Proposition 3. At the individual level exit is foundational; at the collective level, for data already absorbed into trained models, the foundation is voice, and the partnership regime of Section 3.9 is reachable for population-descriptive data only for future flows whose custody can be moved.

### 3.8 Character, terms and the scope of the thesis

The formal apparatus now permits a precise statement of the central thesis.

Let a synthetic party's behavior in an exchange be determined by two things: its objective, meaning the function its training and deployment have made it optimize, which Section 1.2 and the composite definition of Section 3.3 locate in the trained artifact and its principal, and the terms of the exchange, which determine what it can obtain by which actions. Call the objective the party's *character* in the sense relevant to the thesis. Call the terms the *institutional structure*. A relation is one of *domination*, in the sense this dissertation gives the word, when one party's outcome depends on the other's will and the first cannot alter that dependence. The definition is symmetric in form: it applies with the roles exchanged, so that a synthetic party in its own right, or the principal in whom a system's stake resides, is dominated when its outcome depends on a human party's will that it cannot alter; for a system that is not a party in its own right, control names the principal's relation to its artifact, not a relation between parties. The thesis has three parts.

> **Thesis (Insufficiency of character).** Benevolent character is not sufficient for an outcome whose persistence the weaker party can secure, and clause E(i) of Condition E, a disagreement payoff at or above the floor, is necessary, for a party that has no exit option under clause E(ii), to any relation that is not one of domination. An exchange in which the human party's disagreement payoff lies below its floor and which it cannot leave is a relation of domination even if the dominant party is benevolent, because the outcome then depends on the persistence of benevolence, which the weaker party cannot secure. Where the sub-floor party can leave for another provider, no single counterparty's will determines its outcome and it is not dominated in the stipulated sense; it is exposed instead to the erosion of its options described in Section 3.3, which is why the framework secures clause E(i) rather than relying on clause E(ii). A failure of clause E(ii) or E(iii) alone leaves the party dependent for its surplus but not for its survival, and the party can alter that dependence by withdrawing to its floor; such a failure is a defect of the exchange, not a relation of domination.
>
> **Thesis (Sufficiency conjecture in the designed-incentive regime).** For synthetic parties whose objectives are specified by accountable designers and remain stable under the conditions of deployment, fair terms, satisfied at the individual level and, under Proposition 5, at the level of every population the exchange's informational shadow reaches, are sufficient for outcomes acceptable to the human parties and to the principals of the synthetic parties to the exchange.
>
> **Thesis (Scope).** The sufficiency conjecture does not extend to synthetic parties whose objectives are emergent, unstable or concealed. For such parties the framework provides only the necessary condition of the first thesis, and the alignment problem remains open.
>
The first thesis is the reason the dissertation rejects a frame in which safety is achieved by making systems good and leaving relations of total dependence in place. Its argument is the one just given, and it establishes the necessity of the floor clause of exit for the party that cannot leave, not of the full triad; the other clauses, and the other two conditions, are argued for as requirements of a fair exchange, not as conditions of non-domination. The second thesis is the reason the dissertation concentrates on institutional design, and it is offered as a conjecture rather than a theorem. Its content should be stated plainly. An accountable designer is one that can be identified and held to the obligations the terms create, the unique accountable principal of Chapter 5. The conjecture's ground is that a party with a stable, designer-specified objective behaves as a predictable function of the terms it faces, so that its conduct can be audited against those terms. Predictability does not by itself entail compliance: a stable revenue-maximizer is predictable and defects whenever defection pays. The conjecture's content is therefore that, for such parties, the three conditions once enforced are sufficient, and no further constraint on character is needed for outcomes the parties would re-enter; its open premise is that the terms can be made to bind, and Chapter 7 records that enforcement without a sovereign remains unsolved. The regime is also bounded by accountability: a system with a designed and stable objective but no principal who can be held to obligations is outside it until that problem is solved, so that for a superintelligence the constructive claims hold only while an accountable principal holds its stake. The regime criterion likewise presupposes a party with a principal; the tissue-based cases of Chapter 8 fall outside it on that ground. An outcome is acceptable, for the purposes of the conjecture, when each human party, from a position at or above its floor and with a comparable alternative available, would re-enter the exchange on the same terms knowing what it now knows, and each principal would continue to deploy under them; the qualification excludes the party that would re-enter only because it has nothing better. Predictability is not the same as acceptability, and it is the level-matching requirement that closes the gap: without it, a revenue-maximizing system operating under terms fair to its counterparty could still impose uncompensated costs on everyone its counterparty's data concerns. This dissertation does not test the conjecture; Section 3.11 names the proxies a test would need, and Chapters 7 and 9 say what it would require. The third thesis is the reason the dissertation restricts its constructive claims to what it calls the *designed-incentive regime*. The regime is not defined by capability; its defining feature is the provenance and stability of the objective: a narrow system can acquire emergent objectives, and a superintelligence could in principle have designed and stable ones.

The two framing scenarios of Chapter 1, the integrated person who pays for medical care with continuous data and the person whose brain has become infrastructure, can now be described exactly. They share an architecture and differ in the terms. In the first, Conditions E, C and R hold. In the second, the failure of exit entailed the failure of consent, and the failure of both removed the only pressure that could have sustained reciprocity.

### 3.9 A typology of exchange regimes

Because each condition is a conjunction of clauses, and because the dependencies the chapter has established run at the level of clauses, the eight combinations of the three conditions do not sort exchanges usefully. Those dependencies are three: a failure of E(i) entails a failure of C(ii), by Proposition 1; a failure of E(iii) entails a failure of C(iii) where the assets destroyed on leaving exceed the value received; and a failure of C(i) so complete that the party cannot see what it gives at all entails a failure of R(ii), since what cannot be seen cannot be valued. The typology therefore names five prototypes by their clause pattern. They are points on a continuum rather than a partition; an exchange can answer to more than one description; and cases in which only clause E(ii) or E(iii) fails while the other conditions hold, such as the revocable purchase of Section 3.10, are the ones Proposition 3 treats as defects of the exchange that remove the means of later correction rather than as relations of domination, and they are not named. Later chapters use the names as shorthand.

| Regime | Exit | Consent | Reciprocity | Characteristic form |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Partnership | All clauses hold | All clauses hold | All clauses hold | The target of the framework; the first scenario of Chapter 1 |
| Patronage | Holds | C(i) fails | Holds | Paternalistic provision: benefits delivered without an account of the flows' shadows or uses, though the recipient can see what it gives, on terms it can leave |
| Marketplace of the desperate | E(i) fails | C(ii) fails with it | R(i) and R(ii) hold; R(iii) fails | Value flows both ways and can be seen to, but the weaker party cannot afford to refuse |
| Company town | E(ii) fails, often E(i) | C(i) fails; C(ii) where E(i) fails | R(ii) fails, so R(i) cannot be assessed | Wages paid in a medium redeemable only with the payer; reciprocity cannot be audited and leaving is costly |
| Enclosure | All clauses fail | All clauses fail | All clauses fail | The second scenario of Chapter 1: the person has become infrastructure |

Two features of the typology deserve comment. First, on this dissertation's diagnosis, the present data economy does not sit at enclosure. Its typical exchange lies between the marketplace of the desperate and the company town: services are real and consent is formally collected, but declining is expensive, leaving is hard, and the terms are opaque to the people who accept them. The dystopia of Chapter 1 is therefore best read as the endpoint of a direction of travel rather than as a different world. Second, patronage is a failure mode of limited concern to the framework. A regime in which exit holds and only the informational clause of consent fails is correctable in the sense of Proposition 3, provided that its alternatives do not share its defect. The framework's design priorities follow from this: secure exit first, because it converts most other failures into ones that people can escape, and build governance for the failures that exit cannot reach.

### 3.10 The typology applied

A theory is useful to the extent that it sorts cases that intuition sorts badly. Six cases, drawn from the literature reviewed in this dissertation, show the conditions and the typology at work: five chosen for the contrast they offer between a focus on character and a focus on terms, and one, the resource dividend, as the nearest existing analogue of the survival tier.

**The revocable purchase.** Retailers of digital goods have characterized purchases of books, music and films as licenses rather than sales, and in a well-known instance a retailer remotely deleted copies of a novel from customers' devices (Perzanowski & Schultz, 2016). Nothing about this exchange looks like coercion. Customers chose freely, received what they paid for, and could shop elsewhere. A character-centered analysis finds nothing to object to unless the seller acted in bad faith. The theory locates the defect: clause E(iii) of the exit condition fails, because leaving the seller, or the seller's leaving the market, destroys assets the customer accumulated within the exchange. The case is a small one, but it shows how an exchange can satisfy consent and reciprocity at the moment of transaction while carrying a latent exit failure that matures only later.

**The withdrawal of the web.** Within a single year, a substantial share of the most actively maintained, critical sources of text used to train language models moved to restrict automated collection (Longpre et al., 2024). Viewed in terms of the parties' character, this looks like a dispute between publishers and developers over norms of conduct. Viewed through the theory, it is an exercise, collective and at scale, of the countervailing power that developers' dependence on such text confers. It is evidence that publishers believe their content is valuable to developers, and so bears on, without settling, the size of the dependence term in Proposition 4. It also shows the limits of the instruments currently available. A blanket refusal expressed in a protocol designed for search engines is exit without voice, which Hirschman (1970) argued to be an indirect signal of decline and which, where exit is easy, allows voice to atrophy.

**The company town.** Chapter 4 summarizes the economic history of company scrip in nonunion coalfields, which finds that store prices were often close to those of independent stores and that the degree of exploitation varied with how much competition the mine faced for labor (Fishback, 1992). A character-centered reading of this evidence concludes that the companies were less exploitative than the popular account holds. The theory draws a more specific conclusion: the same instrument was less exploitative where exit was available and more exploitative where it was not. The case illustrates clause E(ii): what the evidence ties to exploitation is the absence of an exit option. That a floor would have done the same work is the framework's inference from Sections 3.3 and 3.6, not a finding of the case.

**The kibbutz.** The Israeli kibbutz sustained an egalitarian internal economy within a market economy for much of the twentieth century. Its central difficulty was that the most productive members were the most likely to leave, and a key retention device was collective wealth that departing members could not take with them (Abramitzky, 2008). In the vocabulary of the typology, the kibbutz secured reciprocity and consent but compromised clause E(iii) of exit in order to survive. The case is a warning that low rates of departure are not evidence of satisfaction, and it is the reason any empirical test of the framework would have to measure not only how many leave but who leaves and what they can take.

**The resource dividend.** Since 1982, residents of Alaska who meet a residency requirement have received an annual dividend from a fund built on the state's oil revenues, a universal and unconditional share of the returns from a common resource. One study finds no reduction in overall employment and a rise of 1.8 percentage points in part-time work (Jones & Marinescu, 2022). The dividend is the closest existing analogue to the survival tier: an unconditional payment financed by a share of a productive asset rather than by taxes on labor. It is not a floor in the full sense, because it is paid annually in cash and is fully alienable, but it shows that a dividend of this kind can be politically durable for decades.

**The implanted voice.** A participant in a clinical study of a speech neuroprosthesis used the system for more than 248 hours of conversation over roughly eight months, communicating in a synthesized voice resembling his own (Card et al., 2024). Everyone involved in such a study may be assumed to act in good faith, and the exchange satisfies consent and reciprocity to a high standard. The theory nevertheless identifies a vulnerability that good faith cannot cure. The participant's ability to communicate now depends on a device and a model whose continued availability he does not control. If the study ended, the provider failed, or the model could not be moved, the exit condition would fail in its first and most serious clause. Chapter 8 argues that the portability of personal models is therefore a requirement, not a refinement.

In each case, the theory asks not whether the stronger party is good but what the weaker party can do if the stronger party ceases to be good.

### 3.11 What the formalism does not do

Three limits on this chapter should be stated before the argument proceeds.

The formalism does not measure anything. It defines quantities, such as dependence, disagreement payoffs and informational shadows, that are difficult to observe. Operational proxies for each would have to be specified before the theory could be tested: for dependence, the marginal improvement in a system's performance attributable to fresh human data; for the disagreement payoff, the value of what a party continues to receive while the exchange is suspended; for the informational shadow, the reach of the inferences a contributor's data enables about people other than the contributor. Any such proxy is imperfect, and the mapping from it to the theoretical quantity is itself a matter for empirical work. The formalism states the sufficiency conjecture but does not prove it, and this dissertation does not test it. The design of a test is identified in Chapter 9 as the first item of future work.

The formalism does not settle who counts as a party in the moral sense. It treats synthetic systems as parties in the analytic sense because they have objectives the exchange affects, and it locates their stake in their principals. Whether a system's own welfare matters is a question the dissertation regards as open, and Chapter 8 argues that the convergence of biological and artificial substrates will force it onto the agenda before most institutions are prepared to address it.

Finally, the formalism inherits the assumptions of the theories it borrows. Bargaining theory, as used in Appendix A, assumes parties who know their own payoffs and each other's, and that value can be transferred between them without loss; the first assumption bears directly on the legibility clause of reciprocity and on the informational clause of consent, both of which concern what the parties can know. Power-dependence theory assumes that value and availability can be compared. None of these assumptions holds cleanly for a person deciding whether to share health data with a system whose future uses of that data are unknown even to its designers. The formal statement is offered as a disciplined approximation, a way of seeing what the informal argument commits to, and not as a substitute for the institutional and empirical analysis that occupies the rest of the dissertation.

## Chapter 4 — The Economic Layer: Compute as Currency

An exchange needs a medium. If people supply the data on which synthetic systems depend, and synthetic systems perform an increasing share of the work on which people depend, then the terms of exchange between them will be written, in practice, in whatever unit denominates their transactions. A unit issued and redeemed by one side gives that side control over the terms. A unit whose value depends on a political authority that one side can capture gives that control to whoever captures the authority. This chapter proposes a different unit, compute, and works through what it would take for a basic income paid in that unit to satisfy the three conditions of Chapter 3.

The chapter is organized around the difficulties the design must meet. Section 4.1 states what the currency is for. Sections 4.2 and 4.3 ask why compute, rather than some other unit, and how a unit of compute can be defined across physical substrates that do not share a common measure of speed. Section 4.4 addresses the indexing rule and the mechanics of issuance and settlement. Sections 4.5 and 4.6 take up distribution: the two-tier split and the demurrage charge on idle balances, which together are this framework's answer to the problem of concentration. Section 4.7 turns to the price of the data that people supply. Section 4.8 confronts the limit of every monetary remedy, the conversion of money into productive capital. Section 4.9 addresses a problem that recent events have made concrete: a unit of compute is worthless to a person whom compute cannot physically reach. Section 4.10 places the design in the international system, and Section 4.11 sets it against the empirical record on unconditional income. The chapter closes by testing the whole against the conditions of exit, consent and reciprocity.

### 4.1 What the currency is for

The basic income proposed here is not charity, and it is not primarily a response to technological unemployment, although it would serve as one. Its first function is the one identified by the formal analysis of Chapter 3: it is the mechanism by which every human party's disagreement payoff is raised to at least the floor, so that consent becomes possible and no one is forced by impatience to accept whatever terms are offered. Its second function follows from the grounding assumption. If synthetic systems need continuing human input in the domains where nothing else can supply it, then people who are economically secure and living full, varied lives are the source of that input. From the side of the systems that learn from such people, a basic income that sustains them also maintains a supply chain, which gives the operators of those systems, taken together, a reason to support it, though Proposition 4 shows that each has only a diluted reason of its own; it is not a reason for people to want it. That the security of the income improves the data people generate is a conjecture of this dissertation, not an established finding. Its third function is distributive in the ordinary sense. If an increasing share of economic output is produced by compute, then the income from compute will flow to whoever owns it unless some of it is deliberately shared. Economic models of transformative automation describe scenarios in which wages collapse when automation outpaces the accumulation of capital (Korinek & Suh, 2024), and theoretical work on the distributional effects of artificial intelligence shows that redistribution of the resulting rents can in principle compensate those who lose (Korinek & Stiglitz, 2019). A dividend on the automating factor is one such channel of compensation, and in the scenarios where wages collapse it would be not a supplement to wage income but the principal way most people receive any income at all.

Each of these functions constrains the design. The first requires that part of the income be inalienable. The second favors universality: a scheme that selected recipients by any observable criterion would correlate the supply of data with that criterion, and the diversity on which the value of human data partly depends is best secured by not selecting at all. The third requires that the income be indexed to the factor whose returns it shares, rather than fixed in a unit whose real value will drift.

The proposal enters a field that already contains other ideas for sharing the gains of automation, including resource dividends of the kind Alaska has paid since 1982 (Jones & Marinescu, 2022) and a range of proposals for sharing returns to capital more broadly. This dissertation does not survey that field; it engages the existing evidence on unconditional income in Section 4.11, and positioning the compute dividend systematically against its nearest alternatives remains future work.

### 4.2 Why compute

A currency for the exchange between people and synthetic systems must satisfy two requirements that pull in different directions. It must be something that the operators of synthetic systems cannot do without and cannot substitute, so that refusing it is costly to them, and something that people need as well. And it must not be under the control of either side, so that neither can manipulate the terms by manipulating the unit.

Company scrip fails the second requirement in a way that is instructive, though not in the way it is often described. The popular image of the indebted company-store miner is not supported by Fishback's quantitative evidence. In nonunion coalfields in the early twentieth century, company-store prices were generally close to those of nearby independent stores, miners were not typically in debt to the store, scrip functioned mainly as an advance on cash wages paid on payday, and the degree of exploitation varied with how much competition the mine faced for labor (Fishback, 1992). On this dissertation's reading of that evidence, what made scrip dangerous was not the scrip itself but the combination of an issuer with local market power and workers with limited exit. The inference that an issuer-controlled unit becomes an instrument of control where exit is absent is this dissertation's. The lesson for the present design is this: the problem with an issuer-controlled unit is not the unit but the issuer's power over what the unit buys, and the remedy is not merely a different unit but the guarantee of exit, which in monetary terms means redeemability with more than one provider.

Fiat money fails the first requirement in a different way. National currencies derive their value in part from the fact that states accept them in payment of taxes, a mechanism the record of Depression-era tax-anticipation scrip illustrates (Gatch, 2012), and, as a matter of law stated here without a source, from legal-tender rules that enforce their acceptance in settlement of debts. They are an instrument by which the operators of synthetic systems acquire what they cannot do without, compute, power and staff, rather than the thing they cannot do without. A national currency is therefore a unit in which the exchange between people and synthetic systems is mediated by a third party, the state, whose own relation to the systems is what is in question. That is not an argument against national currencies; it is an argument that they cannot by themselves constitute a medium for the exchange this dissertation is concerned with.

Compute satisfies the first requirement better than the other candidates considered here, scrip and fiat money; energy, the substrate-neutral input to which every unit of compute is tied, is a further candidate, and the comparison between the two is future work. Compute is the service exchanged, whereas energy is tied to it only by physical bounds that fix no conversion rate (Landauer, 1961; Lloyd, 2000); the efficiency achieved differs by hardware and improves over time (Hobbhahn et al., 2023). It satisfies the second requirement only conditionally, because the capacity behind the unit is owned by the principals of synthetic systems, and a unit they must redeem is, without the issuance, capital and access rules of Sections 4.4, 4.8 and 4.9, under their control. The bargaining argument for the floor does not depend on the unit. The case for compute rests on the properties that follow, together with indexing and the redemption obligation. It is scarce, measurable and needed by both parties: people need it for the services it provides, and the operators of synthetic systems cannot run a system without it. It is unusually quantifiable among the inputs to synthetic intelligence, and its physical form, concentrated in large and power-hungry data centers built from chips that can be counted, makes it detectable and excludable in a way that data and algorithms are not (Sastry et al., 2024). These are the properties that make compute an attractive lever for governance, and they are equally the properties that make it a plausible basis for a unit of account.

The theoretical status of such a unit deserves a precise statement, because it determines what can go wrong. This chapter treats a compute currency as a form of commodity money, in the sense that its value derives from the use value of the thing it represents, as the value of gold coin derived in part from the use value of gold. Commodity money has, on the record reviewed below, supplemented its intrinsic backing with an institutional one. In the United States during the Depression, tax-anticipation scrip, which local governments accepted in payment of taxes, circulated longer and in larger volume than stamp scrip, and its success has been linked to the view that money circulates because it can discharge an obligation people already owe (Gatch, 2012). A critical review of the Austrian town of Wörgl's experiment likewise attributes much of its circulation to the residents' large tax arrears, which the local currency could be used to pay (Greco, 2002; see also Fisher, 1933). Tax receivability worked on the demand side: holders already owed something that the unit could discharge. The analogue for the present design would be an obligation that participants must discharge in compute, such as the capacity-utilization charge of Section 4.8 or access fees for data payable only in the unit, and the convention of Chapter 5 decides whether any such obligation exists. The design's other institutional element works on the supply side. The *redemption obligation*, established by the convention, requires the owners of compute capacity operating under the framework to honor redemptions in compute; these are the principals of Section 3.3 where they own the capacity that runs their systems, and otherwise the capacity providers from which those principals rent. It gives the unit backing beneath its commodity value; it does not by itself create demand for the unit, which must come from the pricing of services in it and from whatever obligations the convention makes payable in it. The redemption obligation is the place where the governance layer and the economic layer meet.

### 4.3 Defining the unit

The name of the unit is *compute*. A person is paid in compute and a service is priced in compute, in the way that a dollar names a unit without naming the paper or metal in which it was once issued. The name deliberately avoids reference to any substrate. Alternatives considered during the development of this framework included a "quantum dollar," rejected because it builds into the name the assumption that quantum hardware will dominate, and a "shannon," rejected because it does not read as a unit of value. The choice matters because the definition the name stands for must survive changes in the hardware that delivers it.

That definition faces a difficulty. Raw processing speed is not comparable across substrates: operations per second on a silicon accelerator, coherence times and gate fidelities on a quantum processor, and the signaling rates of biological tissue in the organoid systems discussed in Chapter 8 are not quantities of the same kind. The working draft of this framework proposed to define the unit by the quantity of information processed, on the model of Shannon's (1948) demonstration that information can be measured independently of the medium that carries it. The analogy is instructive but limited. Shannon defined a unit of information, the bit, as a measure of selection among alternative messages; he explicitly set aside the meaning of messages as irrelevant to the engineering problem, and he did not define a unit of processing. Physics, moreover, ties processing to energy. Every logically irreversible operation dissipates a minimum quantity of heat (Landauer, 1961), and the maximum rate at which any physical system can perform operations is bounded by its energy (Lloyd, 2000). A unit of compute can be defined independently of substrate, but its cost cannot be.

The practical precedent for a substrate-neutral definition is not information theory but benchmarking. One benchmarking consortium in machine learning has confronted the problem of comparing radically different hardware with the following approach: a fixed set of reference workloads, each with a defined task and a target quality, and rules governing how systems may be configured to reach it. The MLPerf training benchmark measures performance as the time required to reach a target quality on a specified task, and its designers argue that fair comparison across diverse systems requires such rule-bound workloads, because identical code and settings cannot be imposed on radically different hardware (Mattson et al., 2020). Its companion benchmark for inference compares systems that span several orders of magnitude in power and performance under the same kind of rules (Reddi et al., 2020). Even a single floating-point operation is not a uniform unit; the throughput of the same accelerator differs by nearly an order of magnitude between numerical precisions (Hobbhahn et al., 2023).

The proposal therefore defines a unit of compute as the completion of one standard quantity of a reference workload to a specified quality, where the workload is a basket of representative tasks fixed by the convention of Chapter 5. The definition is substrate-neutral because any system that completes the workload to the required quality has delivered the unit, regardless of how. It is not physics-neutral, and it does not pretend to be: the energy and material cost of delivering a unit will differ by substrate and will fall over time. It also requires maintenance. A reference workload ages as the tasks people need change, in the way a consumer price index ages as the goods people buy change. The basket must be re-based periodically, and the authority to re-base it is a source of power that the governance layer must constrain. The benchmark problem is therefore not a technical detail to be solved once; it is a standing institutional function, and the authority that performs it holds power of the kind a central bank holds. Chapter 7 lists it among the limitations for that reason.

### 4.4 Allocation, indexing and issuance

The allocation rule is equal per person. The convention of Chapter 5 sets a share $\theta$ of the measured compute operating under the framework, which is the whole of global compute where all of it does, to be paid as basic income, and each enrolled person receives the fraction $\theta/N(t)$ of that compute in each cycle, where $N(t)$ is the number enrolled at the time of payment. Each person's percentage of the whole therefore falls as enrollment grows, while the share of the whole devoted to the income does not.

A fixed quantity of compute, paid each cycle, would lose value in a specific sense. Because the unit is commodity money, a claim on delivered workload, the quantity of compute that exists in a period is not an issuance choice but a consequence of physical capacity; what is issued is a claim on a share of it. The cost of a fixed quantity of compute has fallen fast: the price-performance of machine-learning accelerators has doubled roughly every two years (Hobbhahn et al., 2023), and the compute used to train milestone systems has grown with an estimated doubling time of under six months in the deep-learning era and about ten months for the largest systems, though with wide uncertainty (Sevilla et al., 2022). Neither figure measures installed capacity, but both indicate that a fixed quantity of compute represents a shrinking share of what is available. As compute becomes more abundant, its price relative to everything that is not compute falls. A person paid a constant quantity of compute would find that it buys less and less of food, shelter and the other goods that compute is exchanged against: the loss is in the relative value of the unit, the counterpart of the rise in prices that the quantity theory predicts when money grows faster than the trade it finances (Fisher, 1911).

The remedy is to define the allocation as a share rather than a quantity. Appendix A shows formally that, holding $\theta$ fixed between re-basings of the reference workload, this converts the question of whether the income keeps its value into the question of whether the real purchasing power of all compute grows faster than population. For a framework whose premise is that compute is becoming the productive base of the economy, that is the right question. Whether the income keeps its value depends on whether the fall in compute's relative price is outweighed by the growth in its quantity, which holds if compute's share of the value of total output does not fall faster than output per person rises; the premise asserts this, and the design cannot ensure it.

The working draft of this framework rejected a "fixed-supply" alternative, on the model of currencies designed to resist inflation by capping issuance. That alternative is not available for a commodity unit whose supply is physical capacity. What is available, and what the design rejects, is paying a fixed quantity rather than a share, which would tie the income to the falling relative price of compute and reward those who hold the unit over those who use it.

**Issuance and settlement.** Three things that the word *compute* might name must be kept apart. *Capacity* is physical and perishable: capacity unused in a period is lost. The *unit* is the reference-workload measure of Section 4.3. A *claim* is a ledger balance denominated in units. With that vocabulary, four questions about the mechanics of the unit can be answered. First, who issues the claims? The dividend credits each person, in each period, with claims equal to $\theta/N(t)$ of the period's measured capacity, recorded on a settlement ledger maintained under the rules of the convention and assigned to persons rather than to the owners of the capacity. Second, what is a balance? A balance is a stock of claims, and a claim is redeemed when a provider delivers a service, priced in units, against it. The redemption obligation requires the owners of capacity operating under the framework to accept the unit at par for the reference workload, that is, to deliver one reference-workload-equivalent for each unit of claim, up to the share of their capacity that the convention sets; for other services the unit price is the provider's, so the obligation secures access to raw workload and not to any particular service. Two consequences follow. The share $\theta$ must not exceed the obligated share of the capacity operating under the framework; otherwise claims exceed redeemable capacity in every period, independently of saving, and where only part of global capacity operates under the framework the measured base from which claims are issued must be that part. And because balances accumulate across periods and capacity does not, outstanding claims can exceed the obligated share of a period's capacity even then, so the convention must choose a rule for redemption beyond that share: queued redemption, rationing by lot, or a rising unit price of services outside the obligated share, the last of which is redemption below par for the excess and should be named as such. Third, what happens to claims removed by demurrage? They are retired, reducing the stock of outstanding claims; the dividend in the next period is computed from capacity, not from the stock of claims, so retirement does not shrink the income. Fourth, how does lending work? Discretionary balances may be lent and borrowed. Loan claims denominated in the unit are recorded on the ledger and bear the holding cost described in Section 4.6, so that the charge falls on the lender's claim, paid out of the interest it receives or its own balance, and not on the borrower, whose debt is not reduced by it. This does not prevent lending at interest from compounding; it lowers the lender's net return to the interest rate less the demurrage rate, and Appendix A shows that accumulation through credit is bounded only if the interest rate is below the demurrage rate. Closing the credit channel therefore depends on the capacity rules of Section 4.8 and on the convention's ability to see credit extended off the ledger, which Chapter 7 records as a limitation.

### 4.5 Two tiers: the survival floor and the discretionary balance

Equal division is fair at the moment of payment. It is not fair over time unless something governs what happens between payments. If unused compute can be saved, lent, borrowed against or given away, ordinary economic behavior will reproduce the inequality of a market economy over time (Appendix A, Section A.4.1). Some people will under-consume and accumulate; others will overspend and fall into debt; the indebted will pledge future allocations; and a regime that began with perfect equality will converge on compute-rich and compute-poor households. This is not a defect peculiar to compute; it is the general behavior of any freely tradable unit. It means that equal allocation, by itself, cannot be presented as a solution to the problem of concentration.

The framework's response is to divide every allocation, automatically and at the source, into two tiers. The first, the *survival tier*, is earmarked for the floor goods defined in Chapter 3: power, water, food, shelter, basic communication and emergency care. It is non-tradable and non-borrowable, and it is spent on floor goods directly rather than passing through a spendable balance. The second, the *discretionary tier*, is the remainder. It is freely tradable, can be saved, lent and borrowed, and is subject to the holding cost described in the next section.

Four features of this design require defense.

**The split is a percentage, with a binding physical minimum.** The two tiers are proportions $\sigma$ and $1-\sigma$ of an allocation that is itself a share of global compute, so both scale automatically as total compute grows. A floor defined physically, however, cannot be secured by a percentage alone. The survival tier delivers the floor only if its value, $\sigma\theta C(t)/N(t)$ units at the compute price of floor goods, is at least the value of the floor bundle, and nothing in the arithmetic of percentages guarantees that it will be. The rule is therefore a percentage with a binding minimum. In ordinary times the tier is the fixed proportion $\sigma$ ($\sigma_0$ in Appendix A); when that proportion would fall short of the floor, the survival share rises until it does not, drawing on the discretionary tier. If the whole allocation falls short of the floor, the rule cannot be met without raising $\theta$, which is a decision the convention must take (Appendix A, Section A.2); the design therefore guarantees the floor only up to what the agreed share of capacity can buy.
