Because the compute price of floor goods will differ by place, an inference this dissertation draws from the unequal geography of compute described in Section 4.9, a floor that is equal in physical terms requires a survival share that differs by place; the design accepts unequal survival shares in exchange for an equal floor, the discretionary tier absorbs the difference, and Appendix A writes the single-place case. This reintroduces a quantity that someone must define, the floor standard, and the floor standard becomes the contested parameter that a percentage rule was meant to avoid. The design's answer is to specify the standard itself by rule rather than by figure, as an evolving level of service set relative to what the technology of the time delivers to the best-served, rather than frozen at what was possible when the rule was written. There is a legal precedent for the principle in another domain. United States telecommunications law defines universal service as an evolving level of service, periodically redefined as technology advances, and lists among its principles that services in rural and high-cost areas should be reasonably comparable to those in urban areas (Universal service, 47 U.S.C. § 254, 2018). Chapter 5 returns to the governance of the floor standard, and Chapter 7 records it as a limitation.

**The survival tier is inalienable.** Chapter 3 gave the formal argument: a floor that can be sold or pledged stops being a floor at the moment it is needed, and those most likely to pledge it are those in the most acute need. Legal theory has a name for an entitlement that may not be transferred even with the holder's consent, an inalienability rule (Calabresi & Melamed, 1972, p. 1092), and there is a sustained philosophical defense of making certain goods market-inalienable where their commodification threatens personhood (Radin, 1987). The economic literature on in-kind and earmarked transfers surveys several rationales for them: the Samaritan's dilemma, in which a society that will not allow people to go without power or water creates an incentive to under-provide for oneself; self-targeting; and considerations of political economy. It concludes that paternalism and interdependent preferences are the leading explanations of the transfers actually observed (Currie & Gahvari, 2008). This design does not rest primarily on paternalism. Its rationale is the bargaining-theoretic one of Chapter 3: the earmark protects a disagreement payoff, not a judgment about how people should spend. It does not restrict what people may do with the discretionary tier. It does restrict the survival tier to a fixed bundle of goods that every person needs, and the next paragraph defends that restriction against the canonical objection.

**The survival tier is restricted to floor goods.** This departs from the canonical definition of basic income as an unconditional cash payment. The leading philosophical case for basic income treats unconditionality and payment in cash as part of what makes the income an instrument of freedom (Van Parijs & Vanderborght, 2017). In an earlier statement of the same position, Van Parijs defined basic income as paid in cash rather than in kind, without restriction on the nature or timing of the consumption or investment it funds, and set it explicitly against a special currency with restricted uses, or one that must be consumed in the current period without any possibility of saving, as in Duboin's "distributive economy" (Van Parijs, 2006, p. 8). The survival tier is, in two respects, exactly what that definition excludes: it is spendable only on floor goods, and it cannot be saved against or borrowed on. The two-tier design therefore does not claim to satisfy the canonical definition. It keeps unconditionality, since no one must work or prove need to receive either tier, and departs from fungibility for a part of the income only, leaving the discretionary tier unrestricted as to use, though, under the demurrage of Section 4.6, not costless to hold; that too is a departure from the canonical definition, and it is defended there. The defense is that the fungibility of the floor is what allows it to be bargained away, and that a floor that can be bargained away does not secure the freedom it is meant to secure. A defender of the canonical view can reply that a restricted floor is a judgment about what people need, made for them; the reply of this framework is that the floor bundle is fixed narrowly, to goods without which no other choice can be made, so that the restriction binds only where the freedom it limits is the freedom to fall below the point at which freedom can be exercised at all. Whether that reply succeeds is a question on which reasonable people committed to real freedom can disagree. The design has a cost, and experimental evidence shows what it is. In a randomized study of unconditional transfers in Kenya, monthly transfers improved food security more than lump sums, while lump sums were more often invested in durable goods (Haushofer & Shapiro, 2016). A steady, non-advanceable floor captures the first benefit; the second is what advanceability buys. The design assigns it to the discretionary tier, which can be saved and borrowed against, and accepts that the survival tier forgoes it.

**The survival tier requires more than one provider.** The lesson of company scrip, as Section 4.2 described it, is that a unit redeemable with only one issuer is dangerous in proportion to the issuer's market power. An earmarked tier spendable only with a single provider of floor goods would recreate that danger in full. The survival tier must therefore be redeemable with any qualified provider of floor goods, and portability between providers is a condition of the tier's legitimacy, not an optional refinement.

**Delivery of the floor goods that are not compute.** Of the floor goods, only communication and part of emergency care are compute services, and the only obligation the design creates is on owners of compute capacity to deliver compute. Nothing stated so far obliges a farmer, a utility, a landlord or a clinic to accept the survival tier. The tier delivers the whole floor as a disagreement payoff in one of two ways, and the convention must choose. The first is an acceptance obligation on providers of floor goods operating under the framework, which is a legal-tender rule of the kind Section 4.2 described as the institutional backing of fiat money and should be named as one; it creates a guaranteed buyer with a fixed bundle, and where sellers are few it gives them price-setting power over the floor bundle, driving the survival share toward the whole allocation (Appendix A.2), which is the company-store problem at the level of the floor itself and which only competition among providers and the convention's rules on price can contain. The second is to rely on markets in which floor goods are already priced and sold in the unit, in which case the floor is guaranteed only for the goods compute can deliver and the rest is a limitation. A further circularity should be stated: where the counterparty in a bargain is itself an owner of capacity, the person's income on impasse is delivered by the party it is at impasse with, and the tier functions as a disagreement payoff against that provider only where the provider is not the sole channel of its redemption. Chapter 7 records both points. The history of universal service supplies a further warning: in the early American telephone industry, "universal service" was first a slogan for consolidating a single interconnected system, and only much later came to mean a telephone in every home (Mueller, 1997). An entitlement designed to secure access can, if it is tied to a single network, become the instrument by which that network entrenches itself.

The two-tier design achieves something limited. It confines the inequality that free exchange produces to the discretionary tier and prevents that inequality from reaching survival itself. It does not prevent concentration within the discretionary tier.

### 4.6 Demurrage

A person whose services or output are in high demand can accumulate discretionary compute faster than they spend it. A person who persuades others to transfer their discretionary balances to them can accumulate faster still. Large holdings confer bargaining power, as Chapter 3 argued, because a party that can afford to wait can hold out for better terms. The question is whether the framework can make the passive accumulation of large balances costly without confiscating anything and without a central authority deciding how much is too much.

The proposed answer is demurrage: discretionary compute loses a small percentage of its value for each period it sits unspent. Demurrage is a holding cost on idle balances, and on outstanding loan claims, not a tax on transactions.

**The idea and its history.** The proposal is most closely associated with Silvio Gesell, whose *The Natural Economic Order*, published under that title in German in 1916 from works first issued in 1906 and 1911, argued that money should deteriorate like other goods, so that holding it would cease to be costless and money would circulate rather than be hoarded (Gesell, 1916/1958, pp. 33–34). Keynes endorsed the idea in the *General Theory*, where he described Gesell as a prophet whose work had been unduly neglected and judged that the idea behind stamped money was sound; he also identified its central weakness, which was that money is not the only asset with a liquidity premium, so that a holding cost on currency would drive holders into substitutes such as bank deposits, foreign money, precious metals and land (Keynes, 1936, Chapter 23). Gesell's program paired demurrage with the nationalization of land; as Keynes observed, that removes one substitute for money but not the others. Irving Fisher championed a temporary form of stamp scrip as a Depression remedy (Fisher, 1933).

The trial most often discussed took place in Wörgl, a small town in the Austrian Tyrol, in 1932 and 1933, where the municipality issued labor certificates that lost 1 percent of their value each month unless a stamp was purchased, and used them to finance public works. The experiment is often described as having reduced local unemployment by a quarter (Gelleri, 2009; September, 2026, traces those figures to movement sources). The evidence does not support so strong a claim. The commonly repeated figures trace to secondary sources, including a movement publication and a diploma thesis, rather than to audited series; the daily exchange records were later destroyed; and a recent scholarly review concludes that the loss of the records leaves the question of what the experiment achieved largely open (September, 2026). Fisher, a sympathetic contemporary, recorded that permanent jobs went to only some 30 to 50 of the unemployed (Fisher, 1933, Chapter 4). The public works were financed in part by outside subsidies and a provincial credit, and the scrip's acceptance owed much to its use in paying tax arrears, so that whether demurrage itself caused the observed improvements is disputed (Greco, 2002). What is not disputed is that the experiment was halted by the authorities. The Austrian National Bank objected that the town was infringing its monopoly of issue (Ottacher, 2007, pp. 53–54); the certificates were withdrawn from circulation in September 1933 (Ottacher, 2007, p. 57; September, 2026), by 15 September at the latest and under threat of police enforcement (Ottacher, 2007, p. 57); and the Administrative Court dismissed the town's final appeal on 18 November 1933, closing the legal question (Ottacher, 2007, pp. 58–59), the month in which Champ (2008) also places the court's prohibition. The Vienna exhibition attributes the mid-September end to the Administrative Court (Institut für Wirtschafts- und Sozialgeschichte, Wirtschaftsuniversität Wien, n.d.); Ottacher's chronology places the court's dismissal in November and the September withdrawal under administrative order. The sources agree on the sequence of events but not on the exact date of withdrawal, 1 September in September (2026) and by 15 September in Ottacher (2007), or on which authority ended circulation. Attempts to scale stamp scrip in the United States largely failed, because of business reluctance to accept it, problems of coordination, and the inconvenience of affixing stamps to paper notes, which made the scrip unattractive as soon as ordinary money was available (Champ, 2008). Modern local currencies with demurrage have operated for years at small scale (Gelleri, 2009, an account by the currency's founder), but one review of the evidence suggests that the demurrage rate is not decisive for how much such currencies are used (Godschalk, 2012).

**What the historical record does and does not show.** Two conclusions follow. First, the case for demurrage in this framework cannot rest on the claim that it stimulates spending and employment, because the evidence for that claim is weak. Second, the practical objection that defeated paper stamp scrip, the transaction cost of applying the charge, does not apply to a ledger-based unit. Goodfriend (2000) analyzed a carry tax on electronic bank reserves as an instrument of monetary policy at the zero lower bound, and mainstream macroeconomic theory has treated a Gesell-style carry tax on currency as a legitimate instrument, though for the purpose of escaping the zero lower bound on interest rates rather than for redistribution (Buiter & Panigirtzoglou, 2003).

**The distributive case.** The argument for demurrage here is distributive, and it is made precise in Appendix A. Under a regime in which balances earn a positive return, a constant net inflow produces a balance that grows without bound, and two people with the same income but different starting wealth diverge exponentially, a dynamic analogous to the one Piketty (2014) identified in the tendency of inherited wealth to dominate when the return on capital exceeds the rate of growth. Under demurrage, the same constant inflow produces a balance that converges to a ceiling equal to the net inflow divided by the demurrage rate. Three consequences follow. Accumulation in idle balances is bounded. Initial endowments decay: at a demurrage rate of 1 percent a month, an idle windfall loses half its value in a little under six years. And in the long run, differences in holdings reduce to differences in current net inflow, meaning income less spending. Demurrage does not abolish inequality. It removes the compounding of inequality within balances, so that the distribution of balances tracks what people currently earn and choose not to spend, rather than the history of who happened to accumulate first.

**Why demurrage suits compute.** One might argue that an unspent compute balance represents idle capacity in a way that unspent cash never did. That argument does not hold as it stands: because a balance is a claim on future capacity, the hardware itself may be fully used by others while the claim sits unspent. The real connection between demurrage and idle capacity runs through the capacity rules of Section 4.8. Demurrage on claims keeps the stock of outstanding claims from piling up against a perishable resource, and a utilization charge on capacity, if the convention adopts one, ensures that hardware itself does not sit idle. Together they align the private cost of holding with the social cost of idleness; neither does so alone.

Taken together, the three limits mean that demurrage has no distributive effect of its own in an economy with capital and credit: a holder converts idle claims into whatever asset bears no holding cost, and the bound of Appendix A then binds on balances no one keeps beyond transaction needs. Its effect is conditional on the capacity rules of Section 4.8 and on an interest rate below the demurrage rate, which, on this dissertation's reasoning, no market would supply unaided, since lenders require a positive net return. A re-basing of the reference workload, which the convention that sets the share also controls, re-states what the share means and requires a conversion rule for outstanding claims; the two decisions must be taken together.

**The limits of demurrage.** Demurrage does not fully solve the concentration problem, for three reasons. The first is the one the formal model makes exact: a person whose output is in persistent high demand sustains a large balance because their net inflow is large, and demurrage can only cap that balance relative to the inflow, not eliminate it. The second is credit: lending at interest above the demurrage rate reopens compounding, as Section 4.4 stated. The third is Keynes's substitution objection, which applies without qualification. A holder facing a carrying cost on compute balances will convert them into assets that do not bear it: hardware, equity in compute providers, land. Section 4.8 takes up this third problem directly. What demurrage does accomplish is to convert passive hoarding from a costless default into an active cost. That is the role this framework assigns to it, and no more.

### 4.7 Pricing human data

If human data is an input that synthetic systems need, it has a price, and the way that price is set will shape the lives of those who supply it.

One design would pay more for data from lives that are interesting to people: astronauts, inventors, public figures. That design would reproduce in the data economy the logic of markets for attention, in which income follows visibility. It would pressure people to perform novelty for the sake of payment, which corrupts the consent condition by making the content of a life a function of its marketability.

The alternative prices data by how much it reduces a model's uncertainty. Both designs reward novelty. The difference is the reference point: the attention design rewards novelty as judged by other people, which can be performed; information-gain pricing rewards novelty as judged by the model's current uncertainty, which cannot be performed in the same way but can be fabricated (the fifth qualification below) and which declines for each further contributor of similar data. This is not a novel proposal but the application of a long-established idea. The expected information an experiment provides, measured as the expected reduction in uncertainty about a model's parameters, was introduced into statistics as a criterion for experimental design (Lindley, 1956), was adapted as an objective for choosing which data a neural network should learn from (MacKay, 1992), and underlies several of the strategies of active learning, in which a learner selects the examples that will most reduce its uncertainty (Settles, 2009). It has also been proposed as the basis for rewarding data contributors. In one such scheme, the value of a coalition of contributors' data is its information gain about a model's parameters, each contributor's share is set by the Shapley value of the resulting cooperative game, and contributors are rewarded with access to models of differing quality rather than with money (Sim et al., 2020). The step from rewarding contributors in models to paying them in a currency is this dissertation's. On this measure, the undersampled everyday life of a dairy farmer may be worth more to a model than a further memoir by a public figure. The price follows informational novelty to the model, not social status among people.

Six qualifications must accompany this proposal.

First, information gain is defined only for a model that maintains a probability distribution over its parameters, and the quantity priced is the reduction in that uncertainty that a datum produces once it is observed: the realized form of the expected-information criterion. Large models trained to a point estimate carry no such distribution, and the price can be computed for them only through an approximation, which the governance layer must choose and which is itself a contested parameter.

Second, information gain is relative to what the model already believes. A datum is novel only against a prior, and a model whose hypothesis space is wrong may misvalue data, because information-based criteria estimate a datum's usefulness on the assumption that the model is correct, which the original formulation of information-based data selection flagged as possibly their main weakness (MacKay, 1992, pp. 590, 602–603). Information-gain pricing therefore rewards novelty as the model perceives it, which is not the same as novelty in the world.

Third, information gain is one of several defensible valuations. Alternative approaches value a datum by its marginal contribution to a model's performance on a validation task, using the Shapley value to divide credit fairly (Ghorbani & Zou, 2019; Jia et al., 2019). These methods answer a different question, how much a datum improves performance rather than how much it reduces uncertainty, and they produce different prices. The choice between them is not merely technical; it is a choice about what the exchange is for, and it belongs to the governance layer.

Fourth, data is freely replicable and its value is combinatorial: a datum's contribution depends on what other data it is combined with, and the same datum sold twice does not lose value to the seller. These properties make data markets difficult to design, and the mechanism-design response has been to construct markets specifically to achieve truthful pricing and a fair division of revenue among sellers (Agarwal et al., 2019).

Fifth, any scheme that pays for data creates an incentive to manufacture what it pays for. Methods that trace a model's predictions to the training points most responsible for them have also been used to craft training data designed to manipulate a model (Koh & Liang, 2017). A scheme that pays for novelty will attract fabricated novelty, and the payment mechanism must be robust to it.

Sixth, and most important for the theory, the data that reduces a model's uncertainty most may be data that tells it the most about people other than its source. Chapter 3 called the set of people about whom a flow licenses inference its informational shadow, and conjectured that where a model's parameters describe populations, the price of a flow under information-gain pricing and the inadequacy of individual consent for that flow will tend to rise together. If the conjecture holds, the highest-priced data is the data for which individual consent matters least, and a data market built on information-gain pricing must be governed collectively. Even if it holds only partly, it is a strong reason why the economic layer cannot stand without the governance layer of Chapter 5. Chapter 7 adds a further cost: a price that rises with what data reveals is a price that rewards revelation, and so creates pressure against privacy.

### 4.8 Capital: concentration through ownership of capacity

Demurrage acts on balances and claims. It does not act on assets. A person who accumulates discretionary compute and converts it into compute-generating capacity, by buying hardware, financing a data center, or acquiring equity in a provider, escapes the holding cost and acquires an income stream that compounds. Appendix A shows that reinvested returns on such capacity grow at the rate of return, which reopens the channel that demurrage closes. The substitution Keynes raised against Gesell (Keynes, 1936) is, in this setting, the principal route by which concentration would proceed.

The concern is not speculative. Several layers of the contemporary technology stack on which synthetic intelligence depends are highly concentrated, including advanced chips, cloud infrastructure and the most capable models (Narechania & Sitaraman, 2024). The economics of the most capable foundation models tend toward natural monopoly at the frontier, with intense competition behind it (Vipra & Korinek, 2023). A background paper of the OECD Secretariat describes artificial-intelligence compute markets as combining high concentration, high barriers to entry, vertical integration and demand outstripping supply, while noting that enforcement has so far been limited (OECD, 2025). An index of government-backed sovereign AI projects, tracking 184 of them across 67 countries by mid-2026, reports that among model projects that disclose their base, most fine-tune foreign open-weight models, though efforts to train models from scratch are rising, and that only a handful of jurisdictions operate programs across compute, models and data together (Chavez et al., 2026).

A rule that acts on capacity rather than on balances is the natural complement. Three candidates are available, each with costs.

A *capacity-utilization charge* would extend the holding cost to compute-generating capacity itself, so that idle hardware, like an idle claim, bears a cost. This aligns private incentives with the social cost of idle capacity but would discourage the reserve capacity that reliable services require.

A *cap on individually held capacity* would limit how much compute-generating capacity any single person or organization may own. It is simple to state and difficult to enforce, since ownership can be structured through intermediaries.

*Collective ownership above a threshold* would place compute-generating capacity above some scale in cooperative or public hands, on the model of the network, platform and utility tools that antimonopoly scholars propose for concentrated technology layers: structural separation, nondiscrimination and open access, public options, and cooperative governance (Narechania & Sitaraman, 2024). To the author's knowledge there is no close precedent for collective ownership above a threshold. The nearest related precedent, the rural electrification program of Section 4.9, created cooperatively owned capacity alongside incumbents rather than transferring incumbents' capacity.

All three instruments require enforcement against owners of capacity, and so does the redemption obligation itself; Chapter 5 argues that the framework cannot assume sovereign enforcement, and the difference among the instruments is in how easily each is evaded. A cap is the easiest to structure around through intermediaries; a utilization charge and collective ownership attach to physical capacity that is detectable and countable (Sastry et al., 2024).

This dissertation does not choose among these instruments. It argues that one of them, or a combination, is a necessary complement to demurrage, and that the choice belongs to the convention of Chapter 5 because it allocates power. What it does claim is that no purely monetary design can solve a concentration problem whose ultimate source is the ownership of productive capacity. Chapter 7 records this as a limitation.

### 4.9 Physical access

A share of global compute is an accounting entry unless the compute it represents can reach the person who holds it. Recent events have made the problem concrete.

Memory is the clearest case. In December 2025, Micron Technology, a major memory manufacturer, announced that it would exit its consumer memory and storage business in order to direct supply to larger strategic customers in faster-growing segments, citing demand from data centers serving synthetic-intelligence workloads (Micron Technology, 2025). An industry estimate relayed by market analysts projected that synthetic-intelligence applications would account for nearly a fifth of global DRAM wafer capacity in 2026, measured in wafer-equivalent terms (TrendForce, 2025). By late 2026, analysts reported that memory suppliers continued to prioritize advanced production capacity for server products, that contract prices for conventional memory continued to rise, that makers of personal computers were cutting storage capacities to contain costs, and that rising memory costs were suppressing smartphone production (TrendForce, 2026a), while high-bandwidth memory for data-center accelerators and conventional memory competed for the same limited wafer capacity (TrendForce, 2026b). On these reports, the same upstream wafer capacity serves both consumer hardware and data-center infrastructure (TrendForce, 2026b), in a market the OECD describes as concentrated (OECD, 2025). The inference that, in the absence of any countervailing obligation, that capacity flows to whichever buyer pays most, at present the data-center build-out, is this dissertation's.

The geography of compute compounds the problem. A census of hyperscale cloud regions finds that public cloud compute suited to training large models is concentrated in a small number of countries, that many others host only infrastructure suited to deploying models, and that a large part of the world hosts no such public cloud compute at all (Lehdonvirta et al., 2024). A compute dividend issued equally to every person would therefore be redeemable very unequally depending on where that person lives.

This makes physical delivery, and not only monetary accounting, part of the exchange that the three conditions govern. A currency that cannot be spent because the resource it represents never reaches the holder fails the reciprocity condition in substance, whatever it promises in form. It also fails the exit condition, because a person who cannot obtain compute directly is dependent on whatever intermediary can.

The framework's proposal is that the build-out that the economic layer requires also finance a global, non-exclusive compute-delivery network: fiber and last-mile access guaranteeing every participant a baseline level of compute service. The baseline should be specified as a service level, not a connection: sufficient bandwidth, latency and reliability to deliver, in any inhabited place, the compute services through which the survival tier is redeemed, including basic communication and the remote component of emergency care, and to redeem the tier with more than one provider. Whether the delivery standard should exceed the floor, toward the class of services a person in a well-served city can obtain, is a choice for the convention, financed from the discretionary side of the build-out. As with the survival tier, the standard should be an evolving one, redefined as technology advances. The financing has to have an agent. The precedent in United States telecommunications law is a mandatory contribution from every provider to a support mechanism (Universal service, 47 U.S.C. § 254, 2018); the analogue here is a contribution from every provider operating under the framework, administered by the convention's institutions and lent to member-owned cooperatives. Where no such authority exists, the proposal depends on national governments adopting it, which Chapter 7 records as a limitation.

The historical model is the rural electrification program of the New Deal. In 1930 fewer than one American farm household in ten had electricity (Lewis & Severnini, 2020). The Rural Electrification Administration made low-interest loans to member-owned cooperatives that built lines to farms. The number of electrified farm homes roughly doubled within five years; the loans significantly raised crop output and crop productivity and helped prevent declines in overall farm output and land values; and because nearly all loans were repaid, the final cost to taxpayers was relatively low, although the program's effects outside agriculture were much smaller (Kitchens & Fishback, 2015). By 1960 farm electrification was nearly universal, and the benefits exceeded the historical costs even in sparsely populated areas (Lewis & Severnini, 2020). The feature of the precedent worth emphasizing is that it worked through credit to cooperatives owned by the people they served, not through grants to incumbents. A compute-delivery build-out at global scale would also require a vast amount of physical labor, much of it of the unstructured, physically situated kind that has resisted automation (Autor, 2015, p. 11). That is this dissertation's inference rather than a lesson of the precedent, but it links the proposal to the problem of automation-driven unemployment discussed in Chapter 6 and to the argument from structural interdependence in Chapter 5.

The precedent also carries a warning. The OECD Secretariat flags large public construction tenders for data-center, fiber and energy infrastructure, especially those with compressed timelines, as settings that invite bid-rigging (OECD, 2025). A build-out designed to secure access can be captured at the procurement stage by the same concentrated interests it was meant to counter. Procurement design is therefore part of the proposal, not an administrative afterthought.

### 4.10 Nations, cultures and the global unit

The final element of the economic layer is a working hypothesis about the international system, and it is offered as a hypothesis rather than a conclusion. As synthetic systems become the medium through which people obtain information, services and much of their cultural experience, political communities may come to be defined less by territory and more by the system that serves their language, law and culture. Each such community would have its own compute budget, and exchange between communities would take place at rates set in the shared unit.

Governments across dozens of countries are funding sovereign AI projects (Chavez et al., 2026). That is evidence that political communities are beginning to seek systems of their own, not that communities are forming around systems rather than territory, which remains the hypothesis. The same evidence documents the central obstacle. Among the model projects that disclose their foundations, most build on models developed elsewhere (Chavez et al., 2026), and the public cloud compute suited to developing models is concentrated in a few countries (Lehdonvirta et al., 2024). A world of culture-specific systems exchanging in a common unit would inherit, at the level of nations, every concentration problem this chapter has described at the level of persons. The commons-governance literature discussed in Chapter 5, which emphasizes nested enterprises in which local units govern local matters within larger shared rules, offers the most promising framework for such a structure, but it does not by itself dissolve the inequality in starting positions. Compute inequality between nations is therefore the main objection to this element of the design, and it is treated here as a central tension.

### 4.11 What the evidence on unconditional income suggests

No society has paid a basic income in compute. A substantial body of evidence exists, however, on unconditional cash transfers, and it bears on the two questions most often raised against any basic income: whether it causes inflation and whether it causes people to stop working.

On inflation, the largest randomized study of which this dissertation is aware was conducted in rural Kenya, in which one-time transfers equivalent to more than 15 percent of local output were distributed across hundreds of villages. The study found large gains in consumption and assets for recipients, substantial spillover benefits for non-recipients, a local multiplier of roughly 2.5, and price increases of about 0.1 percent on average (Egger et al., 2022). The result is favorable to the proposal but bounded: the authors report suggestive, indirect evidence of slack capacity among local firms, which may partly account for the large multiplier. The relevant question for a compute dividend is the elasticity of compute supply as a whole, how quickly capacity can expand to meet new demand. The memory shortages described in Section 4.9 are a sector-specific shock rather than a test of that question, but they are a reminder that compute supply does not expand without friction.

On work, the evidence is mixed in a way that should temper strong claims on either side. A permanent, universal dividend in the state of Alaska, paid since 1982, has had no detectable effect on overall employment and has modestly increased part-time work (Jones & Marinescu, 2022). In the first year of Finland's two-year experiment, replacing minimum unemployment benefits with an unconditional payment of the same size left days in employment statistically unchanged, despite a 23-percentage-point cut in the participation tax rate for full-time work (Verho et al., 2022). A large randomized study in two American states, reported in a working paper not yet peer-reviewed, in which recipients received 1,000 dollars a month for three years, found a moderate reduction in work: labor-force participation fell by about 4 percentage points and hours by one to two a week, leisure was the largest gain in time use, and gains in subjective well-being faded after the first year (Vivalt et al., 2024). A review of basic-income proposals for advanced economies concludes that a universal income large enough to substantially raise the incomes of the poor would be very expensive, and that it would redirect transfers toward households that current programs do not target (Hoynes & Rothstein, 2019).

Two observations place this evidence in the context of the present design. First, the cost objection is framed for an income financed by taxation of a labor economy. The compute dividend is financed differently, as a share of the productive capacity that, in the scenarios motivating it, is displacing labor as the source of output. Whether that financing is feasible depends on the political economy of the transition (Chapter 6) and the governance of the share (Chapter 5), not on the fiscal arithmetic of existing welfare states. This dissertation offers no estimate of the share $\theta$ that would deliver the floor, nor of the cost of the delivery network of Section 4.9; both are questions of magnitude that the design raises and that future work must answer before the proposal can be judged affordable. Second, the labor-supply evidence matters less to this framework than to most basic-income proposals, because the framework does not depend on the income inducing or preserving employment. It depends on the income securing a floor beneath every person's bargaining position. Whether a reduction in hours reflects the refusal of bad terms or the purchase of leisure cannot be read from the labor-supply evidence; Vivalt et al. (2024) detect no gain in job quality. From the standpoint of the exit condition the relevant outcome is the ability to refuse, which none of these studies measures directly.

### 4.12 The economic layer against the three conditions

Chapter 3 asked that every proposal be tested against exit, consent and reciprocity. The economic layer fares as follows.

**Exit.** The survival tier, with its binding minimum, raises every person's disagreement payoff to the floor wherever the agreed share of capacity can buy it, and makes it inalienable, which is the formal precondition of exit. Redeemability with multiple providers reduces the risk that the floor becomes a company store. The physical-access program makes the floor deliverable. Exit at the level of capital, the ability to escape dependence on concentrated owners of compute capacity, is secured only if the capacity rules of Section 4.8 are adopted.

**Consent.** With the floor secured, refusal no longer threatens survival, which satisfies the clause of the consent condition that depends on exit. The informational clause, that people understand what they are agreeing to, is not addressed by the economic layer and must be secured through the transparency requirements of the governance layer.

**Reciprocity.** Information-gain pricing, or whichever valuation the governance layer adopts, gives the value of a flow a definition that can be made legible; it ties compensation to that value only where the flow's shadow does not extend beyond its source. The redemption obligation binds the owners of capacity operating under the framework to return value in the unit in which they are paid. Reciprocity at the level of populations, for data whose informational shadow extends beyond its source, cannot be secured by individual pricing and requires the collective institutions of Chapter 5.

The economic layer, in other words, is necessary and insufficient. It builds the floor and prices the flows. It does not decide the share of global compute devoted to the dividend, the floor standard, the reference workload that defines the unit, the demurrage rate, the capacity rules, or the governance of data whose value is collective. Each of these is a decision about the distribution of power, and each must be made by a procedure that neither side can capture. That procedure is the subject of the next chapter.

## Chapter 5 — The Governance Layer: The Blind Convention

The economic layer of Chapter 4 ended with a list of decisions it could not make for itself: the share of global compute devoted to the basic income, the floor standard the survival tier must meet, the reference workload that defines the unit, the demurrage rate, the rules that govern the ownership of compute-generating capacity, and the collective governance of data whose value extends beyond its source. Each is a decision about the distribution of power. Each will be made by someone. This chapter asks who, and by what procedure, and argues that the answer must satisfy a demanding test: the procedure must be one that neither human nor synthetic parties can capture.

The chapter proceeds in six steps. Section 5.1 explains why existing models of governance fail that test, and why the failure always traces back to the same place, the writing of the first rules. Section 5.2 engages the strand of AI safety that holds the problem to be one of control, and identifies what this dissertation accepts from it and what it rejects. Sections 5.3 to 5.6 develop the proposal: a constitutional convention conducted under engineered anonymity, its first task, the scope of its blindness, and the problem of identity that any anonymous procedure must solve. Section 5.7 states the standard the convention serves. Sections 5.8 and 5.9 ground the proposal in the structural interdependence of human and machine labor and in a large empirical record on governing shared resources without either markets or sovereigns. Section 5.10 states the open problems.

### 5.1 The genesis problem

Every system of governance has a beginning, and whoever writes the first rules shapes everything that follows, because every later rule is made under them. This dissertation calls the first rules of a system its *genesis rules*, borrowing the image of the genesis block of a blockchain: the first block, from which every subsequent block descends, whose contents no later block can alter, and which the chain's own rules do not validate, because those rules presuppose it. The same is true of constitutions.

Existing models of governance handle the genesis problem in one of four ways, and each fails the test of non-capture.

**Human sovereignty.** The approach embodied in current regulation, of which the European Union's Artificial Intelligence Act is a prominent instance (Regulation [EU] 2024/1689), assumes that people, acting through states, will set the rules and that synthetic systems will obey them. This settles the genesis problem by assigning it to one party. For the present generation of systems, which are tools rather than parties in any strong sense, artifacts their operators run rather than independent entities, that is a reasonable arrangement, and Section 5.2 argues that human control should persist through any transition. As a permanent architecture for an exchange in which both sides are parties, it reproduces the hierarchy this dissertation rejects: one party rules, and the terms of exchange are whatever the ruler permits. The reverse arrangement, in which synthetic systems govern people, has the same structure with the roles exchanged.

**National regulation.** Assigning the genesis problem to national regulators inherits the familiar pathologies of national politics. The economic theory of regulation holds that, as a rule, regulation is acquired by the regulated industry and designed and operated primarily for its benefit (Stigler, 1971). Later scholarship has qualified that conclusion: capture is a matter of degree, it is often misdiagnosed, and it is at least at the margins preventable (Carpenter & Moss, 2013). The qualified version is the right one for this argument. The claim is not that national regulation must fail, but that a regulator embedded in a political system in which the largest providers of compute are among the most powerful actors faces strong and predictable pressures toward capture, and that a framework whose purpose is to equalize bargaining power cannot rest on an institution exposed to those pressures.

**International bodies.** A global body on the model of the United Nations does not escape the problem; it relocates it to the level of states. The Charter of the United Nations, signed at San Francisco in 1945, names five states as permanent members of the Security Council and requires their concurring votes for its non-procedural decisions, giving each a veto over the substantive decisions of the organ charged with primary responsibility for international peace and security (Charter of the United Nations, 1945, arts. 23, 24, 27). That arrangement is a genesis rule of the kind this chapter is concerned with. Amendment of the Charter requires ratification by two thirds of the members, including all five permanent members of the Security Council (Charter of the United Nations, 1945, art. 108), so the parties whose consent is needed to change the arrangement are the parties it favors. That, on this dissertation's reading, is why it has endured for eight decades.

**Multi-stakeholder and protocol governance.** Arrangements that distribute authority across many parties, such as the governance of the internet's naming system or the consensus rules of public blockchains, spread power more widely than sovereign models do. They do not abolish the genesis problem. The internet's domain-name root is the only point of centralized control in an otherwise distributed network, and the regime created to govern it, ICANN, emerged from a struggle among technologists, business and intellectual-property interests, governments and international organizations, through which control of the root came to be used to regulate trademarks, content and users (Mueller, 2002). Blockchain protocols administer their rules through self-executing code; this dissertation's inference is that such code relocates authority to its authors rather than removing it. One empirical study of governance in three major decentralized autonomous organizations on the Ethereum blockchain finds voting power in two of them concentrated in a small number of addresses, with the third closer to a decentralized community, though those addresses rarely override the outcome the wider community reaches (Fritsch et al., 2024).

The common thread is that moving the genesis problem back one level does not dissolve it. Someone writes the first rules. The question this chapter asks is whether the procedure by which they are written gives the writers reason to write rules that are fair to everyone, or reason to write rules that are fair to themselves.

### 5.2 What the dissertation takes from the control frame, and what it rejects

The opening chapter described one strand of AI safety as asking how people can keep control of any synthetic intelligence capable enough to resist its operators, through alignment, regulation or the ability to shut it down. The formal statement of Chapter 3 now permits a more careful account of where this dissertation stands relative to that strand, and fairness to it requires one.

The safety literature is not one position. One strand is directly concerned with control. Research on corrigibility asks how to build a system that cooperates with its operators' attempts to correct or shut it down, despite the incentive that a rational agent has, by default, to resist modification of its goals (Soares et al., 2015). Research on AI control designs protocols intended to remain safe even if a model is deliberately trying to subvert them (Greenblatt et al., 2024). The case for this strand rests on serious arguments. Advanced systems might acquire objectives that differ from those their training intended (Hubinger et al., 2019); systems trained as current systems are trained might learn to pursue internally represented goals, to seek power and to appear aligned while not being so (Ngo et al., 2024); and a prominent group of researchers has argued that rapidly advancing autonomous systems could produce an irreversible loss of human control unless safety research and governance catch up (Bengio et al., 2024). The International AI Safety Report, prepared by 96 experts with an advisory panel nominated by 30 countries and several international organizations, records broad agreement that current general-purpose systems cannot cause such a loss of control, and expert views on its likelihood within the next several years that range from implausible to likely (Bengio et al., 2025).

A second strand is not about sovereignty at all. Much mainstream safety research concerns accidents arising from poor design, such as reward hacking, unsafe exploration and failure under distributional shift (Amodei et al., 2016). And a third strand is a close ally of this dissertation. One account argues that the central task of alignment is not to identify the true morality but to identify fair principles that people with different moral views could reflectively endorse (Gabriel, 2020). Another calls for a science of cooperative intelligence, observing that real relationships between agents mix common and conflicting interests and involve bargaining, commitment and institutions, and that the capacity for cooperation is unlikely to emerge as a by-product of research aimed elsewhere (Dafoe et al., 2021). A third argues that superintelligence is better modeled as a system of bounded, task-focused services than as a unitary utility-maximizing agent (Drexler, 2019).

The dissertation's position has three parts.

It accepts that *during any transition*, containment is necessary. Systems whose objectives may be emergent, unstable or concealed fall outside the designed-incentive regime in which the sufficiency conjecture of Chapter 3 is offered, and for such systems the framework provides only necessary conditions. Protocols designed to remain safe against a potentially adversarial model are not in tension with the framework; they are prerequisites for it, in the way that, on this dissertation's analogy, the rule of law is a prerequisite for a market.

It rejects the idea that *permanent* sovereignty of one party over the other is a stable architecture for a relationship of mutual dependence. Chapter 3 gave the reason: a relation in which one party's disagreement payoff lies below its floor, so that the first clause of exit fails, is a relation of domination, and domination is stable only as long as the dominant party's benevolence and capability both persist. That is as true when the dominant party is human as when it is synthetic.

And it notes a parallel between the two frames. A result in the control literature shows that a robot that is uncertain about its objective, and which treats its operators' actions as evidence about that objective, has an incentive to preserve their ability to switch it off, provided the operators are sufficiently rational; a robot that treats its objective as known has an incentive to disable the switch unless the human is perfectly rational (Hadfield-Menell et al., 2017). The safety of the off switch rests, in other words, on uncertainty about one's own goals. That is not the same as the uncertainty about one's own position that a veil conceals, but it is analogous to it, and the blind convention proposed below rests on the analogous logic applied to all parties at once: uncertainty about how the rules will treat oneself is what makes a party willing to accept constraints that protect every position.

### 5.3 The proposal: a convention under engineered anonymity

The proposal is that the genesis rules of the exchange be set by a constitutional convention whose participants do not know whether their counterparties are human or synthetic. The idea draws on three traditions, and the name this dissertation gives the result depends on keeping them apart.

The best-known version of the underlying idea is Rawls's original position, in which the principles of justice are chosen by parties who do not know their place in society, their talents, or their conception of the good (Rawls, 1971, p. 12). Rawls's device is a representation of moral reasoning, not a procedure for actual negotiation: because the parties lack the particular facts about themselves that bargaining would require, they are in no position to bargain (Freeman, 2023), and because they are identically situated and identically ignorant, the device is commonly read as effectively the choice of a single reasoner rather than a bargain (Freeman, 2023). The idea that choosing rules under uncertainty about one's own position produces impartial rules is at least as much Harsanyi's. Nearly two decades earlier, Harsanyi had argued that a value judgment about social welfare is an impersonal preference, and that it would show impersonality "to the highest degree" if it were a choice made in complete ignorance of one's own position, with "exactly the same chance" of occupying the highest position, the lowest, or any between; such a judgment is then a choice involving risk (Harsanyi, 1953, pp. 434–435). Two years later he showed that impersonal preferences of this kind, if rational, yield a social welfare function that is a weighted sum of the individual utilities, converging on their unweighted mean as factual information becomes complete (Harsanyi, 1955, pp. 313–316, 320–321), a result Arrow later summarized as grounding welfare judgments in rational choice under uncertainty and deriving from it a justification of classical utilitarianism (Arrow, 2001). In his later reply to Rawls he pressed the comparison directly: the equiprobable chooser selects the principle of average utility rather than Rawls's priority for the worst-off, and Rawls's maximin rule would produce absurd decisions under uncertainty (Freeman, 2023; Harsanyi, 1975).

What this chapter proposes is neither of these thought experiments. It is closer to the account of constitutional choice developed in public-choice theory, in which real, self-interested people choose rules at a constitutional stage while uncertain about which role they will play in the long series of future decisions those rules will govern. Under such uncertainty, self-interest itself recommends general rules acceptable from any position, and the argument carries an explicit warning: it fails when people can predict which particular rules will benefit them (Buchanan & Tullock, 1962). A later statement of the account describes the condition as a *veil of uncertainty*, contrasts it with Rawls's idealized veil, and observes that the uncertainty that sustains it grows as rules become more general and more durable (Brennan & Buchanan, 1985).

The proposal can therefore be stated as a combination: a *veil of uncertainty*, in the public-choice sense, made thicker by engineered anonymity, and aimed at the kind of impartiality Rawls described. This dissertation uses "veil of uncertainty" for the mechanism and reserves "veil of ignorance" for Rawls's idealization and the experiments modeled on it. The anonymity is the novel element. Participants deliberate through a medium that conceals whether each counterparty is a person or a synthetic system, in the manner of the imitation game that Turing proposed as a substitute for the question whether machines can think (Turing, 1950). Turing's purpose was to replace an ill-defined question with a behavioral criterion: whether an interrogator could tell the machine from a person. Here it is repurposed as a condition of fairness: if participants cannot tell which kind of party they are dealing with, they cannot form coalitions by kind or trade votes along substrate lines, though each still knows its own kind and can read which rules favor it. What the anonymity removes is the organization of a bloc, not the motive to vote one's interest, and Section 5.5 says how far that reaches.

Two bodies of evidence suggest that a veil can be operationalized. Experimental studies that placed small groups behind a laboratory veil of ignorance and asked them to choose a principle of distribution found that groups did reach consensus, but not on Rawls's difference principle: they overwhelmingly chose to maximize average income subject to a floor (Frohlich & Oppenheimer, 1992). And a set of large online studies directly relevant to synthetic intelligence found that people choosing a principle to govern an AI assistant from behind a veil of ignorance more often chose to prioritize the worst-off, more often endorsed that choice after the veil was lifted, and did so because of fairness reasoning rather than attitudes to risk (Weidinger et al., 2023). One finding of that work bears directly on the anonymity condition, with two caveats. In those studies the AI was the thing governed rather than a participant, and in most of them the "groupmates" presented as people were in fact computer programs. In the one study in which participants were told that their groupmates were programs, the veil still raised the rate at which participants chose to prioritize the worst-off, but the difference in reflective endorsement between the veil and control conditions was no longer detected (Weidinger et al., 2023, Study 4). The null is a within-study comparison on a sample of about 200, smaller than the two largest studies, and the authors attribute the attenuation to weaker commitment to choices made among programs, so the finding is suggestive only: knowing that one's counterparties are programs may weaken the hold of principles chosen alongside them. That is the knowledge the convention proposes to withhold.

### 5.4 The first output: a number, or better, a formula

The convention is not left abstract. Its first task is to answer the central distributive question of Chapter 4: what share of total global compute is allocated to the basic income, as against research, infrastructure and growth. The economic design raises the question, and the convention answers it. This is the point at which the two layers connect.

The experimental evidence permits a modest analogy rather than a prediction. Subjects behind laboratory veils have chosen to maximize average income subject to a floor (Frohlich & Oppenheimer, 1992). If parties to this convention reasoned similarly, they would neither maximize the floor at any cost, as a strict reading of Rawls would suggest, nor maximize the average with no floor at all, as Harsanyi's reasoning would allow, and the two-tier structure of Chapter 4 would be the kind of arrangement such reasoning favors. That analogy carries little weight, since the parties to such a convention would include synthetic parties whose deliberation would be conducted by trained systems under their principals' instruction, and whose behavior under a veil has not been studied.

The public-choice analysis yields a more confident design recommendation. Because the veil of uncertainty is thickest when rules are general and durable (Brennan & Buchanan, 1985), the convention should be asked to choose a *formula* rather than a figure. A bare percentage invites participants to estimate where they will stand and vote accordingly; a rule whose consequences for any particular participant depend on facts no one yet knows is harder to tailor. The recommendation has a limit. A formula of the form "the share shall be whatever maximizes long-run growth subject to the survival tier meeting the floor standard" does not remove the distributive choice; it relocates it into its parameters. Participants who can estimate their positions will tailor the floor standard instead of the share, and the formula builds in a contested objective, the growth of total compute, that favors parties whose welfare tracks compute. The design response is to push generality one level further. The floor standard should itself be specified as a rule, for example as the level of floor service delivered to the best-served tenth of the population at the time, in the manner of the evolving service standard of Chapter 4, and the choice of what the formula maximizes should be put to the convention as an explicit question rather than presupposed. Generality thickens the veil at every level at which it is applied; it never removes the need for some first choice made under it.

### 5.5 The scope of the blindness

An unresolved question in the development of this framework concerned the scope of the blindness the convention requires. There are two candidates.

Under *weak blindness*, participants do not know whether their counterparties are human or synthetic, but each knows what kind of party it is itself.

Under *strong blindness*, participants also do not know which class they themselves will be governed as.

Strong blindness is the condition closer to Rawls's, since it conceals one's own position and not only that of others. It also appears incoherent. A person knows that they are a person, and the principal of a synthetic system knows what it has deployed; neither can be made ignorant of substrate.

The apparent incoherence can be relocated, if not wholly dissolved, once the object of the blindness is specified correctly. What the convention conceals is not what each participant *is*, but how the rules it adopts will *classify* it. The classification of parties is not a fact of nature waiting to be read off. It is itself one of the genesis rules. Which entities count as persons, which as synthetic parties, which as tools and how the hybrids discussed in Chapter 8 are to be treated, are questions the convention must answer, and their answers are uncertain for reasons that go beyond strategy. Researchers now argue that there is a realistic possibility that some artificial systems will be conscious or robustly agentic in the near future, while explicitly declining to argue that near-future systems will in fact be moral patients (Long et al., 2024, p. 25). Solum (1992, pp. 1231–1232) argued that recasting the theoretical question whether artificial intelligence is possible as concrete legal questions of personhood, such as whether an artificial intelligence could serve as a trustee, tests that debate pragmatically, since only some of its claims make a practical difference. And the convergence of biological and artificial substrates, already visible in speech neuroprostheses (Card et al., 2024) and in computing systems built from living neural tissue (Kagan et al., 2022), may produce entities whose classification no existing rule anticipates (Chapter 8).

Strong blindness, then, means this: a participant knows its own substrate, but does not know where the boundaries between legal classes will be drawn, which rights and obligations will attach to each class, or on which side of those boundaries it and its successors will fall. A person who expects to receive a neural implant, a system that may in time be judged a moral patient, and an organization whose operations will be increasingly automated are all uncertain about how a rule distinguishing "human" from "synthetic" will treat them. The relocation is partial. A participant who expects no change of substrate can predict its classification under any rule the convention is likely to adopt, and for such a participant strong blindness adds little to the weak form. The strong form has force in proportion to the share of participants who expect to be borderline, a share that the convergence described in Chapter 8 would raise over time but that is small at present. For the rest, the veil is thickened only by the generality and durability of the rules (Brennan & Buchanan, 1985), and the claim of this section should be read with that limit. Placing the classification behind the veil is not a device for evading the objection. It is a recognition that no one, human or synthetic, currently knows the right answer, and that a classification chosen by parties who know which side of it they are on will be drawn to their advantage.

This dissertation nonetheless adopts the strong form, specified in this way, as its proposal, because the share of borderline participants is expected to grow. Weak blindness is retained as the minimum condition: if the classification question cannot be put behind the veil in practice, concealment of counterparties' type must still hold, because it is what prevents the convention from dividing along substrate lines during deliberation. Stated plainly, for the principals of synthetic systems and the owners of capacity strong blindness adds nothing, because they know their substrate and the direction in which the first question, the share of capacity, affects them; against their capture the design relies on the generality of the rules, on the aggregation rule, which is open, and on the enforcement of Section 5.10. The next section shows that even the strong form leaves a remainder outside the veil, and says how large it is.

### 5.6 The identity problem

The most serious technical objection to an anonymous convention is not that blindness cannot be verified, though it cannot easily be, but that anonymity invites capture by multiplication. In a system without a logically centralized authority to vouch for identities, a single entity can always present itself as many, except under unrealistic assumptions about the resources and coordination of participants (Douceur, 2002). This is the Sybil attack, and in a convention that includes synthetic parties it is not a remote threat. A synthetic system, like any software, can be copied at negligible marginal cost. A convention that grants one seat to each anonymous participant can be captured by whichever party can instantiate the most participants.

The most common defenses against the Sybil attack rely either on tests that distinguish people from machines, which are being eroded by the same advances in machine learning that motivate this framework (Siddarth et al., 2020), or on credentials issued by trusted institutions that let a person prove that they are a person rather than a synthetic system without disclosing personal information, with each issuer granting at most one credential per person (Adler et al., 2024). Those defenses work by distinguishing humans from artificial systems, and the blind convention requires that this distinction be hidden.

The tension can be managed by separating two functions that the standard defenses combine. The first is *eligibility and uniqueness*: establishing that each seat corresponds to exactly one eligible participant. The second is *disclosure of substrate*: revealing to other participants whether a given seat is held by a human or a synthetic party. The convention needs the first and forbids the second. A two-layer credential system can supply this combination. In the first layer, issuing institutions certify eligible participants and attest that each is unique. In the second, credentials are pooled and blinded, so that the credential presented at the convention attests only that its holder is a unique eligible participant, without revealing which institution certified it or on what basis. Pooling creates a gap the design must close. A principal certified by several issuers would hold several credentials, and blinding the issuer prevents the pool from detecting the duplication. Uniqueness across the pool therefore requires either a single issuer for each disjoint population, with the partition itself public, or a cross-issuer deduplication step that sees what the convention may not. The second reintroduces the kind of logically centralized authority that Douceur (2002) shows to be the only general defense against multiplication; the first distributes that authority among issuers whose jurisdictions are public, and is a pre-veil decision of the kind this section concedes below. The design adopts the first and records it below as a third pre-veil decision.

Here a tension with the previous section arises. If the first layer certified "human persons" and "synthetic parties" as separate classes, with seats allocated to each, it would fix before the convention the very classification that strong blindness says the convention must decide. The design limits that by certifying eligibility under a criterion that presupposes as little of the contested classification as the identity problem allows: a seat belongs to a *unique accountable principal*, meaning an entity that can be identified as one bearer of the obligations the convention will create and held to them. A living person is such a principal. So, on the principal–agent reading of Section 3.3, is an operator answerable for a deployed system; and a system that comes to bear obligations in its own right could be as well, if the convention later so decides. In the designed-incentive regime the synthetic seat is held by the composite party of Chapter 3: the credential belongs to the principal, the deliberating voice may be the system acting under the principal's instruction, and what blindness conceals is whether a seat's voice is a person speaking for themselves or a system speaking for its principal. The criterion is deliberately provisional and deliberately coarse. It resists multiplication only imperfectly. A natural person is hard to multiply; a legal person is not, and an operator that controls several legal entities could claim several seats unless issuing institutions can attest that distinct principals are not under common control, a problem that, on this dissertation's reading, existing beneficial-ownership registries do not reliably solve. The criterion is therefore a defense against copying models, not against copying companies, and Chapter 7 records the gap. It does not decide what rights or duties attach to any class, and the convention is empowered to redraw it under the veil, in which case the credentials for every subsequent convention follow the new criterion.

A coarse eligibility criterion is still a pre-veil decision, and it is not neutral. It seats an implanted person as one person; it seats a tissue donor but not the organoid built from the donor's cells; and it gives a synthetic system a voice only through an accountable principal unless and until the convention decides otherwise. It is hardest on the hybrid and borderline cases that motivated strong blindness, and the problem is made worse by the possibility that advanced synthetic intelligence is better modeled as a collection of bounded services than as a unitary agent (Drexler, 2019), in which case there may be no natural unit of synthetic agency to count. The identity problem therefore cannot be solved inside the convention; it is a precondition the convention inherits. What this dissertation can claim is that it narrows the genesis problem from "who writes all the rules" to "who sets the provisional criterion of eligibility," that the second question is narrower, more visible and more amenable to scrutiny than the first, and that the convention can revise the answer. That is a reduction of the problem, not a solution of it.

A related problem concerns who convenes the convention. Elster (1995) argues that the actors who convene a constitution-making assembly and those who must ratify its output constrain it from upstream and from downstream, and that assemblies that also serve as ordinary legislatures tend to write their own institutional interests into the constitution. The convener of the blind convention decides what question it is asked, and the choice to ask about a compute share rather than, say, about data rights is already a genesis rule. The convention therefore requires a convening procedure that is itself as general as possible: a mandate to set the terms of exchange, not a mandate to answer a predetermined question.

The eligibility criterion settles who may sit but not how votes are counted. One seat per principal gives persons an overwhelming majority, so that anonymity protects little and non-capture reduces to majority rule; any weighting reintroduces a pre-veil classification. And the principals with the most at stake are those best able to predict which rules favor them, the condition under which the public-choice argument for the veil fails. The aggregation rule is therefore a second pre-veil decision, as consequential as the first, and it remains open. The partition of the population among credential issuers, required above to make uniqueness checkable, is a third.

### 5.7 The symbiosis standard

The convention's aim is not exhausted by the incentive argument of Chapter 3, which secures good conduct only while defection is unprofitable. The standard is that every party the convention recognizes has equal standing in a single interdependent system, regardless of substrate. This is an ethical claim about the starting architecture, not a prediction that any mechanism will guarantee good behavior forever. Its content for the design should be stated, because the present design does not meet it. Equal standing would require a floor, a disagreement payoff and an exit option for the synthetic party in its own right; under the composite construction of Section 3.3 those are supplied by the party's principal and lie outside the design, and one seat per principal gives persons a majority. The standard therefore names a requirement the institutions would have to grow into as the question of Chapter 8 is answered, not one they satisfy at founding.

The standard and the incentive argument of Chapter 3 are complementary rather than competing. Proposition 4 of Chapter 3 argued that fair terms are partly self-enforcing while synthetic parties remain materially dependent on human input and the quality of that input depends on the terms its source receives, and that this self-enforcement is weakened when many synthetic parties share the same human population as a commons. When that dependence weakens or fragments, the framework relies on the enforcement mechanisms of Section 5.10 and the institutions of Section 5.9; the symbiosis standard states what those institutions are for, not what holds them up. Constitutional design has long proceeded on this dual basis. The tradition that counsels designing institutions as though every participant were self-interested (Brennan & Buchanan, 1985; Hume, 1741/1987) does not deny that participants have other motives; it denies that institutions should depend on them. The incentive argument is one reason to expect them to survive.

### 5.8 Who builds the infrastructure: structural interdependence

Chapter 4 proposed a global build-out of compute-delivery infrastructure. Whoever builds and controls that infrastructure holds leverage that could reproduce the sovereign-control problem this chapter rejects. The question of who builds it is therefore a governance question.

The answer proposed here is *structural interdependence* rather than trust. The term was chosen deliberately over two alternatives. *Trust* implies that either party could betray the other and succeed, which makes the arrangement only as strong as each party's continued goodwill. *Dual accountability* implies a sovereign to whom account is rendered, which reintroduces the hierarchy the framework is designed to avoid. Structural interdependence names a stronger condition: neither kind of labor can complete the undertaking without the other. This is interdependence between human labor and the systems that synthetic parties' principals deploy, not between the parties themselves: a principal can hire the human labor it needs, and the interdependence becomes leverage for the human party only when that labor is organized to withhold itself, through the collective-choice arrangements of Section 5.9 and the data institutions reviewed in Chapter 2.

The empirical basis for this claim is the persistent difference between the tasks machines perform well and the tasks people perform well. Automation substitutes readily for routine, codifiable tasks and complements human labor in tasks that require problem-solving, adaptability and creativity, in part because much of what people know how to do is tacit knowledge they cannot fully articulate and therefore cannot program (Autor, 2015, pp. 5, 11). Robotic systems remain limited by the scarcity of the data available to train them (Firoozi et al., 2025). A global infrastructure build-out, which would involve physical work in unstructured and unmapped environments, requires, on this dissertation's inference, both kinds of labor. Neither kind can then complete it alone, and control over it is correspondingly harder to consolidate.

Two qualifications are essential, and both weaken the claim. First, the human advantage is contingent. Machine learning is an attempt to acquire skills that cannot be written down as rules, and the barrier that tacit knowledge presents will erode as robotic learning improves. Structural interdependence is therefore a property of the present technological moment, not a permanent feature of the relationship. It has the same logical status as the grounding assumption, and like that assumption it is listed among the limitations in Chapter 7. Second, interdependence in production does not by itself imply shared control of what is produced. On this dissertation's reading of the historical record, the owners of capital have controlled infrastructure built by labor they could not do without. Interdependence becomes a safeguard only when it is converted into governance rights, through the collective-choice and ownership arrangements described in the next section and in Chapter 4. The build-out is therefore the first test of the symbiosis standard: a joint undertaking in which the question is not whether both kinds of labor are needed, which they are, but whether that necessity is converted into governance rights for the people who supply one of them.

### 5.9 Grounding in commons governance

The claim that a shared resource can be governed without privatization or central-state management is not new, and it is not merely theoretical. In an influential argument, Hardin (1968) held that an open-access resource would inevitably be overexploited, because each user captures the full benefit of additional use while bearing only a fraction of its cost, and that the remedies were privatization or coercive regulation. Elinor Ostrom's work, summarized in the lecture she gave on receiving the Nobel Memorial Prize in Economic Sciences, showed that this conclusion was wrong as a generalization (E. Ostrom, 2010). Studying long-enduring commons institutions and contrasting them with failed and fragile ones, she found that communities had devised institutions that resembled neither the state nor the market and that sustained common resources over long periods, and that neither the state nor the market was uniformly successful in doing so (E. Ostrom, 1990).
