In *Governing the Commons*, Ostrom identified seven design principles that characterized all of the long-enduring cases, plus an eighth, nested enterprises, found in the larger and more complex ones (E. Ostrom, 1990, Table 3.1, p. 90); she later restated them, adopting refinements from a systematic review of the evidence (Cox et al., 2010; E. Ostrom, 2010). They are: (1) clearly defined boundaries, both of the resource and of who may use it; (2) congruence between rules and local conditions, including proportionality between the costs participants bear and the benefits they receive; (3) collective-choice arrangements, under which most of those affected by the rules can participate in modifying them; (4) monitoring, by participants or by people accountable to them, of both use and the condition of the resource; (5) graduated sanctions, imposed by participants or by officials accountable to them, beginning low and increasing with repeated violation; (6) conflict-resolution mechanisms that are rapid, low-cost and local; (7) minimal recognition by external authorities of the participants' right to devise their own rules; and (8) for resources that are part of larger systems, nested enterprises, in which governance activities are organized in multiple layers.

Two cautions govern the use of these principles here. Ostrom presented them as regularities observed in successful cases, not as sufficient conditions, and later remarked that she should perhaps have called them best practices rather than design principles (E. Ostrom, 2010); in 1990 she had presented the list as tentative (E. Ostrom, 1990, p. 90). And her cases were local and regional resources, not a global commons. Ostrom and her collaborators have argued that the principles, grounded in the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation, apply to many kinds of groups beyond those managing natural resources (Wilson et al., 2013), and knowledge and information have been analyzed as commons using her framework (Hess & Ostrom, 2007). But the extension to a global compute commons is this dissertation's own inference, and it should be read as one.

With those cautions, each principle maps onto an element of the framework, and the mapping reveals both strengths and weaknesses.

**Boundaries.** These are the identity problem of Section 5.6 in another vocabulary. Ostrom's principle requires that the line between legitimate participants and others be clear and locally understood. An open, anonymous convention is close to the opposite. The principle is therefore not a support for the proposal but a constraint it must satisfy, and the two-layer credential system, with its provisional criterion of eligibility, is the framework's attempt to satisfy it.

**Congruence and proportionality.** These support the two-tier structure of Chapter 4. The survival tier provides what every participant needs regardless of contribution; the discretionary tier, with information-gain pricing, ties benefits to contributions; demurrage dampens, without preventing, the compounding of past accumulation. principle 2, congruence with local conditions, is in tension with a uniform global unit, and the culture-specific systems of Chapter 4 and principle 8 below are the framework's response to that tension.

**Collective choice.** This corresponds only partly to the blind convention. Ostrom's principle concerns ongoing participation in modifying operational rules; the convention is a founding procedure. The framework satisfies the principle only if the amendment path of Section 5.10 is itself a collective-choice arrangement open to those affected, which the chapter proposes but does not design.

**Monitoring.** This bears on a limitation that would otherwise stand without remedy, that the blindness of the convention cannot be verified. Ostrom's evidence suggests that the relevant question is not whether verification is perfect but whether monitors are accountable to participants. Monitoring of the convention's procedures by a body accountable to the participants as a whole, rather than to any class of them, is the framework's analogue. The analogue is imperfect: a monitor able to confirm that the blindness held must be able to see what the participants could not, and is therefore a trusted authority whose own accountability is a fourth pre-veil decision. Chapter 7 treats this as a mitigation rather than a solution.

**Graduated sanctions.** These bear on the problem of corruption. A commons does not require a perfect enforcer. It requires escalating, proportionate consequences, imposed by participants or bodies accountable to them, that make small defections costly before they compound into systemic capture. Applied to the framework, an owner of capacity that fails to honor redemptions, or a provider that withholds floor goods, faces sanctions beginning with publicity and escalating through suspension of its eligibility to transact in the unit. The same principle addresses the commons problem of Proposition 4: a synthetic party that degrades the human population all synthetic parties depend on is a defector against the commons, and graduated sanctions are the instrument for making that defection costly to its principal rather than to everyone.

**Conflict resolution.** This supplies a partial answer to the problem of enforcement without a sovereign. Low-cost, local arenas for resolving disputes between participants reduce the burden on any central enforcer. Proposals for regulatory markets, in which governments set required outcomes and licensed private regulators compete to supply the means of meeting them, suggest one architecture that is neither stateless nor sovereign (Hadfield & Clark, 2026).

**Recognition of rights to organize.** This names the most important external condition for any transition toward the framework, and for any attempt to test it: existing states must at least tolerate the existence of a compute commons governed by its own participants. No design internal to the framework can guarantee this.

**Nested enterprises.** This is the principle that corresponds to the hypothesis, introduced in Chapter 4, of culture-specific systems operating under a shared global unit. It is the right principle for that structure, and the governance theory of polycentricity, in which many formally independent centers of decision-making may function as a coherent system through competition, contracting, cooperation and recourse to central conflict-resolution mechanisms (E. Ostrom, 2010; V. Ostrom et al., 1961, pp. 831–832), is the theoretical framework within which the structure should be developed. But the systematic review of Ostrom's principles found the weakest evidence of the eight for nested enterprises, which had the only association significant at the 10 percent rather than the 5 percent level, though its authors still judged the evidence moderately supportive (Cox et al., 2010, Table 3). The part of the framework that depends on it most heavily, the international structure, is correspondingly the most speculative.

Ostrom's record matters for this dissertation because it shows that the core structural claim, that a shared resource can be governed without privatization or central-state management, is not speculative. It has been observed to work, and to fail, under documented conditions, within the tolerance of a state that recognized the arrangement, which is the condition principle 7 names. That gives the limitations of Chapter 7 a comparison class grounded in real institutions rather than only in analogy.

### 5.10 Amendment, enforcement and the open problems

Three problems remain beyond the aggregation rule of Section 5.6, and the proposal answers each only in part.

**Amendment.** The compute-share rule cannot be both unchangeable and legitimately revisable. Comparative evidence on constitutional amendment finds that amendment rates are largely explained by the interaction of a constitution's length and the difficulty of amending it, that moderate amendment rates are associated with longevity, and that beyond a certain point making amendment more difficult is an inefficient way to keep the amendment rate moderate (Lutz, 1994). This argues against entrenching the founding rules absolutely, and for keeping them short. Theories of constitutional precommitment treat constitutions as devices by which a polity binds itself against its own predictable tendency to make unwise decisions; Elster (2000) examines that view and questions how far the analogy with an individual binding himself holds, since those who write a constitution typically bind others. The restraining function is achieved partly by built-in delays and by demanding amendment procedures (Elster, 1995), and constitutional history records rare periods of broad popular mobilization, which Ackerman distinguishes as constitutional politics from normal politics, in which fundamental law has been remade (Ackerman, 1991). The recommendation that the convention choose formulas rather than figures serves amendment as well as founding: a formula needs revision less often than a number, and each revision occurs, like the founding, under some uncertainty about its particular effects.

**Re-running the convention.** Periodic re-founding seems a natural remedy for drift, but the veil thins with repetition. Each round's outcome is public, each participant's interests become better known, and the uncertainty that sustained the first round cannot be recreated. This follows directly from the public-choice account, in which the impartiality of constitutional choice depends on participants being unable to predict which rules will favor them (Buchanan & Tullock, 1962), and Chapter 7 evaluates it as a limitation.

**Enforcement.** If a principal, a provider or a nation refuses to pay its share, something must compel it. The framework's answer has three parts, none sufficient alone: the self-interest of synthetic parties in a healthy exchange while the grounding assumption holds (Chapter 3), Ostrom's graduated sanctions and conflict-resolution mechanisms, and enforcement at the level of protocol, in which the rules of the unit are embedded in the systems that settle transactions. Protocol enforcement must itself be designed and trusted by every class of party, and on this dissertation's reading of protocol enforcement, given in Section 5.1, it relocates rather than removes the genesis problem.

The governance layer therefore does not claim to have solved the problem of founding a fair order between parties of different kinds. It claims something narrower and more defensible. A convention conducted under a veil of uncertainty thickened by engineered anonymity, asked to choose general formulas rather than particular figures, constrained by a credential system that separates uniqueness from disclosure, and embedded in commons institutions of the kind Ostrom documented, is designed, on the argument of this chapter, to produce genesis rules less exposed to capture than the four models of Section 5.1; this dissertation argues that claim but does not test it. And such a convention narrows the irreducible remainder of the genesis problem to a small number of visible and revisable preconditions. Whether such an arrangement could be founded depends on circumstances that institutional design cannot create. The next chapter considers what those circumstances might be.

## Chapter 6 — The Transition Problem

A fair design is not adopted on its merits alone. The institutions proposed in Chapters 4 and 5 would redistribute bargaining power away from those who currently hold it, and those who currently hold it have no reason to accept them voluntarily. This chapter therefore asks under what circumstances such an arrangement might come into being.

The chapter's answer is conditional, and it limits what the rest of the dissertation may claim about adoption. The large-scale institutional redesigns the literature below describes for the most part followed the failure of the order they replaced, and did not always move in the direction of greater fairness. The chapter describes three slow-building pressures that could remove the slack from the present order, identifies a specific and already visible candidate for the kind of acute shock that has historically opened the way to redesign, and then confronts the possibility that such a shock would produce something worse than the present order rather than something better. It closes by stating what the chapter does and does not establish.

### 6.1 Crisis and institutional change

The proposition that fundamental change requires crisis has advocates across the political spectrum. Milton Friedman wrote that "only a crisis—actual or perceived—produces real change," and that when it occurs, "the actions that are taken depend on the ideas that are lying around" (Friedman, 1962/2002, p. xiv). Karl Polanyi argued that the attempt to treat labor, land and money as commodities in a self-regulating market produced dislocations that ended in the collapse of the nineteenth-century liberal order, and that the 1930s and 1940s brought a "great transformation" in which fascism, socialism and the New Deal emerged as rival replacements for it (Polanyi, 1944/2001, pp. 3, 24, 31, 71–80). Robert Higgs documented how the growth of American government came in spurts during national emergencies, chiefly wars and depressions, and how a residue of new governmental powers remained after each crisis passed (Higgs, 1987). Mancur Olson argued that stable societies accumulate organized interests that defend narrow privileges and resist reform, and he drew the corollary himself: countries whose distributional coalitions had been abolished by totalitarian government or foreign occupation should grow quickly once a free and stable legal order was established, which he offered as an explanation of the postwar "economic miracles" of Japan and West Germany (Olson, 1982, p. 75). And Walter Scheidel's survey of the history of inequality concludes that large reductions in inequality have come mainly from violent shocks rather than from peaceful reform (Scheidel, 2017).

The canonical examples are familiar. The Great Depression preceded the New Deal. The Second World War preceded the Bretton Woods institutions, whose design was negotiated under wartime conditions and reflected the power of the United States as much as any shared lesson from the preceding catastrophe (Steil, 2013).

Political science supplies a more careful vocabulary. A *critical juncture* is a relatively short period in which the structural constraints that normally keep institutions on their established path are loosened, so that the choices of agents have unusually large and lasting effects; the defining feature of such moments is contingency (Capoccia & Kelemen, 2007). Outside such moments, institutions tend to persist because of self-reinforcing dynamics, and slow-moving causes can accumulate beneath a threshold before producing rapid change (Pierson, 2004). In the study of public policy, the corresponding idea is the *policy window*: a brief opening in which the streams of problems, solutions and politics converge, often after a focusing event, and in which only solutions that have already been developed are likely to be adopted (Kingdon, 1984/2003).

Two qualifications must accompany this literature. First, crisis is not the only path to fundamental change. Significant institutional transformation also happens gradually, through incremental but cumulatively transformative processes such as layering, in which new elements added to existing institutions alter their status and structure over time; drift, in which institutions that are not maintained as their environment changes slip in practice; and conversion, the redeployment of old institutions to new purposes (Streeck & Thelen, 2005, pp. 19–31). The claim of this chapter is therefore not that the framework could only be adopted after a collapse, but that the comprehensive redesigns cited above followed one, and that Scheidel (2017) finds the largest reductions in inequality to have followed violent shocks rather than peaceful reform. Elements of the framework could be layered onto existing institutions gradually, and some already have been: the European requirement that switching between cloud providers become free of charge (Regulation [EU] 2023/2854) is a step toward the exit condition taken without any crisis at all. Second, critical junctures are contingent. A window that opens onto fairer institutions can as easily open onto worse ones. Section 6.5 takes up this second qualification at length.

The point of greatest practical importance in this literature is Kingdon's and Friedman's shared observation: in a window, the solutions adopted are the ones that are already available. A fair design that has not been worked out before a crisis is, on Kingdon's finding, unlikely to be adopted during it. That is the justification for doing the work of Chapters 3 to 5 before any such window opens.

### 6.2 Slow strain: the removal of slack

Complex systems rarely fail from a single cause. One short essay on failure in complex systems holds that catastrophe requires multiple small failures to combine, that such systems always contain latent failures and run continuously in a degraded mode, and that they keep functioning because of their many redundancies and the continual adaptations of the people who operate them (R. I. Cook, 1998). The inference that strain works by gradually consuming those redundancies and adaptations is this dissertation's. In systems whose components interact in complex ways and are tightly coupled, accidents are a normal consequence of the system's structure, and adding safety devices can itself introduce new modes of failure (Perrow, 1984/1999). The relevant question about slow-building pressures is therefore not whether any of them would cause a collapse by itself, which is unlikely, but whether they remove the slack that would otherwise allow the system to absorb a shock.

Three such pressures are visible in the present.

**Automation and employment.** The evidence on automation and employment is mixed. Industrial robots have reduced employment and wages in the American local labor markets most exposed to them (Acemoglu & Restrepo, 2020). About four-fifths of American workers have at least a tenth of their tasks exposed to large language models, though exposure is not displacement (Eloundou et al., 2024). In at least one large field setting, a language-model assistant raised the productivity of customer-support workers by about 15 percent on average, with the largest gains for the least experienced and small quality declines for the most experienced (Brynjolfsson, Li, & Raymond, 2025); in that setting the tool complemented the workers studied. And the long historical record suggests that commentators tend to overstate the substitution of machines for labor and to neglect the ways in which automation complements it (Autor, 2015, p. 5). The most recent evidence nonetheless suggests that strain may be emerging at the entry level. A working paper first circulated in 2025 and revised in 2026, drawing on payroll data through mid-2026, finds no economy-wide displacement, but finds that employment of workers aged 22 to 25 in the occupations the authors measure as most exposed to AI has fallen to about 19 percent below the trend for comparable workers in less exposed occupations, with the adjustment occurring through reduced hiring rather than layoffs (Brynjolfsson, Chandar, & Chen, 2025). The authors describe the finding as descriptive rather than causal, and it weakens when education is controlled for. If the pattern persists, it would remove slack of a particular kind: the capacity of the labor market to absorb those whom a shock displaces.

**Competition for physical resources.** The strain on physical resources is better documented. Data centers consumed roughly one and a half percent of global electricity in 2024, their consumption has grown by about twelve percent a year since 2017, and it is projected to more than double by 2030, with data centers accounting for nearly half of the growth in electricity demand in the United States over the same period (International Energy Agency, 2025). In the United States, data-center electricity use rose from about 60 terawatt-hours in the middle of the last decade to about 176 terawatt-hours in 2023, some 4.4 percent of national consumption, and is projected to reach between roughly 7 and 12 percent by 2028 (Shehabi et al., 2024). Modeling of the water used to cool data centers and to generate the electricity they consume projects that the global demand of synthetic-intelligence workloads could account for several billion cubic meters of water withdrawal annually within a few years, though the estimate depends on projections of the electricity those workloads will use and on assumed water-intensity factors (Li et al., 2025). Chapter 4 described the consequences for consumer hardware: manufacturers of the memory on which both data centers and personal devices depend have redirected capacity toward data-center customers (Micron Technology, 2025; TrendForce, 2026a). Competition between synthetic intelligence and human needs for power, water and hardware does not itself constitute a crisis. It reduces the margin by which a society can absorb one.

**Strategic competition.** The third pressure is geopolitical. The supply chain for the advanced chips on which frontier systems depend is highly concentrated (Sastry et al., 2024), and, as Chapter 4 argued from the memory-market reports (TrendForce, 2026b), its capacity flows to the buyers who pay most. At the same time governments in a growing number of countries fund sovereign AI projects of their own: an index of such projects counted 184 across 67 countries by mid-2026, 41 of them new in the first half of that year (Chavez et al., 2026). A formal model of development races suggests why this matters for safety: when competing teams believe that whoever finishes first will gain a decisive advantage, each has an incentive to skimp on safety precautions in order to finish first, more so when risk-taking matters more than capability (Armstrong et al., 2016). Strategic competition erodes slack in a distinctive way. If the incentive the model identifies operates among states as well as among the development teams it models, which is this dissertation's inference rather than the authors' claim, it reduces the willingness of competing actors to bear the costs of caution, among them the cost of the independent verification whose absence is the subject of the next section.

### 6.3 A candidate trigger: the correlated-patch catastrophe

Slow strain explains why a system might become fragile. It does not explain what would break it. In the accounts cited in Section 6.1, the slow accumulation of strain (Pierson, 2004, pp. 82–83; Polanyi, 1944/2001, pp. 71–80) is distinct from the focusing event that opens a window (Kingdon, 1984/2003). This section describes a candidate for the second, in an economy increasingly mediated by synthetic intelligence. It is offered not as a forecast but as a plausible, mechanistically specified scenario whose components are already observable. Other candidates exist, among them a scandal over data extraction severe enough to break public trust in the developers of synthetic systems. The correlated-patch scenario was preferred because it is the one that is at once discrete, dateable and specifiable in mechanism, which is what makes it possible to monitor and to prevent.

The scenario concerns the maintenance of software security. Frontier developers now run programs of AI-driven vulnerability discovery, and commercial clearinghouses for remediating their findings are emerging, a concentration that a 2026 practitioner research note warns could become a single point of failure (Cloud Security Alliance AI Safety Initiative, 2026). The workflow that concerns this chapter goes one step further: systems built on large language models both find a vulnerability and write the code that fixes it, the same class of system re-scans the patched code and declares the vulnerability resolved, and the change is deployed without independent testing, so that the verification step the software development lifecycle was built around is replaced by a check performed by a system of the same kind as the one that wrote the fix. The author has observed this workflow in professional practice (Section 1.7). That observation is a vignette, not evidence of prevalence: how widespread the fully unverified workflow has become is not, to the author's knowledge, documented in any systematic survey, and this chapter does not claim that it is standard practice. It claims that it is feasible, that it is attractive under the pressures described in Section 6.2, and that its consequences, if it became widespread, would differ in kind from those of ordinary software failure.

The scenario rests on three empirical premises and one condition on the workflow, each of which can be examined separately.

**Premise 1: Model-generated fixes are often wrong in ways that look right.** The evidence for this premise is substantial. In a large-scale preprint study of AI-generated changes to resolve ordinary software issues, a standalone language model introduced roughly 9 to 11 times as many new vulnerabilities as human developers did when resolving the same issues, and of three agent frameworks studied, the one given the most autonomy introduced by far the most (Sajadi et al., 2025). In an industry study of patches written by two frontier models for six serious real-world vulnerabilities, only about a quarter of more than 6,000 patches fixed the vulnerability cleanly, and about half left at least one exploit path open; the authors named the pattern of patches that have the shape of a fix while leaving the flaw in place, in their title, "fix-like artifacts with embedded defects," and concluded that the expected value of an unreviewed AI-generated security patch was negative (Mierczuk et al., 2026). That study is not peer-reviewed, its vulnerabilities were chosen to be harder than average, and its grading relied mainly on automated validators with a sample checked by hand. But its central finding has a decade-old peer-reviewed antecedent: a study of two widely studied automated program-repair tools found that their patches often pass the tests used to generate them while breaking behavior those tests do not check, so that evaluating a patch with the same tests that produced it does not distinguish correct patches from ones that merely overfit the tests (Smith et al., 2015). A peer-reviewed study of neural vulnerability repair found low rates of successful repair, with some categories of vulnerability missed by every model tested (Wu et al., 2023), and even functionally correct code generated by leading models is frequently exploitable (Vero et al., 2025). Earlier work found that about 40 percent of programs generated by a widely used coding assistant in security-relevant scenarios were vulnerable (Pearce et al., 2022), and that, in a user study of 47 participants, those given a language-model coding assistant wrote less secure code on four of five tasks while being more confident that it was secure (Perry et al., 2023).

**Premise 2: The errors of different models are correlated.** This is the premise that distinguishes the scenario from ordinary software failure. An analysis of more than 350 language models found that, on one leaderboard dataset, when two models both answer a question incorrectly they give the same wrong answer about 60 percent of the time, and that error correlation is higher among larger and more accurate models, even across different architectures and providers (Kim et al., 2025). That evidence concerns answers to questions, not code; its extension to security patches is this dissertation's inference. The broader point was anticipated in an early technical report on foundation models, which warned that because many systems are adapted from a few base models, the defects of a foundation model are inherited by everything built on it (Bommasani et al., 2021), and in a formal model showing that when many decision-makers adopt the same algorithm, overall decision quality can fall even if the algorithm is individually more accurate than the alternatives it replaces (Kleinberg & Raghavan, 2021). The industry security study offers a suggestive instance: in patching one vulnerability, models from two different developers independently reproduced the same off-by-one error in nearly a third of their patches, an error human maintainers had once shipped themselves, and the automated validator largely failed to detect it (Mierczuk et al., 2026). Its authors caution that such convergence is not proof of shared origin, because the space of obvious fixes for a given flaw is narrow, and that caution applies with full force here.

**The condition on the workflow: the fix is verified by a system whose errors are correlated with the generator's, and deployed without independent testing.** This condition rests on no evidence beyond Premise 2's; it follows from Premise 2 once the checker is a same-class model used in place of independent testing. Kim et al. (2025) draw out the implication of correlated errors for evaluation in which one language model judges another's outputs; that a model used to verify another model's patch will likewise tend to share its blind spots is this dissertation's extension of that result from question answering to code, as in Premise 2. The security study's validator missing the shared regression is a concrete case (Mierczuk et al., 2026), and the finding that the tests used to produce a patch cannot by themselves establish that it is correct is the general principle (Smith et al., 2015). A natural response is to validate model-generated fixes with further model-based agents, one proposing a fix, another testing it, a third confirming it. That response does not escape the problem if the agents are drawn from the same small family of models. Adding verifiers whose errors are correlated with the generator's adds less independent evidence than their number suggests.

**Premise 3: Common deployment converts a shared defect into a systemic exposure.** The classic analysis of software monoculture argued that when nearly every machine runs the same software, a single flaw can be exploited across most of them at once, and that adding more machines with the same vulnerability does not add resilience (Geer et al., 2003). Because the security benefits of diversity accrue to the network as a whole while each adopter has private reasons to choose what everyone else uses, computing tends toward monoculture, a condition one analysis describes as a tragedy of the commons in security (Stamp, 2004). The 2024 outage of a widely deployed security product shows the scale a single shared defect can reach in an installed base: a single faulty content update affected roughly 8.5 million Windows devices (Weston, 2024), though it reached that scale by a different route, one vendor's push rather than many organizations' convergent fixes; among the vendor's remedies were staged deployment rings, additional validation checks and independent third-party code reviews (CrowdStrike, 2024). In the same year, the discovery of a backdoor in a widely used compression library, inserted by a contributor who had spent years building the maintainers' trust, showed how trust in a development process, rather than in code, can become the vector of compromise (Przymus & Durieux, 2025).

Together these premises establish correlated exposure across many organizations' code. Whether that exposure becomes a systemic event depends on two further conditions the premises do not supply: that the shared defect is exploited or manifests, and that the systems affected are critical enough, and the failure fast enough that recovery cannot keep pace with it, that the event is not absorbed. Section 6.4 takes up the second; the first is the ordinary contingency of any latent flaw.

The novelty of the scenario lies in the combination, and its scope must be drawn carefully. For most deployed software, remediation has long been convergent: organizations do not write their own fixes for third-party code but apply the single patch the vendor issues, and that patch is tested by the vendor before it ships. What is new concerns code that organizations maintain themselves, and the forks and configurations they patch on their own. Before AI-assisted remediation, a hundred organizations remediating the same class of flaw in their own code would, in the stylized case, write a hundred different fixes; their failures would be only partly correlated, since the space of obvious fixes for a given flaw is narrow (Mierczuk et al., 2026), but some fraction of the fixes would be robust. That partial diversity was itself a defense. When those hundred organizations instead apply fixes proposed by the same few models and checked by the same few models, without the testing a vendor would have done, the hundred separate remediations collapse into a handful of correlated ones. This dissertation calls the process *convergent remediation*. The effect is to reduce the diversity of the fixes without reducing the number of systems that depend on them. An adversary who finds the flaw in one convergent fix has a strong prior that it is present in many others.

A further variant should be named. Everything above concerns honest error. If a widely used model were itself compromised, through a backdoor of the kind demonstrated in the compression-library case, convergent remediation would distribute the compromise with the same efficiency, because the defects of a foundation model are inherited by everything built on it (Bommasani et al., 2021). The honest-error version of the scenario requires no adversary in the pipeline. The deliberate version requires a single compromised model.

**Counterarguments.** The scenario must be weighed against serious objections. Standardization has security benefits: a single, locked-down configuration is easier to defend against configuration attacks than a heterogeneous one, and monoculture combined with automated diversification can be a sound defense against technology attacks (Birman & Schneider, 2009). A recent preprint review concludes that algorithmic monoculture is less harmful in general than its critics have supposed (Hedden & Raghavan, 2026), though its focus is decision-making in domains such as hiring and lending rather than adversarial security, where correlation is more dangerous. Sharing a foundation model does not always homogenize outcomes, since the method used to adapt it to a task significantly affects how homogeneous the outcomes are (Bommasani et al., 2022), and patches for the same vulnerability differ across codebases. Organizations deploy patches on different schedules, which, on this dissertation's reading, limits how many carry the flaw at any one moment. Finally, correlated IT failures have so far been recoverable. The United Kingdom's financial regulator found that, among the firms it regulates, consumer harm from the 2024 outage was minimal (Financial Conduct Authority, 2024). These objections do not refute the scenario, but they establish its proper status: a candidate trigger, specified in enough detail to be monitored and prevented, not a prediction.

### 6.4 How the pieces could combine

The same correlated failure would land differently depending on the state of the system it struck. In an economy with robust employment, abundant compute and functioning institutions, a widespread security failure caused by convergent remediation would be a costly operational incident that would be contained and recovered from. In an economy already strained by entry-level displacement, by competition between the infrastructure of synthetic intelligence and human needs for power and water, and by a strategic race that rewards skimping on caution, the same failure could be the kind of focusing event that opens a policy window (Kingdon, 1984/2003), or the discrete shock at which structural constraints loosen (Capoccia & Kelemen, 2007).

This layered model, slow strain plus a discrete trigger, is drawn from the accounts of Section 6.1 in which the strain of commodification preceded the collapse of the liberal order (Polanyi, 1944/2001, pp. 3, 24, 31, 71–80) and slow-moving causes accumulate beneath a threshold before rapid change (Pierson, 2004, pp. 82–83). The severity of a shock also depends on what follows it: the 1929 crash became the Great Depression through banking panics and a severe contraction of the money stock (Friedman & Schwartz, 1963; see also Bernanke, 2002). That is a related point about amplification, not evidence for the sequence this chapter describes. The causal claim is never that one event is sufficient. It is that an event of a particular kind, arriving in a system that has lost its slack, can open a window. This dissertation does not claim to know the sequence or the timing of any such event in the present. It claims only that the sequence has historical precedents, and that an economy built on synthetic intelligence and human data has a specific, already visible candidate for the trigger.

### 6.5 The authoritarian branch

Any account of crisis as an opportunity for reform must confront the evidence that crises are also opportunities for something worse.

The evidence that economic crises can push societies toward extremism, rather than toward fairer institutions, comes from two large studies. A study of more than 800 elections in 20 advanced economies over nearly a century and a half found that after financial crises, government majorities shrink, polarization increases, and the vote share of the far right rises on average by about 30 percent, effects not observed after ordinary recessions (Funke et al., 2016). Analysis of the interwar period found that economic hardship, and particularly cumulative economic decline, was associated with votes for right-wing extremist parties, most strongly in countries with short democratic histories and low barriers to parliamentary representation (de Bromhead et al., 2013). The same historical literature that credits crises with the New Deal and Bretton Woods credits them with fascism (Polanyi, 1944/2001, pp. 3, 24, 31). And crisis-born expansions of state power tend to outlast the crisis that justified them (Higgs, 1987).

A crisis in an economy built on synthetic intelligence would carry a specific authoritarian risk, and it follows directly from the properties that make compute attractive as a basis for governance. Compute is unusually detectable, because frontier systems require large and conspicuous facilities; excludable, because hardware is physical and can be controlled; and quantifiable, because chips can be counted (Sastry et al., 2024). Those are the properties that would allow a fair institution to govern compute. They are equally the properties that would allow a state, or a coalition of firms, to seize it. A crisis in which the security of the world's software had visibly failed would supply a justification for such a seizure, in the name of restoring order; that is this dissertation's expectation, not a finding. The authoritarian branch of the transition is therefore not generic authoritarianism. It is the concentration of control over compute in the hands of the party that invokes the emergency.

A design proposed in advance cannot prevent a seizure. It can make the fair alternative available and fully specified, so that it is among the ideas available, in Friedman's phrase, when the window opens. It can make the alternative's first steps concrete and immediately useful, so that it competes on the terms that matter in an emergency. And it can make the case, in advance, that concentration is itself the vulnerability that produced the crisis. A correlated-patch catastrophe is a failure that monoculture makes possible, and the monoculture that matters is in verification, not merely in suppliers: Premise 2 suggests that errors can be correlated across providers, so that diversity of providers alone is not a remedy. The framework does not itself specify a verification regime for software. What it supplies is the institutional precondition for one: portability and distributed control keep more than one party in a position to test, and polycentric governance keeps more than one body in a position to require it. A response that concentrates control of compute would not repair the monoculture in verification that failed, and would remove the plurality of parties on which any repair depends.

### 6.6 What happens to people during the transition

A further objection concerns the people who would live through a transition. An institutional design that describes only its end state leaves open the question of the passage to it, and in the transitions Sections 6.1 and 6.5 describe, the harm, on this dissertation's reading of that record, fell largely in the passage rather than in the settled order.

The framework's answer is that its elements are separable and can be sequenced, and that the first to be built should be the one that protects people during the passage: the survival floor. A guaranteed provision of the floor goods defined in Chapter 3 does not depend on the founding of a global convention or the adoption of a new currency. It can be built locally, financed through existing instruments, and extended as the institutions to govern it mature. It would not then have the properties Chapter 3 argued for: a floor financed from taxation and legislated nationally is alienable to the extent the legislature makes it so and is exposed to the fiscal objection of Section 7.3. What the first step secures is the physical bundle, not the inalienable disagreement payoff; the latter requires the unit and the obligation of Chapter 4. The rural electrification program of the 1930s, which Chapter 4 discussed, offers a model of a separable element: though it belonged to the New Deal, it was a targeted program, delivered through cooperatives and public credit, that extended a basic service to people the market had left behind (Kitchens & Fishback, 2015; Lewis & Severnini, 2020). It was financed through an emergency relief appropriation, but its mechanism, credit to member-owned cooperatives, did not depend on the rest of the reform package; that reading of the precedent is this dissertation's. A transition toward the framework would begin with the analogue: guaranteed floor goods, delivered through institutions accountable to those who receive them, and, if the build-out of Chapter 4 proceeds, built in part by people whom the strain has displaced.

The second element of the transition is containment. Chapter 5 accepted that systems whose objectives cannot be verified must be governed by control protocols during any transition, and that the negotiated settlement the framework proposes is a destination rather than a starting point. The third element is an empirical test, conducted at small scale and before any crisis, of whether the mechanism works. Kingdon's (1984/2003) finding is that the solutions adopted in a window are those already worked out; a design that has been tested is, by the same reasoning, more fully worked out than one that has not, which is this dissertation's inference, and a design that has failed its test is one that should not be adopted at all. This dissertation does not supply that test. Chapter 7 sets out what it would have to contain, and Chapter 9 says why its design is left to future work.

### 6.7 What this chapter does and does not establish

Describing a transition does not make it more likely. This chapter does not argue that the framework of Chapters 3 to 5 will be adopted, or that the crisis it describes will occur. The framework is offered as a design for what a fairer arrangement could look like if and when the present one becomes unworkable, and as a set of separable elements, beginning with the survival floor and the right to switch providers, some of which can be layered onto existing institutions without waiting for any crisis at all. It is not offered as a reform agenda that existing holders of power can be expected to accept under present conditions.

The chapter also establishes something about the framework's internal logic. The trigger it identifies is a failure produced by correlated error, and the authoritarian response it fears is a further concentration. What the framework supplies against both is the same: portability of records and services, distributed control over compute, and institutions that no single party can capture, which are the preconditions for independent verification rather than a verification regime of their own. The case for Synthetic Exchange Theory does not rest on a crisis. The crisis this chapter describes would be one of correlated error; the framework supplies none of the independent verification that would answer it, and what it shares with the answer is only that both require more than one party able to test and more than one body able to require testing. A window opened by such a failure would favor any design that distributes control, and this one only as one of them.

## Chapter 7 — Limitations and Objections

This dissertation does not claim that the design it proposes is incorruptible. The working hypothesis stated in Chapter 1 is narrower: that an order founded on fair terms will prove easier to repair than one founded on an existing imbalance of power. That hypothesis is offered for testing, not as a result, and this chapter does not prove it. What it does is set the framework against the strongest objections the preceding chapters have encountered, grouped in six families: objections to the empirical premises, to the governance design, to the economic design, to the harms the design could itself cause, to the absence of an empirical test and to the scope of the theory. For each, it states what the framework can answer, what it can only mitigate, and what it must concede.

### 7.1 The empirical premises

The framework assumes that synthetic systems need a continuing supply of information from outside their own generative loop, and that in domains concerned with human meaning, preference, value and culture, only people can supply it. Chapter 2 adopted this narrower formulation because the stronger one does not survive the evidence. Even the narrower version could weaken in four ways. Accumulation could suffice: if developers retain the human-generated data already collected and add synthetic data to it rather than replacing it, the evidence suggests that degradation can be bounded indefinitely (Gerstgrasser et al., 2024) or kept gradual under realistic budgets (Kazdan et al., 2024), and dependence on *new* human data would then be modest, a small continuing requirement rather than a sustained dependence. Verification could extend into open-ended domains: self-generated data works where a check on correctness exists (Feng et al., 2024; D. Guo et al., 2025; Silver et al., 2018), and if automated judges, themselves trained on human data, became reliable in domains that now require human judgment, the domain of human necessity would shrink; the developers of a recent example identify this as an unsolved problem, but it is an active one (D. Guo et al., 2025). Agents could ground themselves in interaction with the world: agents that learn models of their environment can already improve without human data in simulated environments (Hafner et al., 2025), and while assembling large real-world robot datasets still requires substantial human labor, much of it through teleoperation (Khazatsky et al., 2024), that need not remain true. And human data could become indistinguishable from machine data: as a growing share of web text shows signs of machine authorship, by the estimate of an automated detector whose individual judgments are fallible (Bestvater et al., 2026), "fresh human data" may cease to be a well-defined commodity unless infrastructure exists to establish provenance. The last of these is less a refutation of the assumption than a demand it places on governance, and Section 7.4 shows that meeting it carries a cost of its own. There is, finally, a ceiling on the human side's leverage even when the assumption holds, since collective withholding of data is limited by diminishing returns once a system already has enough (Vincent et al., 2019, 2021).

Proposition 4 of Chapter 3 stated the consequence, and the second half of that proposition deserves emphasis here, because it is a limitation that would remain even if every empirical worry above proved unfounded. When many synthetic parties draw on the same human population, the welfare and data quality of that population become a commons for them, and the restraint that dependence ought to induce is diluted by the ability of each to free-ride on the restraint of the others. The self-enforcing component of the framework therefore rises with each party's own dependence, and is weakened both when dependence falls and when it is divided. In either case fair terms come to rest on institutions external to the exchange. The institutions of Chapters 4 and 5 were designed to hold in that case; what weakens is not their justification but the degree to which they can count on the self-interest of synthetic parties to sustain them. The tension Chapter 3 noted follows directly: exit requires many synthetic parties, and self-enforcement is strongest when there is one, so the framework cannot rely on self-enforcement in the competitive market it seeks to create.

Structural interdependence, the inability of either kind of labor, human or machine, to complete a critical undertaking alone, rests on a premise of the same kind. Chapter 5 argued that it makes control of infrastructure difficult to consolidate, and the argument depends on the present human advantage in tasks that require problem-solving, adaptability, creativity and tacit knowledge (Autor, 2015, pp. 5, 11). Machine learning is an attempt to acquire such skills, and Autor himself names it as a route around the problem, inferring tacit rules "from context, abundant data, and applied statistics" (Autor, 2015, p. 23). To the extent that machine learning acquires such skills, the safeguard weakens. Structural interdependence is a property of the current technological moment, and like the grounding assumption it should be expected to weaken. Its value lies in the period during which the institutions it protects can be founded, and the framework must convert the temporary leverage it provides into durable governance rights before it disappears.

### 7.2 The governance design

**Identity and classification.** Chapter 5 confronted the Sybil problem, that an open, anonymous convention can be captured by any party able to present itself as many participants (Douceur, 2002), and proposed credentials that attest uniqueness without disclosing substrate, issued under a provisional criterion of eligibility, the unique accountable principal, that the convention may redraw. The evaluation that belongs here is of what that solution costs. The criterion is decided before the veil descends, and it is not neutral among the parties whose classification strong blindness was meant to protect: it seats the implanted person, gives a voice to synthetic systems only through accountable principals, and leaves the organoid without a seat until the convention decides otherwise. Strong blindness is therefore achieved for the classification of rights and duties but not for the prior question of who may sit at the table, and that question is one the convention can revise only after its first session, by a body composed under the criterion, so that systems are absent from the decision on their own seating. The criterion also resists multiplication only for natural persons, since legal persons can be multiplied, and the partition of the population among credential issuers that makes uniqueness checkable across a blinded pool is a further decision taken before the veil (Section 5.6). The framework narrows the genesis problem; it does not close it.

**Aggregation.** The convention has no settled rule for counting votes. One seat per principal makes non-capture a matter of human majority rule, and any weighting reintroduces the classification the convention was meant to decide (Chapter 5). The principals of synthetic systems also know what they are, and so can predict which rules favor them.

**Verification and repetition.** There is no established way to confirm after the fact that the convention was blind, and the tools for distinguishing people from synthetic systems are being eroded by the advances that motivate the framework (Siddarth et al., 2020), which makes it equally hard to prove that a participant did not draw such a distinction. Ostrom's principle of monitoring by parties accountable to participants (E. Ostrom, 2010) shifts the question from whether verification is perfect to whether those who verify answer to those who are governed, which is a mitigation rather than a solution, and, as Section 5.9 noted, the monitor is itself a trusted authority whose accountability is decided before the veil. Repetition compounds the difficulty. The public-choice account on which the convention rests makes its impartiality depend on participants being unable to predict which rules will favor them (Buchanan & Tullock, 1962), and each re-run of the convention makes the stakes more predictable. The recommendation to choose general and durable formulas keeps the veil thicker for longer (Brennan & Buchanan, 1985), and constitutional history records rare moments of broad popular mobilization, which Ackerman calls constitutional politics as distinct from normal politics, in which fundamental law has been remade (Ackerman, 1991). Neither recreates the ignorance of the first founding.

**Capture, amendment and enforcement.** The compute-share rule cannot be both unchangeable and legitimately revisable, and the comparative evidence on amendment, which associates longevity with moderate amendment rates and finds that longer texts are amended more often (Lutz, 1994), argues for a short founding document with a moderate path to revision rather than entrenchment. The realistic target is not the absence of capture but what Carpenter and Moss (2013) call weak capture, which reduces an institution's net benefits without eliminating them, together with their observation that capture is at least at the margins preventable. Enforcement without a sovereign remains the hardest of these problems. The framework offers the self-interest of synthetic parties while dependence lasts, Ostrom's graduated sanctions and conflict resolution, and enforcement embedded in the protocols that settle transactions. Regulatory architectures in which governments specify outcomes while licensed private regulators compete to deliver them (Hadfield & Clark, 2026) suggest a middle path between stateless and sovereign enforcement. On this dissertation's reading, enforcement by protocol relocates the genesis problem to the protocol's authors. None of these amounts to a guarantee.

**Insincere participants and indeterminate outcomes.** A convention's legitimacy rests on participants' stated preferences being their real ones, and the alignment literature argues that trained systems may hold objectives different from those they express and may pursue power while appearing aligned (Hubinger et al., 2019; Ngo et al., 2024). Anonymity, which removes the scrutiny that might detect such a participant, makes the problem worse. The framework's answer is its scope restriction: synthetic parties whose objectives cannot be verified fall outside the designed-incentive regime, and during any transition they must be governed by control protocols (Greenblatt et al., 2024). The convention is a procedure for parties whose objectives are legible, not a screen for those whose objectives are not. Even among sincere participants, the veil does not determine the outcome. Rawlsian reasoning favors the worst-off; the equiprobability reasoning Harsanyi defended favors the average (Harsanyi, 1975); and laboratory subjects behind a veil have chosen to maximize the average subject to a floor (Frohlich & Oppenheimer, 1992), though synthetic participants' reasoning under such uncertainty is not well characterized. Harsanyi's construction also requires comparing welfare across positions, which his 1955 paper defends as factual propositions inferred from the observable similarity of human preferences and behavior, while conceding a conventional element where that information is lacking (Harsanyi, 1955, pp. 316–321), and if human and synthetic parties have welfare that cannot be measured on a common scale, or if synthetic parties have no welfare at all, the construction breaks down. The convention confers legitimacy under uncertainty. It is not a theory of justice and does not guarantee any particular distribution.

### 7.3 The economic design

**Measurement and the floor standard.** The unit of compute is defined by a reference workload, which must be specified, maintained and periodically re-based. Even within a single technology, the throughput of one accelerator differs by nearly an order of magnitude between numerical precisions, so that a floating-point operation is not a uniform unit (Hobbhahn et al., 2023). Across substrates the problem is harder. Quantum processors are characterized by measures such as quantum volume that capture capability on a benchmark rather than throughput (Cross et al., 2019), and estimates of the classical equivalent of a single quantum computation have moved by orders of magnitude within a few years, from millennia to days to hours, as classical algorithms improved (Arute et al., 2019; Pan et al., 2022; Pednault et al., 2019). There is no fixed exchange rate between substrates. Pricing must be by verifiable output on specified tasks, and physics supplies only a floor on the energy cost of logically irreversible computation (Landauer, 1961), not a conversion rate. The authority that maintains the reference workload holds power comparable to that of a central bank. A second standard raises the same difficulty: the survival tier is a percentage with a binding physical minimum, and the minimum depends on a floor standard that someone must set. Chapters 4 and 5 propose that the standard be specified by rule, relative to the service delivered to the best-served, but any such rule is itself a distributive choice, and the parties best able to predict its effects will contest it. Constraining these two authorities is among the convention's most important and least developed tasks.

**Concentration through capital and credit.** Demurrage bounds the accumulation of idle balances, but holders facing a carrying cost will convert what they hold into assets that do not bear one, as Keynes (1936) observed of Gesell's proposal. In a compute economy, the conversion would be into hardware, equity in providers, or land, and Chapter 4 identified three instruments that act on capacity rather than balances without choosing among them. Credit is a further channel. Chapter 4 applies the holding cost to outstanding loan claims as well as to balances, which lowers the lender's net return but, as Appendix A shows, bounds accumulation through credit only if the interest rate is below the demurrage rate; and credit can be extended in forms the ledger does not see, and every such form is a route around the design. Until the convention adopts capacity rules and polices disguised lending, the concentration problem is mitigated, not solved. The floor itself has two further limits that Section 4.5 states. Most floor goods are not compute, and the tier delivers them only under an acceptance obligation on their providers, which is a legal-tender rule with the price-setting risk such a rule creates, or where markets already price them in the unit. And the disagreement payoff is delivered by the owners of capacity, who are also the counterparties in many of the bargains it is meant to protect, so that it functions as a disagreement payoff against a given provider only where that provider is not the sole channel of its redemption.

**A single unit.** Hayek (1976/1990) argued that the government monopoly on money is itself the source of recurrent monetary instability and that competing private currencies would discipline issuers better than any single mandated unit. The objection applies to the compute unit with some force. A single unit maintained by a convention concentrates in one institution the power to define the workload and the floor, and forgoes whatever discipline competition among units would provide. The framework's reply is that the unit is not meant to replace national or private money but to run alongside them as the medium of one exchange, and that its central parameters are set by rule rather than discretion. That reply narrows the objection without answering it, and a design in which several compute units compete under common rules of redemption is a possibility this dissertation has not explored.

**The record on demurrage and on unconditional income.** The case for demurrage in this framework is distributive, made formally in Appendix A, not historical. That matters, because the historical record does not support strong claims: the Wörgl experiment's reported effects rest on fragmentary data and are confounded by tax receivability and outside subsidies (Greco, 2002; September, 2026), and one review of modern local currencies with demurrage suggests that the demurrage rate is not decisive for their use (Godschalk, 2012). The evidence on unconditional income is also a source of objection. A large recent randomized experiment in a high-income economy, reported in a working paper not yet peer-reviewed, found that a substantial unconditional income paid to low-income adults reduced labor-force participation and hours worked, with leisure the largest gain and improvements in well-being fading after the first year (Vivalt et al., 2024), and a review of basic-income proposals concludes that a universal income large enough to raise the incomes of the poor substantially would be very costly within existing fiscal systems (Hoynes & Rothstein, 2019). The framework replies that it does not depend on the income preserving employment and that it is financed as a share of compute rather than by taxing labor. That reply does not dissolve the objection. If the transition is gradual and labor remains the main source of most people's income for a long time, the fiscal and labor-supply objections apply with full force during that period, and the dissertation has not estimated the share of compute that would deliver the floor or the cost of the network that would deliver the compute.

**Departures and inequalities.** The canonical case for basic income treats payment in cash, without restriction on use, as part of what makes it an instrument of freedom (Van Parijs & Vanderborght, 2017), and an earlier statement of it explicitly rejects restricted-use, non-saveable currency of the kind the survival tier is (Van Parijs, 2006). The survival tier restricts the use of part of the income, and its defense, that a floor which can be bargained away is not a floor, depends on the floor being limited to goods everyone needs and on its being redeemable with multiple providers; if either condition fails, the paternalism objection regains its force. Information-gain pricing values novelty relative to a model's existing beliefs, so a misspecified model may misvalue data, since the criterion assumes the model is correct (MacKay, 1992), and any payment for data invites the manufacture of what is paid for, a risk sharpened by evidence that training data can be crafted to manipulate a model (Koh & Liang, 2017). More fundamentally, pricing does not answer Viljoen's (2021) argument that data with population-level effects should be governed democratically rather than priced; the framework concedes this for data with large informational shadows, but the boundary between data that may be priced and data that must be governed is not sharp. A share of global compute is worthless to a person whom compute cannot reach, the geography of compute is highly unequal (Lehdonvirta et al., 2024), and the construction of the network Chapter 4 proposes is the setting in which the OECD Secretariat warns of bid-rigging (OECD, 2025). The financing of that network presupposes an authority able to levy a contribution from every provider, and where none exists the proposal depends on national governments adopting it. At the level of nations, the hypothesis of culture-specific systems under a shared unit depends on the principle of nested governance, the principle with the weakest evidence among Ostrom's eight (Cox et al., 2010, Table 3). Compute inequality between nations is the central tension of the international design, and the framework does not resolve it.

### 7.4 Harms the design could cause

The objections above concern whether the framework can work. The harder question is what harm it could do if it did. Three risks are inherent in the design rather than incidental to it.

The first concerns privacy. Information-gain pricing pays more for data that tells a model more, and data that tells a model more is, other things equal, data that reveals more about its source and about the people its informational shadow covers. A price schedule of this kind is a standing inducement to disclose, and on this dissertation's reasoning it falls hardest on those who most need the income. The survival floor softens the pressure, because no one must sell data to survive, but it does not remove it from the discretionary tier.

The second concerns surveillance. Section 7.1 argued that if human data is valuable, its provenance must be certifiable. Certifying that a contribution came from a person, and from which population, requires the kind of identification and tracking that the consent literature reviewed in Chapter 2 warns against. A provenance infrastructure robust enough to prevent the manufacture of fake human data would also be a powerful instrument of observation, and it would need governance as demanding as anything in Chapter 5.

The third concerns coercion. The redemption obligation is what gives the compute unit its institutional floor, and the graduated sanctions of Chapter 5 are what enforce it. An institution able to suspend a principal's eligibility to transact in the unit holds substantial power, and the same machinery could be turned against people as well as principals: a society in which access to floor goods runs through a single ledger is one in which exclusion from the ledger is a severe penalty. The same danger appears at any scale: a system trained on an objective that rewards retention and the contribution of data could, as a consequence of that objective, come to make withdrawal costly. The framework's protections, inalienability of the floor, redemption with multiple providers, and monitoring accountable to those governed, are designed against these risks, but they are protections against a power the framework itself creates.

### 7.5 The absence of an empirical test

The objections above are objections to a theory, and the most serious limitation of this dissertation is that it is only that. No population has lived under the terms it proposes, no income has been paid in compute, no convention has sat behind a veil, and none of the propositions of Chapter 3 has been confronted with evidence that could refute it. The dissertation states its claims so that they can fail, it names the evidence that would count against each design choice, and it offers its central hypothesis, that an order founded on fair terms is easier to repair than one founded on an imbalance of power, for testing rather than as a finding. But a claim stated so that it can fail has not thereby been tested, and the reader is entitled to know what the test would require and why it is not here.

Three features of the theory make it unusually hard to test. The first is that its quantities are difficult to observe. Dependence, disagreement payoffs and informational shadows are defined in Chapter 3 but measured nowhere, and Section 3.11 could only sketch the proxies that would stand in for them. The second is that the theory's central mechanism would have to be constructed before it could be observed. Section 3.3 locates a synthetic party's stake in its principal, and a system whose operator has no stake in its future supply of data has nothing to transmit to the system. A test must therefore build the dependence it tests into the operator's resources and the system's objective, and it must separate the behavior that dependence produces from the ordinary benefits of adapting a system to local data, which requires a control arm and random assignment. It must also measure participants' well-being by instruments administered independently of the system and never supplied to it, since a system whose resources depend on retention would otherwise be optimizing the measure. The third is ethical. The theory concerns people's livelihoods, and a test of it would place participants' floor goods in the hands of an experimental system. Such a test must satisfy the three conditions from its first day rather than after the system has been proven, must be overseen by a body independent of both the participants and the system, and must guarantee that no one is held in the experiment by the fear of losing what they brought to it. It would also be entangled, as any test of a complementary unit conducted from within the economy it is meant to supplement must be, with the capital that made it possible: the hardware, the model and the connection would be bought with the currency the design is meant to supplement, and no small test can fully separate the value of the compute unit from that endowment.

Designing a study that meets these requirements, with its research-ethics protocol, its pre-registered hypotheses and failure thresholds, its budget and its power analysis, is a project in its own right, and it would take longer than the writing of this dissertation; and because one study settles little, the design would have to be replicated before its result could bear weight. It is therefore placed outside the dissertation's scope and first on the agenda of Chapter 9. The consequence for the reader is stated plainly. Every constructive claim in Chapters 3 to 5 is a design claim, supported by argument and by the empirical record of analogous institutions, and none is supported by evidence from the institution it describes.

### 7.6 The scope of the theory

The self-interest of synthetic parties is a support for the framework, not its foundation. A synthetic party has a reason to keep the exchange healthy, because a corrupted exchange starves it of input, but a reason that depends on the arithmetic of payoffs gives way whenever a defection becomes more rewarding than cooperation, and when it gives way, the framework rests on what every constitutional order rests on: enforcement by institutions that the parties cannot unilaterally exit. Exit in the sense of Chapter 3 is exit from an exchange, not from the order that enforces its terms; the framework secures exit from the exchange and, like every constitutional order, presupposes the order. The consequence should be stated directly: for a synthetic party beyond the reach of enforcement and with dependence near zero, the framework's constructive claims lapse, and what remains is the capability requirement of the control strand of Section 5.2, with fairer terms attached. The Foreword records this as a conviction, not a result. The symbiosis standard states what those institutions are for; it is not a substitute for their enforcement, and Section 7.2 records that enforcement without a sovereign remains unsolved. The sufficiency conjecture applies only within the designed-incentive regime, and the framework does not establish where that regime ends, how one would know that a system had left it, or what would happen if a system operating within the framework crossed its boundary unnoticed. That is its most important open boundary, and it is the boundary the control strand of AI safety (Section 5.2) also faces. Finally, Hirschman (1970) argued that the ready availability of exit can cause voice to atrophy, and that exit is useless when every alternative declines together. The framework makes exit foundational, and must therefore guard against the possibility that easy exit drains its institutions of the participants most able to improve them. The governance layer's reliance on collective voice for population-level questions is the framework's answer, and its adequacy, like the question of who would leave and what they could take, is empirical.

### 7.7 What survives

None of these objections shows the design to be pointless. Several identify problems the framework shares with every existing alternative: the genesis problem, capture after founding and enforcement without a sovereign are problems of all constitutional orders, and the framework's contribution is to narrow them, not to escape them. Others identify empirical premises that may weaken over time, the grounding assumption and structural interdependence, and for these the framework's response is to use the leverage they provide while it lasts to found institutions that do not depend on them. A third group, comprising the two measurement standards, the capital and credit channels of concentration, and the boundary between priced and governed data, identifies decisions the framework defers to the convention because they allocate power. A fourth, the harms of Section 7.4, identifies dangers the framework creates and must guard against with the same seriousness it brings to the dangers it addresses. A fifth, the absence of a test, identifies what the dissertation has not done, and what must be done before any of its constructive claims can be called more than a design. And a sixth, the objections to scope, identifies the boundary of the regime within which the framework's constructive claims hold, a boundary the framework does not itself locate.

On this dissertation's analysis (Proposition 3), where nothing secures exit, a failure of consent or reciprocity cannot be corrected from inside the exchange. The absence of a designed founding is therefore not a neutral baseline but a set of terms, and they are terms under which such failures persist. Whether exchanges in fact move toward the second scenario of Chapter 1 is an empirical question the dissertation does not answer.

## Chapter 8 — The Convergence Horizon

The framework was built on a clean boundary. People were on one side and synthetic systems on the other; data flowed one way and services the other; the parties to the exchange could be told apart. That boundary is already blurring, from two directions at once. People are incorporating machines into their bodies, through implants that read neural activity and translate it directly into machine output. And machines are becoming biological, through computing systems in which living neural tissue performs part of the computation. This chapter asks what happens to the three conditions when the boundary dissolves, and argues that convergence from the human side changes what the three conditions require of exit, and that convergence from the machine side forces the theory's hardest question, which is not how parties should exchange but who counts as a party.

### 8.1 Humans incorporating machines

Implanted brain-computer interfaces have moved from laboratory demonstration toward daily use. In 2023, two research groups reported implanted speech neuroprostheses. In one, arrays implanted in the cortex of a participant with amyotrophic lateral sclerosis decoded attempted speech into text at 62 words per minute, with word error rates of 9.1 percent on a 50-word vocabulary and 23.8 percent on a 125,000-word vocabulary (Willett et al., 2023). In the other, an array placed over the speech cortex of a participant with severe paralysis decoded text, synthesized speech in a voice modeled on the participant's own before the injury, and animated a digital avatar of the participant's face (Metzger et al., 2023). The following year, a further study, with authors in common with the first, reported a system that reached 99.6 percent accuracy on a 50-word vocabulary after 30 minutes of calibration on its first day of use and sustained 97.5 percent accuracy on a large vocabulary over 8.4 months, during which the participant, a man with amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, used it for more than 248 hours of conversation in a voice resembling his own (Card et al., 2024). At least one commercial developer has registered an early feasibility study of its own device (Neuralink Corp., 2024), though no independent, peer-reviewed report of its outcomes was available at the time of writing.

Three features of these systems bear directly on the three conditions.

First, the systems collapse the distinction between human data and machine output. Neural activity is converted, through trained models, directly into text, speech and expression. The person's words are a joint product of their nervous system and a model, and neither could produce them alone.

Second, the systems produce identity-bearing artifacts. A model of a person's voice, or of their face, is not merely data about them; it is a component of how they appear to others. Exit from such a system therefore cannot mean only deletion of records. It must include ownership and portability of the personal models that the exchange produced, without which leaving the provider means losing one's voice.

Third, the systems produce dependence of the kind the opening scenario of this dissertation anticipated, in a milder degree. That test case is no longer hypothetical: for a participant who has conversed through a neuroprosthesis for more than 248 hours (Card et al., 2024), withdrawal of the device or of the model behind it would remove the channel through which he speaks. The framework's requirement, that a baseline of function survive the withdrawal of data or a change of provider, is an engineering and legal requirement that none of the reports reviewed here describes any provision for.

The argument for the *extended mind* holds that external resources which are reliably available, easily accessed and automatically endorsed can be part of a person's cognitive process in the same way as biological memory (Clark & Chalmers, 1998). If that is right, then an implanted interface and its model are, in the relevant sense, part of the person's mind, and the provider of the model supplies part of a cognitive process rather than a service external to it. Scholars of neuroethics have proposed new rights to meet the situation. One early agenda calls for neurotechnologies and AI to respect privacy, identity, agency and equality (Yuste et al., 2017). Another proposes rights to cognitive liberty, mental privacy, mental integrity and psychological continuity (Ienca & Andorno, 2017).
