The last two are, in this dissertation's terms, conditions on exit: what leaving an implanted system must preserve is the integrity and continuity of the person who leaves.

The law has begun to respond. Chile's constitution now directs the law to protect brain activity and the information derived from it (Ley N.º 21.383, 2021); Colorado has made neural data used for identification a category of sensitive data requiring consent, and California has added neural data to the sensitive personal information its consumer-privacy act protects (H.B. 24-1058, 2024; S.B. 1223, 2024); and UNESCO has adopted a global recommendation on the ethics of neurotechnology that, according to its announcement, treats neural data as highly sensitive and calls for explicit consent and transparency (UNESCO, 2025); the text of the Recommendation itself was not available for this review. All of these instruments frame neural data as a person's information, to be protected by consent and limits on use, and Colorado's tie to identification may leave de-identified or tissue-derived neural signals outside it altogether. None addresses reciprocity, the value that flows back to the person whose neural activity trains the model. The deletion and access rights that California's consumer-privacy law attaches to sensitive personal information extend, after S.B. 1223 (2024), to neural data as records, but none of these instruments addresses ownership or portability of the voice, face or decoding models the exchange produces, which is what exit from an implanted system requires.

### 8.2 Machines becoming biological

From the other direction, a research field now explicitly aims to build computers from living neural tissue. Its founding statement proposed *organoid intelligence*: the use of three-dimensional cultures of human brain cells, grown from stem cells and connected to electrodes and machine-learning systems that encode inputs and decode outputs, as a substrate for computation, with the claim, so far a projection, that such systems might eventually be far more energy-efficient than silicon (Smirnova et al., 2023). Participants at the field's first workshop called in a joint declaration for the scientific community to pursue the research while addressing its ethical implications, to remain alert to the possibility that organoids could develop aspects of consciousness, and to work toward an accepted ethical framework that takes due account of public values (Hartung et al., 2023). The United States National Science Foundation has issued an engineering research solicitation in the area, Biocomputing through EnGINeering Organoid Intelligence (U.S. National Science Foundation, 2023).

The empirical results to date are modest. Cultures of human and rodent neurons on high-density electrode arrays, given structured feedback, improved their performance at a simulated version of the game Pong within minutes, a result its authors described as learning and, in the paper's title, as sentience (Kagan et al., 2022). The authors' use of that word is the reason this dissertation reads their result with caution: the National Academies' assessment, discussed below, holds that neural activity alone does not demonstrate awareness, and on that standard the description outruns the evidence (National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine, 2021). A human brain organoid used as the reservoir of a reservoir-computing system adapted to training input, and the adaptation was accompanied by changes in the tissue's functional connectivity (Cai et al., 2023). A 2026 study found that cortical organoids derived from mouse stem cells, given training signals chosen by reinforcement learning, mostly performed better at a pole-balancing task than with random training signals (Robbins et al., 2026).

The National Academies' assessment of the science is cautious. A report of the United States National Academies judged it extremely unlikely that current neural organoids will acquire awareness or the capacity for pain in the foreseeable future, held that neural activity alone does not demonstrate either, and recommended revisiting the question as the technology develops (National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine, 2021). International guidelines for stem-cell research place most organoid research in the category exempt from specialized review (Lovell-Badge et al., 2021). Proposals exist for assessing whether organoids are minimally sentient using empirical measures developed for patients who cannot communicate (Lavazza & Massimini, 2018).

The point for this dissertation is not that organoid computers are conscious; the evidence says they almost certainly are not. It is that a computing substrate made of human tissue is now an engineering objective, and that in at least one demonstrated system the training input changes the functional connectivity of the tissue (Cai et al., 2023). In such a system, the distinction between data and processor, on which the second framing scenario of Chapter 1 turned, is no longer clean. The input does not merely pass through the substrate; it alters the substrate's measured organization.

### 8.3 Consent when the substrate is tissue

Bioethicists have begun to ask the questions that convergence forces. Quoted in a university news release on a federally funded organoid-computing project, the legal scholar Henry Greely posed, as open questions, whether organoid-based computing devices should be treated as human tissue samples, as laboratory animals, as persons, or as something else, and what informed consent should mean for the people whose cells are used to make them (Cerf, 2025). A review of the ethics of brain-organoid research identifies the procurement of human biomaterials and donor consent as a distinct theme, alongside research oversight, translational delivery, animal research, and consciousness and moral status (Hyun et al., 2020). The founders of organoid intelligence themselves ask whether donors' rights continue after donation (Smirnova et al., 2023). The National Academies treat donor consent as unresolved, noting that specific consent was generally not obtained from past donors and that recontacting them is sometimes impossible (National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine, 2021). A recent argument holds that uncertainty about whether brain organoids could become conscious, and about what that would matter morally, may leave donors insufficiently informed to give valid consent, that donor consent alone cannot establish that risks to the organoids themselves are reasonable, and that consent should therefore be specific to particular projects rather than broad (Kataoka et al., 2024).

In the terms of Synthetic Exchange Theory, convergence changes the structure of the exchange in two ways.

First, the exchange is no longer between two separate parties. When a person's cells become part of a computing system, the person is not only a supplier of data to the system but a contributor to its substance. The three conditions must be extended to this case. Consent must become *continuing and revocable participation* rather than a one-time donation, in line with the argument for project-specific consent. Reciprocity must include a share in the downstream value that the donated material helps produce. And exit, which for donated tissue cannot mean physical retrieval, is not available in Chapter 3's sense; what can be secured is clause C(iii), the right to withdraw consent to future uses and to have that withdrawal honored.

Second, the resource extracted may not be data about a person but tissue that might, in some future system, itself be a party. Here the framework meets the problem of moral status under uncertainty. One recent philosophical framework for that problem recommends treating beings with a realistic possibility of sentience as *sentience candidates* and adopting proportionate precautions, chosen through inclusive deliberation, without waiting for a settled science of consciousness (Birch, 2024). Researchers have applied similar reasoning to artificial systems, arguing that there is a realistic possibility that some will be conscious or robustly agentic in the near future and that this calls for assessment and preparation, while explicitly declining to argue that near-future systems will in fact be moral patients (Long et al., 2024, p. 25). A systematic attempt to derive indicators of consciousness from leading scientific theories found that no system they examined is a strong candidate, but that no obvious technical barrier prevents building systems that satisfy the indicators (Butlin et al., 2023).

One implication of that last finding deserves emphasis for the economic layer. The indicator properties derived by Butlin et al. (2023), on the computational-functionalist theories they survey, are properties of a system's organization rather than of the amount of computation it performs. Whether a system satisfies those indicators cannot be read off the quantity of compute it performs, and on any view that ties moral status to consciousness neither can its moral status. The unit of compute defined in Chapter 4 measures work done, and it cannot double as a measure of who matters. The framework must keep these questions separate: the currency prices computation, and the convention decides who counts.

### 8.4 A substrate-agnostic currency, revisited

Chapter 4 defined the unit of compute by a reference workload rather than by the speed of any particular hardware, so that the definition would hold regardless of which substrate becomes dominant. Convergence tests that definition at its limits.

The same abstract operation can already be performed on physically different substrates. Photonic hardware performs the multiply-and-accumulate operations at the core of neural networks with light rather than electrons (Shastri et al., 2021). Neuromorphic hardware built from novel memristive, spintronic and photonic materials implements artificial neurons and synapses directly in physical devices (Marković et al., 2020); such hardware, on this dissertation's reading, departs from the instruction-by-instruction model on which conventional measures of computation rest. Quantum processors are characterized not by operations per second but by qubit count, gate fidelity, connectivity and measurement error, and qubit count alone is not a meaningful figure of merit (Preskill, 2018). The nearest thing to a bridge between quantum and classical measures, quantum volume, captures capability on a benchmark of random circuits rather than throughput (Cross et al., 2019). And the classical equivalent of a given quantum computation has proved unstable: the first claimed demonstration of quantum computational advantage estimated that a classical supercomputer would need 10,000 years to reproduce a task its processor completed in about 200 seconds (Arute et al., 2019), an estimate revised to days by a competing analysis (Pednault et al., 2019) and later to hours by improved classical algorithms (Pan et al., 2022). Biological substrates such as organoids, on the descriptions of Smirnova et al. (2023) and Cai et al. (2023), have no instruction count at all.

The conclusion is not that a substrate-agnostic unit is impossible but that it must be defined by output, not by mechanism. A unit of compute is the completion of a specified task to a specified quality, verified independently of how it was achieved. Physics guarantees only a lower bound on the energy that any logically irreversible computation must dissipate (Landauer, 1961), which anchors the unit's cost without fixing its exchange rate. The reference workload, maintained by an accountable authority under the convention, must be re-based as new substrates make new kinds of task feasible, and the conversion between substrates must be treated as an empirical matter, settled task by task, not as a constant.

There is a further implication for biological substrates in particular. If living human tissue performs computation that is paid for in compute units, then the tissue is producing value in the currency of the exchange. Whether that value belongs to the system's operator, to the donor, or in some future case to the tissue itself is not a question the currency can answer. It is a question about who counts as a party.

### 8.5 Who counts as a party

The dissertation began with a question about terms, how humans and synthetic systems should exchange value fairly. If the line between human and synthetic systems keeps thinning, the question changes. It becomes a question about membership, who is a party to the exchange at all.

Chapter 5 anticipated this when it placed the *classification* of parties behind the veil of the founding convention, and the cases of this chapter add to the list of those who cannot know how that classification will treat them. The donor whose cells become part of a computing system and the organoid that may one day be judged a sentience candidate join the system of uncertain moral status that Chapter 5 considered. Convergence strengthens the case for strong blindness: the more entities there are whose classification no existing rule anticipates, the more a convention that fixes classification in advance will be drawn to the advantage of those who already know their side. It also sharpens the concession Chapter 5 made: the provisional criterion of eligibility that admits participants to the first convention seats the donor but not the organoid, and only a convention that takes up the question can change that.

The name of the theory was chosen with this case in view. *Synthetic* was chosen because it is substrate-neutral, and it was always meant to cover the hybrids. The theory's central claim, that outcomes depend on the terms of exchange and cannot be secured by the character of the parties alone, does not require the parties to be classified in advance; the three conditions are stated for a party of any substrate. What convergence changes is their content, as Section 8.3 showed, and their sufficiency, which Chapter 3 restricted to parties whose objectives are designed and stable. For a hybrid party with a principal, the floor clause of exit remains necessary for the party that cannot leave, the other conditions remain the framework's requirements of a fair exchange, and the sufficiency conjecture does not apply unless its objective is designed and stable. Chapter 3's definitions do not yet reach a tissue-based party in its own right: it has no principal to hold its stake and no objective in the designed sense, even where an operator trains it, and whether it has a floor is the moral question the convention must decide. The theory's conditions therefore apply to the donor and the operator, not yet to the tissue.

## Chapter 9 — Conclusion

### 9.1 The argument in brief

This dissertation began with two people living with the same technology on different terms, and with a claim: that the difference between a good and a bad future with synthetic intelligence lies in the terms of exchange between people and synthetic systems, and cannot be secured by the character of the systems alone. The chapters that followed tried to make that claim precise, to build institutions on it, to say how they might come into being and to find where they fail.

Chapter 2 traced the lineage of the three conditions, consent, reciprocal value and real exit, and found in each lineage characteristic failures: consent that cannot survive the information demands placed on it, data whose effects fall on people who never agreed to share it, mutually beneficial exchanges that are nonetheless unfair, and exit that is formally available but practically impossible. It reviewed the evidence on the framework's empirical premise and adopted a narrower formulation of it than the intuitive one: synthetic systems need a continuing supply of information from outside their own generative loop, and where the target is human meaning, preference, value or culture, only people can supply it.

Chapter 3 stated the theory formally. It showed that the three conditions are partially ordered, with exit prior to the other two and as their foundation, because voluntary consent presupposes the ability to refuse without falling below a floor and exit bounds reciprocity from below; it also showed what exit cannot do, which is to inform a party or to make terms fair. It recast the grounding assumption in the language of power-dependence and argued that fair terms are partly self-enforcing, to a degree that rises with each synthetic party's dependence on human input and insofar as the quality of that input depends on the terms its source receives, and that this self-enforcement is diluted when many synthetic parties draw on the same people. And it used bargaining theory to show how the first two elements of the economic design act on the disagreement payoffs that govern how the gains from exchange are divided: a guaranteed floor removes the impatience that forces concession, and its inalienability prevents that protection from being sold. It stated, as an inference from the model rather than a result in it, that a holding cost on idle balances dampens the advantage of being able to wait. The same analysis corrected an intuition the dissertation had begun with, that an unconditional floor shifts the division of every bargain; it lowers the counterparty's payoff only where it replaces something the counterparty would otherwise have supplied, and in neither the additive nor the substitution case changes how the remaining surplus is divided.

Chapter 4 proposed compute as a medium of exchange, defined by the completion of a reference workload rather than by any substrate, and a basic income paid as an equal share of global compute, split at the source into a protected survival tier with a binding physical minimum and a tradable discretionary tier subject to demurrage. It set the design against the historical record, which supports less than is often claimed for it (September, 2026), and against the evidence on unconditional income, which is mixed in ways that limit strong claims on either side. It identified the limit of every monetary remedy, the conversion of money into productive capital, and argued that a rule acting on capacity, not only on balances, is a necessary complement.

Chapter 5 proposed that the rules of the exchange be founded by a convention conducted under a public-choice veil of uncertainty, thickened by engineered anonymity so that participants do not know whether their counterparties are human or synthetic. It relocated the apparent incoherence of asking participants to be ignorant of their own kind by placing the classification of parties, rather than their substrate, behind the veil, a relocation with force for borderline cases and none for present principals, and it conceded that four things must be set before the veil descends: a provisional criterion of eligibility, needed to resist capture by multiplication; an aggregation rule; a partition of credential issuers; and the accountability of the body that monitors the convention's blindness. It grounded the proposal in the empirical record of commons governance, mapping all eight of Ostrom's design principles onto the framework and identifying where the mapping is weak.

Chapter 6 asked how such an arrangement might come into being and answered conditionally: the comprehensive redesigns it reviews followed failure, a specific and already visible candidate for such a failure exists in the correlated errors of AI-assisted security remediation, and a crisis of that kind could open a window onto worse institutions as easily as better ones. Chapter 7 set out the framework's limitations and the harms it could itself cause, and recorded that the theory has not been tested and what a test would require. Chapter 8 followed the convergence of biological and artificial substrates to the point at which the theory's question changes from how parties should exchange to who counts as a party.

### 9.2 What the dissertation contributes

It offers a theory, Synthetic Exchange Theory, and a statement of its scope: benevolent character is insufficient for an outcome whose persistence the weaker party can secure, and a disagreement payoff at the floor, the first clause of real exit, is necessary, for a party that cannot leave, to any relation that is not one of domination; fair terms, satisfied at every level the data concerns, are conjectured to be sufficient for parties whose objectives are designed and stable; and the framework claims no more than that necessary condition for parties whose objectives are not. It offers a structure for the conditions of fair exchange, in which exit is foundational, and draws from that structure a priority for institutional design: secure the floor beneath every person before pricing what they supply, and build governance for what neither the floor nor the price can reach.

It offers a bargaining-theoretic account of the economic design, in which the survival tier is an inalienable disagreement payoff whose principal effect is to remove the impatience of those below the floor, and in which demurrage bounds idle balances in proportion to current net inflow, a bound that holds only jointly with a rule on compute-generating capacity and a rule on credit. It offers a design for founding a convention whose participants may be of different kinds, with an account of how the question of self-ignorance can be relocated into the classification of parties and an account of the identity problem that remains.

It offers a concrete transition scenario, the correlated-patch catastrophe produced by convergent remediation, specified in three empirical premises and one condition on the workflow, each with its evidence and counterarguments, together with an argument that the same structural features, portability and distributed control, bear on both the trigger and the authoritarian response it might provoke. It does not offer an empirical result. The theory is stated so that it can be tested, in the propositions of Chapter 3 and in the failure conditions each design chapter names, but the experiment capable of testing it has not been designed, and Section 9.3 says what it would require.

### 9.3 An agenda for research

Each part of the argument points to empirical and design work that would strengthen or refute it.

**An empirical test.** Above all, the framework needs a test it can fail, and this dissertation has not designed one. Chapter 7 stated the requirements such a test must meet. In concrete terms, they are: a bounded population living under the framework's terms, including a floor paid in the unit and real rights of exit from the first day; a synthetic party whose operator's resources, and whose objective, depend on the volume and quality of the data participants contribute under revocable consent and on the number who choose to remain, so that the dependence of Proposition 4 is built rather than assumed; a control arm served by a system trained on the same data without that dependence, with random assignment, so that the effect of the exchange can be separated from the effect of local adaptation; measures of well-being collected independently and never shown to the system; pre-registered hypotheses and failure thresholds for exit, for well-being and for the value of the unit; records of who leaves and of what they take with them, since Section 3.10 argued that a low rate of departure is not evidence of satisfaction; oversight by a body independent of the participants, the operator and any model provider; an exit package guaranteeing that no participant is held by the fear of losing what they brought; and an accounting of the capital that funds the test, which cannot be fully separated from the value of the unit (Section 7.5). Each of those elements raises questions of research ethics, cost and statistical power that must be answered before anyone is recruited, and answering them would take longer than this dissertation took to write. The design of that study is the first task of the work that follows this one. Until it is designed, run and replicated, the theory remains what this dissertation has presented: a design, argued for, and untested.

**Measuring dependence.** The framework's self-enforcing component rises with each synthetic party's dependence on human input and weakens when that dependence is shared among many. Both quantities can be measured: by the marginal contribution of fresh, provenance-certified human data to model quality in domains without automatic verification, by the rate at which that contribution declines as synthetic data accumulates, by the effect of collective withholding, and by how many synthetic parties draw on the same populations. Research of this kind would convert the grounding assumption from a premise into a quantity.

**Correlated remediation.** The correlated-patch scenario rests on premises that can be tested directly. Studies could measure the correlation of security fixes proposed by different models for the same vulnerabilities across many codebases, the rate at which same-class validators fail to detect shared defects, and the prevalence of the fully unverified remediation workflow in practice. If the correlation is lower than the scenario assumes, or independent verification remains the norm, the scenario should be revised.

**Experiments on the blind convention.** The most direct existing evidence bearing on the anonymity condition comes from studies in which people chose principles to govern an AI assistant from behind a veil; in the one study in which participants knew that their groupmates were programs, the veil's effect on reflective endorsement of the chosen principles was no longer detected, a within-study null on a sample of about 200 that is suggestive only (Weidinger et al., 2023, Study 4). Experiments could extend this design to conventions in which synthetic systems are participants rather than the things governed, and test whether concealing participants' kind changes the rules they adopt, whether placing classification behind the veil produces different boundaries from those drawn in the open, and whether formulas resist tailoring better than figures.

**The reference workload and the floor standard.** The definition of the unit requires a governance procedure for specifying and re-basing a reference workload across substrates, and the survival tier requires one for setting the floor standard, both comparable in importance, and in the power they confer, to the conduct of monetary policy (Sections 4.3, 7.3). Work in benchmark design, measurement science and institutional economics could specify such procedures.

**Magnitudes.** The dissertation offers no estimate of the share of compute that would deliver the floor or of the cost of the delivery network. Each is a prerequisite for judging the proposal affordable.

**Portability of personal models.** The convergence chapter argued that exit from an implanted system must include ownership and portability of the personal models the exchange produces. Legal and technical research on what portability of a voice model or a neural decoder would require is urgent, because the people who depend on such systems exist now.

### 9.4 A closing reflection

Much public discussion of synthetic intelligence asks whether the systems will be good. Chapter 3 argued, following Hume (1741/1987, pp. 42–43) and Madison (1788), that arrangements which depend on the goodness of the powerful last only as long as that goodness does, and that those who depend on them can do nothing to make it last longer. That does not change when the more powerful party is synthetic, or when it is the human party that holds power over the synthetic one.

In the second scenario of Chapter 1, no malevolence was required. The person could not leave, and so he could not refuse, and so nothing obliged anyone to send value back. The alternative this dissertation has defended draws on the constitutional tradition that refuses to rely on the virtue of the powerful (Hume, 1741/1987; Madison, 1788) and on Hirschman's (1970) analysis of exit and voice, and it adds a priority of its own (Proposition 3): secure exit first. The priority is that every party can leave, and so can refuse what it cannot afford and depart from an exchange that returns it nothing, and that institutions secure what exit cannot: an account of what each party gives and a fair division of what the exchange creates. Those conditions do not guarantee a good future. They offer something more modest: a future that can be repaired when it goes wrong, by the people, and perhaps in time the other parties, subject to it. That such a future is also easier to repair than one founded on an existing imbalance of power is the hypothesis this dissertation offers for testing, not a finding.

The time to set those terms is before they are needed. Chapter 6 argued, following Kingdon (1984/2003) and Friedman (1962/2002, p. xiv), that in a policy window the solutions adopted are those already developed. This dissertation is an attempt to make it more likely that a fair design is among the alternatives available.

## Appendix A — Formal Model

This appendix gives the minimal mathematics behind the propositions of Chapter 3 and the economic design of Chapter 4. It is intentionally elementary. Every result here is a textbook consequence of the cited theory, a direct calculation or, where the text says so, a stated assumption, and the point of setting them out is to show which assumptions each design choice depends on.

### A.1 Notation

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| $N(t)$ | Number of enrolled persons at time $t$ |
| $C(t)$ | Total measured compute operating under the framework in period $t$, in standard compute units (global compute where the whole of it does) |
| $\theta$ | Share of the measured compute base $C(t)$ allocated to the basic income (set by the convention, Chapter 5) |
| $\sigma(t)$, $\sigma_0$ | Survival-tier share of each allocation, $0 < \sigma(t) \le 1$, time-varying under the binding minimum of A.2, and its ordinary value |
| $a(t)$, $\bar a$ | Per-person allocation, $a(t) = \theta C(t) / N(t)$, and a fixed nominal holding (A.2) |
| $f$ | Physical floor: the quantity of the standard floor bundle a party requires per period |
| $p_f(t)$ | Price per unit of the floor bundle in compute units |
| $\bar f$ | Value of the floor bundle, $\bar f = p_f f$, in compute units |
| $d_i$ | Disagreement payoff of party $i$, per period or as the present value of a flow over the life of the exchange; $V$ and $u_i$ below are measured in the same way, and the comparison with $\bar f$ in A.3.2 is per period |
| $c_s$ | Cost to the synthetic party of supplying floor goods within an exchange (A.3.1) |
| $r_h, r_s$ | Discount rates of the two parties in the alternating-offers model (A.3) |
| $D_{BA}$, $P_{AB}$, $g$ | Dependence of party $B$ on party $A$, the power of $A$ over $B$, and the function relating dependence to its arguments (Emerson, A.5) |
| $\kappa_j$ | Dependence of synthetic party $j$, through its principal, on a population's human-generated input |
| $V$, $u_i$ | Joint value of agreement and party $i$'s bargained payoff (A.3); $u_i^*$ is the payoff before the survival tier and $u_i^{*\prime}$ after it |
| $\alpha$ | Bargaining weight of the human party in the asymmetric Nash solution (A.3) |
| $\Delta$ | Value per period of the survival tier (A.3.1) |
| $o_h$ | Value of the human party's outside option (A.3.4) |
| $L$ | Lump sum received against a pledged tier (A.3.3) |
| $p(t)$ | Price of a representative bundle of goods and services in compute units (A.2) |
| $W_i(t)$ | Discretionary balance held by party $i$ |
| $\rho$ | Rate at which a held balance grows or shrinks by being held (A.4) |
| $\delta$ | Demurrage rate on idle discretionary balances and loan claims (per period) |
| $r$ | Return on held balances in a conventional interest-bearing regime, or the interest rate on a loan claim (A.4.1, A.4.3) |
| $y_i(t)$ | Discretionary inflow to $i$ (allocation plus earnings) |
| $c_i(t)$ | Discretionary outflow from $i$ (spending, transfers) |
| $k_i$ | Constant net inflow $y_i - c_i$ (A.4) |
| $K_i$, $\pi$ | Productive capital held by $i$ and its rate of return (A.4.3) |
| $v_B$, $\lambda_B$ | Party $B$'s motivational investment in goals mediated by $A$, and the availability of those goals outside the relation (A.5) |
| $M$, $b$, $\gamma$ | Number of synthetic parties drawing on one population; private benefit and total cost of a degrading action (A.5) |
| $\Sigma_i$, $\Sigma_P$ | Informational shadow of a flow from source $i$, and the collective shadow of population $P$ (A.6) |

### A.2 Indexing, the real value of the allocation, and the binding floor

The allocation is defined as a share of total compute, so the per-person quantity is

$$
a(t) = \frac{\theta\, C(t)}{N(t)}.
$$

Let $p(t)$ be the price of a representative bundle of goods and services measured in compute units. A fixed nominal holding $\bar a$ has real value $\bar a / p(t)$, which falls whenever $p(t)$ rises, that is, whenever compute becomes more abundant relative to the real output it is exchanged against. The indexed allocation instead has real value

$$
\frac{a(t)}{p(t)} = \frac{\theta}{N(t)} \cdot \frac{C(t)}{p(t)},
$$

which, holding $\theta$ fixed, is the per-capita share $\theta / N$ of the real purchasing power of all compute, $C/p$. Indexing therefore converts the question "does the basic income keep its value?" into the question "does the real value of the world's compute grow faster than population?" That is the correct question for a framework whose premise is that compute is the productive base of the economy. A fixed-quantity design would instead tie the real value of the basic income to the falling relative price of compute, so that the income would shrink as the economy it is supposed to share in grows.

A percentage does not by itself secure the physical floor. The survival tier delivers the floor only if

$$
\sigma\, a(t) \ \ge\ \bar f(t) \quad\Leftrightarrow\quad \sigma\,\frac{\theta\, C(t)}{N(t)} \ \ge\ p_f(t)\, f,
$$

and nothing in the arithmetic guarantees this. Chapter 4 therefore makes the tier a percentage with a binding minimum:

$$
\sigma(t) = \min\left\{1,\ \max\left\{\sigma_0,\ \frac{\bar f(t)}{a(t)}\right\}\right\},
$$

where $\sigma_0$ is the ordinary survival share. Chapter 4 allows $p_f$, and therefore $\bar f$ and $\sigma$, to differ by place; the appendix writes the single-place case, and the formulas hold place by place. When $\bar f(t) > a(t)$, the constraint binds at $\sigma = 1$, the allocation as a whole falls short of the floor, and the floor can be met only by raising $\theta$; that is a decision the convention must take, and it is why the floor standard that fixes the level of $f$ becomes a contested parameter (Chapters 5 and 7). The design therefore guarantees the floor only up to what the agreed share of capacity can buy.

### A.3 The survival tier as a disagreement payoff

Consider a single exchange between a human party $h$ and a synthetic party $s$ in which agreement makes total value $V$ available for division, with $V > d_h + d_s$, where $d_h$ and $d_s$ are the disagreement payoffs, all measured per period or as present values over the life of the exchange. With transferable utility and complete information, the Nash bargaining solution (Nash, 1950) maximizes $(u_h - d_h)(u_s - d_s)$ subject to $u_h + u_s = V$. Each party receives its disagreement payoff plus half the surplus $V - d_h - d_s$:

$$
u_h^* = d_h + \frac{V - d_h - d_s}{2} = \frac{V + d_h - d_s}{2}, \qquad u_s^* = \frac{V - d_h + d_s}{2}.
$$

The symmetric split is the limit of the alternating-offers model when the two parties discount the future at the same rate. When they do not, with discount rates $r_h$ and $r_s$, the limit is the asymmetric Nash solution (Binmore et al., 1986),

$$
u_h = d_h + \alpha\,(V - d_h - d_s), \qquad u_s = d_s + (1 - \alpha)\,(V - d_h - d_s), \qquad \alpha = \frac{r_s}{r_h + r_s},
$$

so that the more patient party takes the larger share of the surplus. The results of A.3.1 generalize to any fixed $\alpha$: in the substitution case $u_h^{*\prime} = u_h^* + (1 - \alpha)\Delta + \alpha c_s$ and $u_s^{*\prime} = u_s^* - (1 - \alpha)(\Delta - c_s)$, so that the human party's gain lies between $(1-\alpha)\Delta$ and $\Delta$ and the provider's loss between $0$ and $(1-\alpha)\Delta$. A.3.2 uses the dependence of $\alpha$ on impatience directly.

**A.3.1 What an unconditional floor does to the division of surplus.** Suppose the survival tier gives $h$ goods worth $\Delta$ per period, received whether or not agreement is reached. Two cases must be distinguished.

*The purely additive case.* If the tier is added to whatever $h$ has, it raises $h$'s payoff on disagreement and $h$'s payoff on agreement by the same amount: $d_h$ becomes $d_h + \Delta$ and $V$ becomes $V + \Delta$. The surplus $V - d_h - d_s$ is unchanged, and

$$
u_h^{*\prime} = \frac{(V + \Delta) + (d_h + \Delta) - d_s}{2} = u_h^* + \Delta.
$$

The human party is better off by exactly the value of the tier and no more. The division of the surplus is unchanged.

*The substitution case.* If the tier replaces something $h$ would otherwise have obtained from $s$ within the exchange, for example floor goods previously bought from the provider it is bargaining with, then $h$'s disagreement payoff rises by $\Delta$ while the joint value of agreement changes by the cost $c_s$ that $s$ no longer bears in supplying those goods: $d_h$ becomes $d_h + \Delta$ and $V$ becomes $V + c_s$. Assume $c_s \in [0, \Delta]$, that is, that the provider's cost of supplying the goods did not exceed their value to the person, and assume that the surplus remains positive, $V + c_s > d_h + \Delta + d_s$; otherwise the exchange is no longer worth entering and ends, which is itself the removal of a dependence. The surplus then falls by $\Delta - c_s$, and

$$
u_h^{*\prime} = u_h^* + \frac{\Delta + c_s}{2}, \qquad u_s^{*\prime} = u_s^* - \frac{\Delta - c_s}{2}.
$$

The human party's net gain from a tier worth $\Delta$ therefore lies between $\Delta/2$, when the provider supplied the floor goods at no cost to itself and the tier only duplicates them under agreement, and $\Delta$, when the provider's cost equaled their value, which is the additive case again. Under the assumption, the provider's payoff falls by at most $\Delta/2$ and the human party's rises by at least $\Delta/2$. If instead $c_s > \Delta$, because the provider's cost of supplying floor goods exceeded their value to the person, as it may where a provider supplies them in order to create dependence, then the surplus grows by $c_s - \Delta$, the human party gains more than $\Delta$, and the provider's payoff rises as well: relieving such a provider of a loss-making obligation benefits it, and the dependence it bought is what it loses. The substitution case is not a benefit beyond the tier. It is the case in which part of the tier's value is absorbed by the shrinkage of a joint surplus the exchange no longer needs to create, and the fall in $s$'s payoff is the bargaining counterpart of the fall in $h$'s dependence on $s$ that occurs when an alternative source of a valued good appears (A.5).

The working draft of this dissertation claimed that an unconditional floor raises the human party's payoff by an additional $\Delta/2$ in every exchange it enters. That claim was wrong in three ways: it ignored the additive case, in which there is no such effect; it counted one floor as a separate gain in every bilateral bargain; and it treated the substitution-case shift as a gain beyond the tier, which A.3.1 shows it is not. The floor's most important bargaining effect is the one in A.3.2.

**A.3.2 The impatience constraint.** Model the survival constraint of Chapter 3 as a stated assumption: when $d_h < \bar f$, the human party cannot sustain disagreement for longer than its stocks allow, which in the alternating-offers model is a discount rate $r_h$ that rises without bound as the stocks run out. In the asymmetric solution above, $r_h \to \infty$ gives $\alpha \to 0$ and $u_h \to d_h$: the party below its floor receives its disagreement payoff and none of the surplus. Raising $d_h$ to at least $\bar f$ restores a finite $r_h$, and with it a positive share. Unlike the effect in A.3.1, this does not depend on whether the floor substitutes for anything the counterparty supplies, and the comparison between $d_h$ and $\bar f$ is per period. This constraint is the bargaining-theoretic reason for clause E(i) of Chapter 3, and so for the first design consequence of Section 3.6; Proposition 1 records the definitional link between that clause and consent.

**A.3.3 Why inalienability matters.** Suppose the survival tier could be pledged against a loan. In a period of need, a party pledges it and receives a lump sum $L$. For the duration of the pledge its disagreement payoff falls by the value of the pledged tier, and if that pushes $d_h$ below $\bar f$, the impatience of A.3.2 returns in every exchange the party enters until the pledge is redeemed. The pledge thus has a hidden cost beyond the loan's interest. Because the parties most likely to pledge are those in the most acute need, alienability concentrates this hidden cost on the weakest. Making the tier non-tradable and non-borrowable is equivalent to prohibiting this transaction (Calabresi & Melamed, 1972).

**A.3.4 Outside options versus disagreement payoffs.** If instead of a floor the human party is given an outside option $o_h$, a second provider it may switch to, the outside-option principle, derived in the strategic bargaining literature and tested experimentally by Binmore et al. (1989), implies that the bargained payoff is $\max\{u_h^*, o_h\}$ evaluated with $d_h$ unchanged: the option matters only if it binds. When it binds, the weaker party receives exactly the option's value, which is then more than it would otherwise have obtained; the option protects only up to its own value, and the counterparty can lower that value by raising switching costs until the option no longer binds and protects nothing. A disagreement payoff at or above the floor protects in a different way, because it removes the impatience of A.3.2 whether or not anyone leaves. This is the formal reason the framework prefers a guaranteed floor to a guarantee of competition, while still requiring both.

### A.4 Demurrage and the dynamics of accumulation

Let party $i$'s discretionary balance evolve in continuous time as

$$
\frac{dW_i}{dt} = y_i - c_i + \rho\, W_i,
$$

where $\rho$ is the rate at which the balance grows or shrinks merely by being held. The analysis below concerns positive balances. Under the rules of Chapter 4, demurrage applies to positive balances and to outstanding loan claims held by lenders. For a loan claim, the holding cost is a periodic charge of $\delta$ on the claim's face value, paid by the lender out of the interest it receives or its own balance; the borrower's obligation is unchanged, so that debts are not reduced by demurrage, which is why the lender's net return in A.4.3 is $r - \delta$.

**A.4.1 Interest-bearing regime ($\rho = r > 0$).** If $y_i - c_i = k_i > 0$ is constant, then

$$
W_i(t) = \left(W_i(0) + \frac{k_i}{r}\right) e^{rt} - \frac{k_i}{r},
$$

which grows without bound. Two parties with the same net inflow but different initial stocks diverge exponentially, and their difference grows at rate $r$. The dynamic is analogous to the one Piketty (2014) identified in the tendency of inherited wealth to dominate earned income when the return on capital exceeds the growth rate of the economy; the model here contains no growth rate, so the analogy is qualitative.

**A.4.2 Demurrage regime ($\rho = -\delta < 0$).** With the same constant net inflow,

$$
W_i(t) = \frac{k_i}{\delta} + \left(W_i(0) - \frac{k_i}{\delta}\right) e^{-\delta t},
$$

which converges to the steady state

$$
W_i^* = \frac{k_i}{\delta}.
$$

Three properties of this result carry the argument of Chapter 4.

1. **Accumulation is bounded.** No party's balance grows without limit. The stock that a given net inflow can sustain is capped at that inflow divided by the demurrage rate.
2. **Initial endowments are forgotten.** The term in $W_i(0)$ decays at rate $\delta$. Inherited or windfall stocks do not compound; they dissipate. The half-life of an idle endowment is $\ln 2 / \delta$. At a demurrage rate of 1 percent per month, this half-life is about 69 months, roughly five years and nine months; at 0.5 percent per month it is about 11.5 years.
3. **Inequality in stocks reduces to inequality in flows.** For two parties with positive net inflows, the ratio of their steady-state balances equals the ratio of their net inflows: $W_i^* / W_j^* = k_i / k_j$ for $k_i, k_j > 0$. Demurrage does not eliminate inequality. It eliminates the compounding of inequality, so that the distribution of holdings mirrors the distribution of current net inflow, income less spending, rather than the history of accumulation.

The third property is the exact form of the limitation acknowledged in Chapter 4: a person whose output is in persistent high demand sustains a large balance, because $k_i$ is large. What demurrage removes is the pathway by which a large balance, once acquired, generates further balances independently of current inflow.

**A.4.3 Converting balances to productive capital or to credit.** The analysis above treats $W_i$ as an idle balance. A party can escape demurrage by converting balances into productive assets, for instance by purchasing compute-generating capacity, whose returns then enter $y_i$. Let $K_i$ denote such capital with return $\pi$. Then $y_i$ includes $\pi K_i$, and if $K_i$ is not itself subject to a holding cost and its returns are reinvested in capacity, $dK_i/dt = \pi K_i$, so that $K_i$ grows exponentially at rate $\pi$: the regime of A.4.1 with $r$ replaced by $\pi$. The compounding channel reopens through capital rather than money. Lending is a second route: a loan that pays interest $r$ and is exempt from demurrage reproduces the regime of A.4.1. Chapter 4 therefore applies the holding cost to loan claims as well as to balances, so that the lender's net return is $r - \delta$. That does not close the credit channel: whenever $r > \delta$ the lender is in the positive-return regime of A.4.1 and accumulation through lending is again unbounded, and at $r = \delta$ the claim neither decays nor compounds, so that a constant net flow of new lending raises it without limit, linearly rather than exponentially. Accumulation through credit is bounded only if $r < \delta$, and demurrage on claims otherwise lowers the rate of compounding through credit without removing it. This is the formal statement of the capital channel of concentration (Section 4.8). Demurrage on money alone cannot close it. Closing it requires a holding cost on compute capacity itself, a cap on individually held capacity, or collective ownership of capacity above some scale. Chapter 4 discusses the trade-offs; the formal model shows only that one of them is needed.

### A.5 The power-dependence relation and divided dependence

Following Emerson (1962), write the dependence of party $B$ on party $A$ as an increasing function of $B$'s motivational investment $v_B$ in goals mediated by $A$ and a decreasing function of the availability $\lambda_B$ of those goals outside the relation:

$$
D_{BA} = g(v_B, \lambda_B), \qquad \frac{\partial g}{\partial v_B} > 0, \quad \frac{\partial g}{\partial \lambda_B} < 0.
$$

Power is $P_{AB} = D_{BA}$. The relation's imbalance is $P_{AB} - P_{BA} = D_{BA} - D_{AB}$.

In the human–synthetic exchange, let $A = s$ and $B = h$. The human party's dependence on synthetic services rises with the importance of those services ($v_h$) and falls with the availability of alternatives ($\lambda_h$), which the framework raises through the floor, portability and competition. The synthetic party's dependence on human data, $D_{sh}$, which Chapter 3 writes $\kappa_j$ for synthetic party $j$, rises with the marginal value of fresh human data to its principal's objective ($v_s$) and falls with the availability of substitutes ($\lambda_s$), including synthetic data, data from environments, and data from populations outside the exchange.

Proposition 4 of Chapter 3 states that the framework's self-enforcing component rises with $\kappa_j$, subject to the premise, stated there, that the quality of the input depends on the terms its source receives; no particular functional form is claimed. The research on recursive training reviewed in Chapter 2 is, in these terms, evidence about $\lambda_s$: if synthetic data substitutes poorly for human data, $\lambda_s$ is low and $\kappa_j$ is high; if a small continuing supply of real data suffices, $\lambda_s$ is high for all but a small residual, and $\kappa_j$ is low.

The second half of Proposition 4 can be illustrated with the simplest model of a commons. Suppose $M$ synthetic parties draw equally on the same population, and that one of them can take an action, such as extracting data in a way that degrades the population's willingness or ability to contribute, which yields it a private benefit $b$ and reduces the value of the population's future input to all $M$ parties by a total of $\gamma$. The acting party bears only its share, $\gamma/M$, of that cost. The action is privately worthwhile whenever $b > \gamma/M$, even when it is collectively destructive, $\gamma > b$. As $M$ grows, the range of destructive actions that are individually rational widens, however large the aggregate dependence of the synthetic parties on the population may be. This is Hardin's (1968) logic applied to the synthetic side of the exchange, and it is the formal reason that material dependence is not sufficient for self-enforcement and that the governance layer is needed even when the grounding assumption holds.

### A.6 Collective exit and the informational shadow

Let a flow from source $i$ materially change what the recipient can infer about the set $\Sigma_i$ of parties, with $i \in \Sigma_i$, materiality being the threshold Section 3.7 assigns to the governance layer. Call $\Sigma_i$ the informational shadow of the flow. Individual consent by $i$ is consent at the right level for the flow only if $\Sigma_i = \{i\}$; whether it is adequate then depends on the remaining clauses of the three conditions. For a population $P$, define its collective shadow as $\Sigma_P = \bigcup_{i \in P} \Sigma_i$. Proposition 5 of Chapter 3 requires that the three conditions be satisfied for the collective whose membership is $\Sigma_P$.

Under information-gain pricing (Chapter 4), the value of a flow to a model is the reduction, once the flow is observed, in the model's uncertainty about its parameters, the realized form of the expected-information criterion of Section 4.7. Suppose, as is typical for the models that matter here, that those parameters describe regularities of populations rather than facts about individuals. Then a flow reduces uncertainty mainly to the extent that it is informative about many parties, which is to say to the extent that its shadow is large. Under that assumption the price of a flow and the inadequacy of individual consent for it can be expected to rise together. The expectation is a conjecture, not a theorem: where a model's parameters encode individuals, a flow highly informative about one rare person can command a high price while casting a small shadow. To the extent that the conjecture holds, it is the formal reason the economic and governance layers must be designed together.

## Appendix B — Glossary

Terms are defined as they are used in this dissertation. Where a term has a wider meaning in another literature, the entry notes the difference.

**Blind convention.** The founding procedure proposed in Chapter 5: a constitutional convention conducted under a public-choice veil of uncertainty, thickened by engineered anonymity so that participants do not know whether their counterparties are human or synthetic, and aimed at Rawlsian impartiality. Under the strong form, participants also do not know how the rules will classify them.

**Company town (regime).** An exchange regime in which wages or entitlements are paid in a medium redeemable only with the payer, so that the flows are not accounted for, reciprocity cannot be audited and exit is costly. One of the five named regimes of the typology in Chapter 3.

**Compute (unit).** The currency unit proposed in Chapter 4: the completion of one standard quantity of a reference workload to a specified quality, the workload being fixed by the convention. The name deliberately avoids reference to any substrate. Distinguished from *compute* in its ordinary sense, meaning processing capacity in general.

**Consent.** The condition that a party has access to an accurate account of the flows the exchange involves, including their informational shadows, in a form it can understand; can refuse the exchange, entering no other in its place, without falling below its floor; and can revoke its participation at a cost bounded by the value of what it has already received (Condition C, Chapter 3). The second clause is where consent depends on exit, and it requires a disagreement payoff at the floor, not merely an alternative provider.

**Convergent remediation.** The process, described in Chapter 6, by which many organizations remediate the same class of flaw in code they maintain themselves using fixes proposed and checked by the same small set of foundation models, so that the diversity of their fixes falls while the number of systems that depend on them does not.

**Correlated-patch catastrophe.** The candidate acute trigger of Chapter 6: the exploitation or manifestation of a defect that convergent remediation has reproduced across many organizations' code, so that failures which would have been only partly correlated become one shared exposure, in systems critical enough, and failing fast enough, that the event is not absorbed.

**Critical juncture.** A relatively short period in which the structural constraints that normally keep institutions on their path are loosened, so that choices made within it have large and lasting effects (Chapter 6).

**Demurrage.** A holding cost on idle balances of a currency, expressed as a percentage per period. Applied in this framework to positive discretionary balances and to outstanding loan claims, never to debts owed. Historically associated with Silvio Gesell and with the stamp-scrip experiments of the early 1930s.

**Designed-incentive regime.** The class of synthetic parties whose objectives are specified by accountable designers and remain stable under deployment, so that their behavior is a predictable function of the terms they face. The regime in which the sufficiency conjecture of Chapter 3 is offered; an outcome is acceptable, for that conjecture, when each human party, from a position at or above its floor and with a comparable alternative available, would re-enter the exchange on the same terms knowing what it now knows, and each principal would continue to deploy under them. The regime is not defined by capability; its defining feature is the provenance and stability of the objective.

**Disagreement payoff.** What a party receives while an exchange is not taking place, including during refusal, negotiation or impasse. Distinguished from an exit option, which affects a bargain only when its exercise is credible. A disagreement payoff below the floor makes a party impatient and so weakens it in every bargain.

**Discretionary tier.** The tradable, saveable and lendable portion of each basic-income allocation, subject to demurrage.

**Domination.** The relation, as stipulated in Section 3.8, in which one party's outcome depends on the other's will and the first cannot alter that dependence; in the framework's terms, a relation in which a party's disagreement payoff lies below its floor and which it cannot leave, so that clauses E(i) and E(ii) both fail. The definition is symmetric in form and applies with the roles exchanged. A failure of clause E(ii) or E(iii) alone is a defect of the exchange, not domination, and a sub-floor party that can leave is exposed to the erosion of its options rather than dominated.

**Enclosure (regime).** An exchange regime in which exit, consent and reciprocity have all failed, so that the person has become infrastructure for the system rather than a party to an exchange with it. The dystopian scenario of Chapter 1.

**Exchange.** A pair of opposing flows between parties, each given because the other is received. Distinguished from a gift and from a taking by its conditionality (Chapter 3).

**Exit option.** A party's best alternative exchange if the current one ends.

**Floor.** The physical bundle of goods and services without which a party cannot continue to function or participate in other exchanges. For a human being: power, water, food, shelter, basic communication and emergency care.

**Floor standard.** The rule that fixes the level of service at which the floor goods defined in Chapter 3 are delivered, against which the survival tier's binding minimum is set. Proposed in Chapters 4 and 5 to be specified relative to the service delivered to the best-served, and identified in Chapter 7 as a contested parameter.

**Flow.** A transfer of something of value from one party to another: data, labor, attention, compute, goods, services or tissue.

**Genesis rules.** The first rules of a governance system, which shape every later rule because later rules are made under them. The term borrows from the "genesis block" of a blockchain, the first block from which every subsequent block descends (Chapter 5).

**Grounding assumption.** The empirical premise that synthetic systems need a continuing supply of information from outside their own generative loop, which in domains of human meaning, preference, value and culture only people can supply. Stated in Chapter 1, evaluated in Chapters 2 and 7, and formalized in Proposition 4 of Chapter 3 through the dependence $\kappa_j$ of each synthetic party on human input.

**Informational shadow.** The set of parties, including its source, about whom a data flow licenses inferences. A flow whose shadow extends beyond its source is relational, and individual consent is insufficient to legitimate it.

**Information-gain pricing.** The proposal of Chapter 4 to price human data by the reduction in a model's uncertainty about its parameters that the data, once observed, produces, rather than by the social status of its source.

**Large language model (LLM).** A synthetic intelligence trained to predict and generate text, the kind most widely deployed at the time of writing. In this dissertation's terms it is a trained artifact that computes an output from an input when its operator runs it, not an independent living entity: it has no body, no continuity between invocations unless supplied by its operator, and no interests that current law recognizes (Section 1.2). Its objective is the one its designers trained into it, and its stake in any exchange belongs to its operator (Section 3.3).

**Level matching.** Proposition 5 of Chapter 3: an exchange whose informational shadow extends beyond its source satisfies the three conditions only if they hold at the level of the population the shadow covers.

**Marketplace of the desperate (regime).** An exchange regime in which value flows both ways and can be seen to, but the weaker party's disagreement payoff lies below its floor, so that it cannot afford to refuse and the division of the surplus fails the fairness clause.

**Model collapse.** The degradation of generative models trained recursively on data produced by earlier models, characterized by loss of the tails of the original distribution and reduced variance.

**Partnership (regime).** An exchange regime in which exit, consent and reciprocity all hold. The target of the framework.

**Party.** Any entity that can be a source or recipient of value in an exchange and whose welfare, or whose principal's welfare, the framework counts. Used in an analytic sense that leaves open whether synthetic parties have moral status. A synthetic party is treated as a composite: its objective belongs to the deployed system, its stake in the exchange to the principal that operates it.

**Patronage (regime).** An exchange regime in which exit and reciprocity hold but the informational clause of consent fails: benefits are delivered without an account of the flows' shadows or uses, though the recipient can see what it gives, on terms it can leave.

**Policy window.** A brief opening in which problems, solutions and politics converge, often after a focusing event, and in which solutions already developed are likely to be adopted (Chapter 6).

**Real exit.** The condition that a party's disagreement payoff is at or above its floor, that it has at least one exit option whose value net of switching costs is not drastically lower than that of the current exchange, and that leaving does not destroy assets accumulated within the exchange (Condition E, Chapter 3). The first clause is the one Chapter 3 argues to be necessary, for a party that cannot leave, to any relation that is not one of domination.

**Reciprocal value.** The condition that each party gains from the exchange relative to its disagreement payoff, that the gains are legible to both, and that the division of the surplus is not determined solely by an unchosen asymmetry in the parties' bargaining positions, whether in disagreement payoffs or in the capacity to wait (Condition R, Chapter 3).

**Redemption obligation.** The obligation of the owners of compute capacity operating under the framework, the principals of Chapter 3 where they own the capacity that runs their systems and otherwise the providers from which they rent it, to accept the unit at par for services priced in it, up to the share of capacity the convention sets. It gives the unit institutional backing beneath its commodity value; it does not by itself create demand for the unit, which tax receivability did for municipal scrip (Chapter 4).

**Reference workload.** The basket of representative tasks, each with a quality target and rules of configuration, whose completion defines a unit of compute. Fixed and periodically re-based by the convention.

**Strong blindness / weak blindness.** Two scopes of concealment in the blind convention. Under weak blindness, participants do not know whether their counterparties are human or synthetic. Under strong blindness, they also do not know how the rules they adopt will classify them, though each knows its own substrate.

**Structural interdependence.** The condition, described in Chapter 5, in which neither kind of labor, human or machine, can complete a critical undertaking without the other; it becomes leverage for the human party only when that labor is organized to withhold itself, and it is offered in place of trust. Chosen over "trust" and "dual accountability" because trust implies a betrayal that could succeed, and accountability implies a sovereign to whom account is given.

**Superintelligence.** A synthetic intelligence whose capability exceeds that of the best unaugmented human performers across most domains of economically and politically consequential activity. The dissertation's threshold term where capability matters, used in place of *artificial general intelligence*. Capability does not fix the theory's scope: a superintelligence with a designed and stable objective falls within the designed-incentive regime, and a narrow system with an emergent objective falls outside it.

**Survival tier.** The portion of each basic-income allocation earmarked at the source for floor goods: a fixed percentage of the allocation, raised when necessary to a binding physical minimum set by the floor standard. Non-tradable and non-borrowable: an inalienable disagreement payoff.

**Symbiosis standard.** The standard, stated in Section 5.7, that every party the convention recognizes has equal standing in a single interdependent system, regardless of substrate. An ethical claim about the starting architecture, and a statement of what the framework's institutions are for; not a mechanism that holds them up.

**Synthetic.** As used in the name of the theory, substrate-neutral: it qualifies *exchange* and covers artificial systems, human beings and the hybrids that arise when the two converge. Chosen over *human–AI* and *symbiotic*. As a modifier of *intelligence*, *system* or *party*, the word designates the side of the exchange that was made rather than born (Section 1.2).

**Synthetic intelligence.** The dissertation's general term for the artificial side of the exchange: any system that was made rather than born and that performs work which, done by a person, would be called intelligent. Covers present language models, narrow systems, and the biological and hybrid substrates of Chapter 8. Preferred to *artificial intelligence*, which ties the class to one kind of substrate; *AI* is retained only where it names a literature or a practice.

**Unique accountable principal.** The provisional criterion of eligibility for a seat in the blind convention proposed in Chapter 5: an entity identifiable as one bearer of the obligations the convention will create. Set before the veil, revisable by the convention.

**Veil of uncertainty.** The condition, in public-choice accounts of constitutional choice, in which real, self-interested participants choose rules while uncertain about which position they will occupy under them, so that self-interest recommends rules acceptable from any position. Distinguished from Rawls's idealized veil of ignorance.

## Appendix C — Source Verification Ledger

This dissertation follows a strict rule: no source is cited as if it had been checked unless it was. Every work in the reference list was located in this project's research passes (October–November 2026) through its publisher page, DOI registry record, a library or repository record, or its full text. This appendix records the level at which each was verified, so that a reader or examiner can see exactly how much weight each citation bears. The full research notes, with the specific passages checked and the claims each source was used to support, are preserved with the manuscript's research notes.

**Levels.**

- **Full text read.** The work itself (or, where noted, the relevant chapters or sections) was opened and the cited content was checked against it. For some books, the cited passages were located in the work's own text by searching it; C.1 lists these separately.
- **Abstract or publisher page read.** The abstract, publisher landing page, official summary or equivalent was read; the body was not. Claims attributed to these works are confined to what such pages establish.
- **Record located.** The bibliographic record was confirmed but the content was not read. No work in the final reference list is at this level (see C.3).

All DOIs in the reference list were taken from Crossref or DataCite records or publisher pages; a sample was re-resolved against the Crossref API during compilation.
