### C.2 Abstract or publisher page read

None. The works that remained at this level after the fifth pass were removed from the reference list or replaced by works that were read, as follows.

- **Thirteen works left at abstract level after the fifth pass.** In a sixth pass, ten were removed from the reference list and their claims re-founded on works that were read or cut: Abramitzky (2018) and Fishback (1986), each cited alongside a companion work that was read (Abramitzky, 2008; Fishback, 1992); Cook and Emerson (1978), whose finding is now cited only as far as Cook et al. (1983) supports it; De Filippi and Wright (2018), whose two sentences were already this dissertation's inferences and now stand without the citation; Emanuel et al. (2000), whose list of requirements was cut, leaving the pilot's ethics on the Belmont principles and the federal rule; Frohlich et al. (1987), replaced by the authors' book on the same experiments (Frohlich & Oppenheimer, 1992), in which the cited findings were located; Mattson, Reddi, et al. (2020), a "see also" that was dropped; Molm (2003) and Rusbult and Van Lange (2003), whose sentences were cut as inessential; and Zwolinski (2012), whose position is now cited from the encyclopedia survey that reports it (Zwolinski et al., 2022). Cai et al. (2023) and Robbins et al. (2026) are now cited only for what their preprints, read in full, report: two clauses that rested on the published abstracts (speech recognition; the persistence of learning) were removed. Severinghaus et al. (1994) was confirmed from the article's opening page.

### C.3 Record located

None. Five works reached only this level in the earlier research passes because every route to their text was blocked (publisher paywalls, bot walls on repository copies, an access-restricted library scan). Each was either read in the final pass or removed; no work is cited at second hand:

- **Harsanyi (1953, 1955).** Read in full in the final pass, from scans of the journal articles. The equal-chance construction and the quoted phrases are on pp. 434–435 of the 1953 note; the weighted-sum theorem, the impersonal chooser and the convergence on the unweighted mean on pp. 313–316 and 320–321 of the 1955 paper; and the treatment of interpersonal comparison as factual inference with a conventional residue on pp. 316–321. Chapters 5 and 7 now cite both directly.
- **Wertheimer (1996).** Not opened, and no longer cited. The account of mutually advantageous exploitation and of the competitive-market benchmark rests on the encyclopedia survey by Zwolinski, Ferguson and Wertheimer (2022), read in full.
- **Balcı et al. (2023) and Kagan et al. (2023).** Two letters in *Neuron* whose text could not be opened. The sentence that reported the exchange was removed; Chapter 8 now treats the sentience description in Kagan et al. (2022) with caution on the standard of the National Academies (2021) assessment, which was read, and says that the caution is this dissertation's.

### C.4 Corrections made to the working draft

The verification passes found and corrected the following errors in the working draft from which this dissertation was developed.

1. The study cited as finding that a language model "introduced nearly nine times more new vulnerabilities than they fixed" compares vulnerabilities introduced by the model with those introduced by human developers; it does not count vulnerabilities fixed, and it studies ordinary issue resolution rather than security patching (Sajadi et al., 2025).
2. The "FLAWED" pattern was attributed to a secondary blog; its source is an industry white paper (Mierczuk et al., 2026), now cited directly and identified as non-peer-reviewed.
3. An AI-generated, unreviewed industry report was cited for the concept of software monoculture; the concept is now sourced to Geer et al. (2003) and Stamp (2004).
4. A sovereign-AI figure ("21 projects as of June 2026") could not be found in its cited source, which tracks 184 projects in 67 countries (Chavez et al., 2026).
5. The Wörgl currency experiment was described as having "measurably reduced local unemployment"; the evidence does not support that claim (Fisher, 1933; Greco, 2002; September, 2026).
6. The information-gain data-valuation paper was misattributed to "Hoang et al."; its authors are Sim, Zhang, Chan and Low (2020).
7. Shannon (1948) was cited as defining a unit of information *processed*; Shannon defines a unit of information, and the precedent for a processing unit is benchmark methodology (Mattson et al., 2020; Reddi et al., 2020).
8. Consumer-hardware claims cited to enthusiast websites and a blog are now sourced to Micron Technology (2025) and TrendForce (2025, 2026a, 2026b); an unverified claim about a shelved consumer GPU was removed.
9. The model-collapse result in *Physical Review Letters* was overstated: real data must be combined with regularization or a prior, and the "single data point" result holds only in specific examples (Jangjoo et al., 2026).
10. The claim that robotic data is "not human-generated" was corrected: large real-world robot datasets are still gathered largely through human demonstration (Khazatsky et al., 2024). (Item 33 records a further correction to this sentence.)
11. An informal blog post on a "95% robotics data gap" was removed; the number has no peer-reviewed source.
12. The open-weight model was claimed to make "every input auditable"; the model releases and the transparency index read for that chapter showed that leading open-weight models do not disclose their training data. The chapter has since been removed (C.6).
13. An organoid review was cited for claims its abstract does not contain; those claims are now sourced to primary research (Cai et al., 2023; Kagan et al., 2022; Robbins et al., 2026; Smirnova et al., 2023) and the National Academies (2021).
14. "Ibarra et al." corrected to Arrieta-Ibarra et al. (2018); Ostrom (1990), cited but missing from the reference list, added.
15. Rawls's original position was described as a negotiation in which designers "might land on either side"; that reasoning is Harsanyi's, and the mechanism actually proposed is closer to Buchanan and Tullock's veil of uncertainty. Chapter 5 now says so.
16. Only four of Ostrom's eight design principles were used, and one was mis-mapped; all eight are now mapped (Chapter 5).

A second, independent review of the completed manuscript compared every factual claim in the empirical chapters against the research notes and found a further set of problems, all now corrected. The most significant were these.

17. A finding about trust and fairness in reciprocal exchange was attributed to Molm (2003), whose cited article establishes only that the form of exchange changes how power is used; the claim was reduced to what the article supports.
18. The comparison level for alternatives was attributed to a modern review rather than to Thibaut and Kelley (1959), and Emerson's four balancing operations were stated inconsistently across chapters; both are corrected, and the operations are now named consistently.
19. Appendix A claimed that an unconditional survival floor raises the human party's bargained payoff by an additional half of its value in every exchange. A floor received whether or not agreement is reached raises both disagreement and agreement payoffs equally and leaves the division of surplus unchanged; the half-value shift arises only where the floor substitutes for something the counterparty would otherwise have supplied. Appendix A, Chapter 3 and Chapter 9 now say so. (C.8 later refined the substitution case by the provider's cost; see A.3.1.)
20. The sovereign-AI index was cited for a claim about the cultural motives of sovereign projects that it does not make, and for a claim about "most national efforts" that holds only for model projects disclosing their base; both are corrected.
21. The Wörgl experiment's end was attributed to the Administrative Court on the strength of sources that do not name it; the court is now sourced to the Vienna University of Economics and Business exhibition page, with the conflicting dates noted.
22. The MLPerf time-to-train claim was cited to the overview article rather than to the training-benchmark paper, and stated as a "finding" rather than an argument; both are corrected.
23. The rural-electrification baseline and the claim that the program was a works program were attributed to a source that does not make them; the baseline is now sourced to Lewis and Severnini (2020) and the works-program claim is presented as this dissertation's inference.
24. Several claims were stated more strongly than their sources allow, among them the correlated-error finding of Kim et al. (2025) (from one leaderboard dataset, about question answering rather than code), the convergence of patches in Mierczuk et al. (2026) (whose authors caution that the space of obvious fixes is narrow), the age band and causal status of the entry-level employment finding, the growth figure for data-center electricity, Skalse et al. (2022) (policy restriction is necessary, not sufficient, for an unhackable proxy), Kanter (1972), Lutz (1994), Elster (1995), Fritsch et al. (2024), Hadfield-Menell et al. (2017), Frohlich et al. (1987) and the International AI Safety Report (a report, not an opinion survey). Each is now stated within its source.
25. The front matter and Chapter 1 claimed that every source had been checked against the claim it supports. Works verified only at the level of their record had not been; the statements now say that each source was checked at the level recorded here.

A third pass opened, wherever a route existed, every work that the first two passes had verified only at the level of its record, and checked each citing sentence against it. It moved 52 of the 57 works at that level to full-text or abstract level, raised two economics sources from abstract to full text, added five works read in the course of the checks (Arrow, 2001; Bernanke, 2002; Freeman, 2023; Van Parijs, 2006; and the 2002 printing of Friedman), and made the following corrections.

26. Ackerman (1991) was cited for constitutional change "outside routine procedures," which the material read does not establish; the sentence now uses his distinction between constitutional and normal politics.
27. The United Nations veto was described in terms that went beyond the Charter ("drafted by the victors"); the sentence now states what articles 23, 24 and 27 provide.
28. The exchange-network experiments of Cook and Emerson (1978) and Cook et al. (1983) were summarized with a mechanism (committed actors stop exploring alternatives) that the sources do not state; the findings are now that power follows network position and that commitment impedes its use.
29. Cook (1998) was cited for the claim that strain consumes slack; the essay attributes resilience to redundancy and human adaptation, and the inference about strain is now marked as this dissertation's.
30. Elster (2000) was cited for a "vocabulary of delay mechanisms and supermajorities"; the sentence now states his precommitment account, with the delaying role of amendment procedures sourced to Elster (1995).
31. Faden and Beauchamp (1986) was described as the account that "dominates bioethics" and as requiring understanding and noncontrol outright; it is now "an influential account" requiring *substantial* understanding and *substantial* freedom from control, with pages.
32. Gesell's *Natural Economic Order* was dated as "first published" in 1916; the 1916 volume combined works of 1906 and 1911, and the sentence now says so.
33. Open X-Embodiment Collaboration (2024) was cited, in Chapters 2 and 7, for the claim that robot datasets are collected by teleoperation, which it does not state; that clause now rests on Khazatsky et al. (2024) alone.
34. Mueller (2002) was cited for the claim that the internet root was shaped by "a small set of insiders with limited public accountability"; the sentence now states the struggle among interests and the use of the root to regulate that the book describes.
35. E. Ostrom (1990) was described as studying "fisheries in Turkey and Canada" alongside her successes and as identifying eight principles shared by all long-enduring cases. The fisheries were cases of failure or fragility, Sri Lanka was omitted, and the book lists seven principles common to all enduring cases plus an eighth found in the larger ones (Table 3.1, p. 90); all three points are corrected.
36. Kanter (1972) was described as studying nineteenth-century communities "and 1960s communes" on equal terms; the longevity finding concerns the earlier communities, and the later communes are an extension.
37. Balcı et al. (2023) and Kagan et al. (2023), letters whose text could not be opened, were characterized as "disputing" claims and "defending" usage; Chapter 8 now says only what their records establish.
38. The early Harsanyi papers (1953, 1955) were cited for the equal-chance construction before they had been opened. They were read in the final pass (see C.3), and the Chapter 5 and Chapter 7 sentences were rewritten against them, with quotations and pages.
39. Wertheimer (1996), whose book could not be opened, was cited alone; the citing sentences now rest on Zwolinski et al. (2022), from which the claims were confirmed.
40. Solum (1992) was said to separate the legal from the metaphysical question of personhood; he uses concrete legal problems to test which philosophical claims make a practical difference, and the sentence now says that.
41. Silver et al. (2018) was said to have defeated "the strongest existing programs entirely through self-play"; the system defeated a world-champion program in each game, given no domain knowledge beyond the rules.
42. Smith et al. (2015) was generalized to "automated program-repair tools"; the study evaluated two tools, and both citing sentences now say so.
43. Egger et al. (2022) was cited for an economy "with substantial idle capacity"; the authors report only suggestive, indirect evidence of slack.
44. Kitchens and Fishback (2015) was cited for raising "agricultural output and productivity"; the abstract reports crop output and crop productivity, and the prevention of declines in overall farm output.
45. Polanyi's account of the 1930s and 1940s was summarized as "institutional reconstruction"; his great transformation includes fascism as well as socialism and the New Deal.
46. Vivalt et al. (2024), an unrefereed working paper, was called "the strongest recent experimental evidence," which was this dissertation's judgment; Chapter 7 now describes it as a large randomized experiment reported in a working paper not yet peer-reviewed.
47. The survival tier's departure from the canonical definition of basic income was acknowledged without engaging the canonical case against restricted-use, non-saveable currency; Chapter 4 now engages it directly (Van Parijs, 2006), and Chapter 3 records Radin's warning that prohibitions can harm those they protect.
48. Smaller precision edits: Higgs (1987) now says expansions came during national emergencies and left a residue of powers; Muennighoff et al. (2023) is stated in terms of epochs at a fixed compute budget; Armstrong et al. (2016) now includes the condition on risk-taking; Arute et al. (2019) gives the processor time (about 200 seconds); Fritsch et al. (2024) is scoped to three DAOs; Vincent et al. (2019) is one study, not "studies"; Verho et al. (2022) and Vivalt et al. (2024) now give their figures; Friedman and Schwartz (1963) is cited with Bernanke (2002); the rural-electrification precedent now cites Lewis and Severnini (2020) for the market's reluctance to serve rural areas.
49. Reference-list corrections: Arute et al. (2019) completed to APA 7 form; Muennighoff et al. (2023) given its published author order, editors, pages and DOI; Hume's essay given its edition and pages; the unverified page range for Ha and Schmidhuber (2018) removed and the proceedings details added; a DOI added to Elster (1995); Cook (1998) given its URL and revision note; Friedman cited to the 2002 printing actually read (p. xiv) instead of an unverified page range in the 1982 printing; Nissenbaum's *Privacy in context* re-dated to 2009, the year in every catalog checked; Vivalt et al. (2024) given its revision date; Streeck and Thelen (2005) cited to the editors' introduction, where the typology is developed; the ambiguous "Mattson et al., 2020" in item 7 resolved (the disambiguating initial was later dropped, C.6).
50. Of the five works that could not be opened in the earlier passes, Harsanyi (1953, 1955) were read and are cited directly. The other three (Balcı et al., 2023; Kagan et al., 2023; Wertheimer, 1996) were removed from the reference list, and their citing sentences now rest on sources that were read (National Academies, 2021; Zwolinski et al., 2022). No secondary ("as cited in") citation remains; see C.3.

A fourth pass sought a legal full text (an open-access publication, an author manuscript, a working-paper or preprint version, or an official document) for every work that the earlier passes had read only at the level of its abstract, and checked each citing sentence against the body of the work. It raised 118 works to full text, recording in C.1 where the version read was a preprint or working paper. The 39 that remain at abstract level are chiefly books and paywalled articles with no open copy. The pass made the following corrections.

51. Sentences that went beyond their sources were narrowed to what the full texts state: Adler et al. (2024) (credentials prove a person is real rather than an AI, one per person per issuer, not uniqueness); Arrieta-Ibarra et al. (2018) (all three sources of countervailing power must combine); Bengio et al. (2025) (expert views concern the next several years); Bommasani et al. (2010) (nested enterprises have the weakest evidence, Table 3, though the authors judge it moderately supportive); de Bromhead et al. Guo et al. (2024) (semantic diversity stays comparatively stable); Hadfield-Menell et al. (2017) (the operators must be sufficiently rational); Hartung et al. (2023) (a call, not a commitment); Hyun et al. (2020) (one review, five themes); Kataoka et al. (2024) (donors *may* be insufficiently informed); Khazatsky et al. (2024) (human labor, much of it teleoperation, rather than "largely" human demonstration); Koh and Liang (2017) (evidence of crafted training data, not a claim about payment); Lee et al. (2024) (an off-the-shelf model, with the point about human training text marked as this dissertation's); Lehdonvirta et al. (2024) (public cloud compute suited to training); Li et al. (2025) (the dependence of the estimate on its assumptions); Long et al. (2024) (the authors decline to argue that near-future systems will be moral patients, p. 25); MacKay (1992) (a misspecified model *may* misvalue data, flagged by MacKay as a possible weakness); the National Academies (2021) (recontacting donors is *sometimes* impossible); Nosek et al. (2018) (what pre-registration fixes in advance); Skalse et al. (2022) (proxies neither trivial nor equivalent); Stamp (2004) (the incentive toward monoculture); U.S. National Science Foundation (2023) (a solicitation, not funded programs); UNESCO (2025) (as its announcement describes it); and Abramitzky (2008) (no longer called "the best" study).
52. Robbins et al. (2026) and Cai et al. (2023) stay at abstract level: their preprints were read in full, but the clauses on the persistence of learning and on speech recognition rest on the published abstracts, which report results the preprints do not contain. The Neuralink registry title was completed.

A fifth pass took up the works still at abstract level, most of them books, and searched each for the specific passage cited. It read ten in full or in the relevant parts, among them Harsanyi (1975), Binmore et al. (1986), Higgs (1987), Lutz (1994) and Scheidel (2017). For sixteen books it found each cited passage in the work's own words. Thirteen works, chiefly paywalled articles, remain at abstract level, and every claim attributed to them is confined to what their abstracts state. The pass made these corrections.

53. Lutz (1994) is now cited for what the article states rather than "by report"; Severinghaus et al. (1994) no longer claims that rising carbon dioxide "would have signaled" the problem; Cai et al. (2023) is cited for a change in the tissue's functional connectivity, not a physical reshaping; Stigler (1971) is paraphrased in his terms (regulation acquired by the regulated industry); Popper is cited for falsifiability, with pre-registration as this dissertation's application of it; Emanuel et al. (2000) lists "value" as the article does; the Manson and O'Neill gloss ,"in a controlled way" not found in the text, was removed; and Molm (2003) is no longer glossed beyond its abstract.
54. An independent editorial review of the whole manuscript led to revisions of argument and prose that add no factual claim. The compute unit is now said to meet the requirement of independence from either side only conditionally. Exit is foundational to voluntary consent and to reciprocity, but cannot secure informed consent or fair terms. The convention's aggregation rule is recorded as an open problem. The floor's effect on bargaining is tied explicitly to a survival constraint. The pilot chapter (since removed, C.6) was described as testing the designed dependence of Proposition 4, not the priority of exit. The fallback when dependence fades is institutional enforcement, not character.

### C.5 Checks closed in the final pass

The earlier passes left a list of bibliographic and page-level checks open. The final bibliographic pass of October 2026 closed every item on that list against the source itself. The items that the later review passes (C.8 to C.11) left open are listed at the end of C.10 and C.11. Each check, and how it was settled:

- **Author lists.** Adler et al. (2024), Arute et al. (2019), Bengio et al. (2025), Bommasani et al. (2021), D. Guo et al. (2025), Hartung et al. (2023), Khazatsky et al. (2024), Reddi et al. (2020) and Touvron et al. (2023) were compared with their Crossref or DataCite records and now give the first nineteen authors, an ellipsis and the final author, as APA 7 requires; D. Guo et al. (2025) gained its issue number. The fourteen authors of Q. Wu et al. (2024) were confirmed against the arXiv record, and the title was corrected to the singular ."multi-agent conversation" No reference entry uses "et al."
- **Thibaut and Kelley (1959).** The comparison level for alternatives is defined on printed p. 21 (§ 2.5), now cited.
- **Faden and Beauchamp (1986).** The three conditions of autonomous action (pp. 241–273) and informed consent as autonomous authorization (pp. 277–280) were confirmed against the paginated text.
- **Rawls (1971).** The description of the original position and the veil of ignorance is on p. 12. The reading of the parties as effectively a single chooser is attributed to Freeman (2023), which states it, not to an unverified page of Rawls.
- **Pierson (2004), Piketty (2014), Zuboff (2019), Van Parijs and Vanderborght (2017).** Each claim was checked against the book's text: Pierson's self-reinforcing processes (p. 10) and threshold effects (pp. 82–83); Piketty's *r* > *g* and the growth of inherited wealth; Zuboff's definition of surveillance capitalism, now quoted with its location (C.8); and the definition of basic income as paid in cash, without conditions (pp. 4, 8). None of these citations uses a pin page that was not seen.
- **Streeck and Thelen (2005).** The editors' introduction was read in its open-access postprint, which carries the book's pagination. The definitions of layering, drift and conversion are in Table 1.1 (p. 31) and the modes are discussed on pp. 19–31, as now cited.
- **Solum (1992).** The article runs pp. 1231–1287. Read in full, it recasts the question whether artificial intelligence is *possible*, not the question of personhood as such, as concrete legal questions; the Chapter 5 sentence was corrected and given pages.
- **S.B. 1223 (2024).** Chaptered as ch. 887, Statutes of 2024; the reference entry now says so. The neural-data category sits in Cal. Civ. Code § 1798.140(ae)(1)(G), confirmed in the California Privacy Protection Agency's compilation; the chapters do not rely on the lettering.
- **Hume.** "Of the independency of parliament" first appeared in *Essays, Moral and Political* (1741), according to the editor's foreword and key to editions in the Miller edition, where the essay runs pp. 42–46. The citation is now 1741/1987.
- **Van Parijs (2006).** The definition and the contrast with restricted-use, non-saveable currency are on p. 8. The volume is edited by E. O. Wright, and its subtitle was corrected from the Library of Congress record.
- **Freeman (2023).** The Winter 2023 archived edition carries the substantive revision of 24 October 2023 and is the edition cited.
- **Ackerman (1991).** The distinction between constitutional and normal politics (Ch. 1, "Dualist Democracy") was confirmed; Chapters 5 and 7 use that distinction instead of the phrase ,"constitutional moment" which was not seen in the text read.
- **Polanyi (1944/2001).** Page citations now come from the 2001 Beacon edition: the fictitious commodities in Chapter 6 (pp. 71–80), and the 1930s transformation, with fascism, socialism and the New Deal, on pp. 3, 24 and 31. An earlier page range that did not support the clause was replaced.
- **Robbins et al. (2026).** The bioRxiv preprint was read in full. Chapter 8 cites it only for the result the preprint reports: training signals chosen by reinforcement learning mostly improved performance at a pole-balancing task. The clause on the persistence of the improvement, which rested on the published abstract, was removed (C.2), as was the word ,"substantially" which came from the release.
- **Lanier and Weyl (2018).** The essay was read. It adopts the term "data dignity" and proposes ,"mediators of individual data" volunteer organizations that negotiate data royalties or wages for their members; Chapter 2 now says so.
- **Olson (1982).** The implication is Olson's own: Chapter 4 (p. 75) predicts fast growth where totalitarian government or foreign occupation abolished distributional coalitions, and offers this as an explanation of the postwar "economic miracles" of Japan and West Germany. Chapter 6 now cites it.
- **Autor (2015).** The published article was read. The substitution and complementarity claim is on p. 5, tacit knowledge on p. 11, and the quoted route around it ("from context, abundant data, and applied statistics") on p. 23; the citations give those pages.
- **Wörgl.** Ottacher (2007), read in full, fixes the dates. The certificates were withdrawn by 15 September 1933 under threat of police enforcement (p. 57), and the Administrative Court dismissed the town's final appeal on 18 November 1933 (pp. 58–59). The National Bank's objection is on pp. 53–54. The Vienna exhibition attributes the mid-September end to the Administrative Court, which Ottacher's chronology does not; Chapter 4 records the discrepancy and cites each source for what it states.
- **The 2026 amendment to the AI Act.** It is Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (the "Digital Omnibus on AI"), read in the official text: published in the Official Journal on 24 July 2026, in force from 27 July 2026, and deferring the high-risk obligations to 2 December 2027 (Annex III) and 2 August 2028 (Annex I). It is now cited formally, alongside the two commentaries that describe it (Agustinoy, 2026; ERTICO, 2026).

### C.6 Removal of the pilot chapter

On 2 November 2026 the author removed the pilot chapter (formerly Chapter 7, "The Prototype: A Closed Agrarian Pilot") and its draft pre-registration protocol (formerly Appendix B) in order to keep the dissertation theoretical. Designing an experiment capable of testing the theory is a project in its own right, and it is now described as future work in Chapters 7 and 9 rather than specified in the text. The removed chapter and appendix are preserved in the project archive. The chapters and appendices that followed them were renumbered: Limitations is Chapter 7, Convergence Chapter 8, the Conclusion Chapter 9, the glossary Appendix B and this ledger Appendix C.

Twenty-one works that were cited only in the removed parts were taken out of the reference list and from the lists in C.1: Barron-Gafford et al. (2019); Bommasani et al. (2023); Diener et al. (1985); T. Guo et al. (2024); Hossain et al. (2019); Hu et al. (2022); Jiang et al. (2023); Kanter (1972); Manheim and Garrabrant (2018); McCambridge et al. (2014); the National Commission for the Protection of Human Subjects of Biomedical and Behavioral Research (1979); Nosek et al. (2018); OECD (2013); Perdomo et al. (2020); Peters et al. (2016); Popper (1959/2002); Protection of Human Subjects (2018); Severinghaus et al. (1994); Skalse et al. (2022); Touvron et al. (2023); and Q. Wu et al. (2024). Popper (1959/2002) was restored in the review pass of C.8, which cites it in Section 1.7 at the level previously recorded. Works the pilot shared with other chapters (among them Abramitzky, 2008; Amodei et al., 2016; Drexler, 2019; Gatch, 2012; Hirschman, 1970; Kapoor et al., 2024; Shumailov et al., 2024) remain cited and remain in the list. The entries in C.2, C.3, C.4 and C.5 that refer to works cited only in the pilot (among them Kanter, 1972; Nosek et al., 2018; Skalse et al., 2022; Touvron et al., 2023; Q. Wu et al., 2024) describe the manuscript as it stood when each correction was made, and are kept as a record of the verification history. Since the removal of Q. Wu et al. (2024) the reference list has one Wu first author, and Wu et al. (2023) is cited without the disambiguating initial; the single Mattson entry is likewise cited as Mattson et al. (2020).

### C.7 Terminology pass and foreword

On the same date the author fixed the dissertation's vocabulary for the artificial side of the exchange (Section 1.2): *synthetic intelligence* as the general term, *superintelligence* as the capability threshold, and *large language model* with an explicit statement that such a model is a trained artifact rather than an independent living entity. The "sub-AGI" shorthand for the designed-incentive regime was retired. No source was added or removed. The two sentences in Chapters 1 and 5 that report Drexler (2019) as modeling advanced systems as bounded services now name his subject as superintelligence, which is the word of his title and of the front matter that was read (C.1); the two further references in Sections 3.3 and 5.6, which use his bounded-services model for the problem of counting synthetic parties, say ."advanced synthetic intelligence" The claim attributed to him is unchanged, and the clause ,"with humans retaining strategic control" not found in the front matter read, was removed from Section 5.2. A foreword in the author's voice was added before the abstract. It cites nothing and states the author's conviction without the qualifications the body keeps.

### C.8 Review pass of 2 November 2026

A review of the whole manuscript for logical flaws, unsupported claims, citation form and non-academic prose was conducted after the terminology pass, and its findings were applied on the same day. The changes that bear on sources are these. Popper (1959/2002) was restored to the reference list and to the confirmed-passage list of C.1, cited in Section 1.7 for the requirement that a theory say in advance what would count against it. Article 108 of the Charter of the United Nations was read and is cited in Section 5.1 for the amendment rule. Zuboff's definition of surveillance capitalism, previously paraphrased, is now quoted with its location. The Friedman (1962/2002, p. xiv) sentence is now quoted. The Weidinger et al. (2023, Study 4) finding is reported as a null result on reflective endorsement rather than as endorsement becoming less firm (C.10 corrects the description of the comparison). The Cai et al. (2023) sentences in Chapter 8 were returned to the "functional connectivity" wording recorded under item 53. Chavez et al. (2026) is no longer cited for chip-supply concentration, which rests on Sastry et al. (2024). Hirschman's ,"political action par excellence" Keynes's "unduly neglected prophet" and Radin's "double bind" are paraphrased rather than quoted, because the pages were not recorded. Claims of scholarly dispute (Fishback), of prevalence (,"most fiction" ,"prevailing approach" "the strongest evidence") and of "unprecedented" or "most influential" standing were removed or hedged, and every claim that is the dissertation's own inference rather than a source's finding is now marked as such. In the formal chapter, the substitution case of Appendix A was corrected to model the provider's cost, Propositions 2 and 3 were restated so that no clause contradicts the definitions, the outside-option gloss was corrected, the asymmetric Nash solution was stated so that the impatience result is a derivation, and the regime typology was made explicit about the combinations it names. Ten reference entries for proceedings papers carry the DOI of the arXiv version; the entries remain as they are, and C.1 records the version read for each.

### C.9 Second verification pass

A second pass on the same day checked the edits of C.8 for errors they might have introduced and re-examined the formal core. The changes that bear on sources are these. Article 108 of the Charter of the United Nations and the time-preference result of Binmore et al. (1986) on which Appendix A.3 now rests are recorded in the research notes. A citation of Funke et al. (2016) and Scheidel (2017) added in C.8 for a claim neither makes, that the harm of a transition falls in its passage, was removed and the claim marked as the dissertation's reading. The Wörgl unemployment figure is now attributed to Gelleri (2009) as a repeated claim that September (2026) traces to movement sources, and the citations for the withdrawal, the court's dismissal and the exhibition's account are given separately for what each source states. A clause asserting that surplus cannot be divided efficiently under private information, which would have required a source not in the list, was removed. K. S. Cook et al. (1983) and R. I. Cook (1998) now carry initials, since the list has two Cook first authors. In the formal chapter, clause C(ii) was reworded so that refusal means entering no exchange in place of the one refused, which makes Proposition 1's identification of C(ii) with E(i) exact; Proposition 4 states the premise on which self-enforcement of terms, as distinct from protection of the input supply, depends; the insufficiency thesis is stated for clause E(i) only, and the scope thesis, Sections 8.5 and 9.2 claim no more than that; the regime typology is stated at clause level and withdraws the claim to partition the eight combinations; and the Foreword now says which of its claims the body argues.

### C.10 Third verification pass

A third pass on the same day checked every in-text citation against the research notes, every internal cross-reference against its target, the arithmetic of Chapter 4 and Appendix A, and the rendered exports. The changes that bear on sources are these. Weidinger et al. (2023) was re-read at the Study 4 results: the veil still raised the choice of the prioritarian principle (odds ratio 4.5, p = .049, N = 213 after exclusions) and the difference in reflective endorsement between conditions was not significant (p = .21); the comparison is within Study 4, not across studies, and Chapters 5 and 9 now say so. Landauer (1961) and Lloyd (2000) are no longer cited for substrate-specific conversion rates, which they do not give; Section 4.2 cites them for the physical bounds only. Solum (1992) is cited for the hypothetical examination of legal personhood, not for present doctrine. Gatch (2012) is cited for tax receivability only; the legal-tender clause is the dissertation's. Gerstgrasser et al. (2024) and Kazdan et al. (2024) are cited for accumulation only, and Alemohammad et al. (2024) and Shumailov et al. (2024) for the fresh-supply disjunct. D. Guo et al. (2025) is no longer cited for expert-level performance, which the paper does not claim. The Pew figure of more than a third is now attributed to the July 2026 sample, as the source states. Longpre et al. (2024) is paraphrased in the authors' terms ("inconsistent"; protocols not designed for the purpose) rather than as ."largely unenforced" Farrell and Klemperer (2007) is paraphrased from its abstract. Hayek is cited as 1976/1990, since the third edition was the text read. Polanyi's C.1 entry now records the 2001 edition from which the page citations come. Section 6.4 cites Polanyi at pp. 71–80 for the strain of commodification as the other sections do. The Elster (1995) attribution of delays and amendment procedures rests on the full-text read recorded in C.4 item 30; the research note does not record the passage, and the page is an open item. The three-author Brynjolfsson works are cited with all authors, as APA 7 requires. Quotations whose wording the notes do not record were paraphrased (E. Ostrom, 1990, p. 90). Claims the notes do not support were reworded to what they do support or marked as the dissertation's: the REA's separability from the New Deal, beneficial-ownership registries, the historical control of infrastructure by capital, the clearinghouse trend, the Cox et al. (2010) ratio, the Robbins et al. (2026) "no training" condition, the Autor (2015) ,"two routes" the Lutz (1994) length–longevity association, and the Kim et al. (2025) evaluation result. In the formal chapter and Appendix A, the dependency between clauses R(ii) and C(i) was reversed to the direction the definitions support; Proposition 3 states the E(iii)–C(iii) exception; the credit channel is bounded only when the interest rate is strictly below the demurrage rate; the symmetric-case bounds of Section 3.6 are qualified to equal patience; and the substitution case states its positive-surplus proviso. In Chapter 4, information-gain pricing carries a sixth qualification on definability, and the redemption obligation is stated with its par anchor and the condition that the dividend share not exceed the obligated share. The glossary gained an entry for domination and corrections to eight others. The Arrow (2001) page range was taken from the contents list of the volume, as its research note records. Open items: page ranges for Gatch (2012), Korinek and Stiglitz (2019), Mattson et al. (2020) and Perry et al. (2023); and research-note entries for Freeman (2023), Bernanke (2002), Agustinoy (2026) and ERTICO (2026), which are recorded in C.1 only; and the ten proceedings entries that carry arXiv DOIs (C.8).

### C.11 Fourth verification pass

A fourth pass on the same day checked the edits of C.10, examined the argument itself rather than its sources, and copyedited every sentence against the prose standard. No source was added or removed. The changes that bear on the argument are these. The insufficiency thesis is now stated for a party that has no exit option, since a sub-floor party that can leave for another provider is not dominated by any single counterparty under the stipulated definition; Sections 1.2, 8.5 and 9.2 and the glossary carry the qualification. The definition of domination is stated as symmetric in form, and the Foreword now says that the body argues one of its claims and stipulates two. The sufficiency conjecture is stated as the claim that character adds nothing once terms bind, with enforceability as its open premise, and the designed-incentive regime is bounded by accountability as well as by the provenance of the objective. Proposition 2 is stated as bounding reciprocity from below rather than establishing its strict form, and the summaries no longer say that exit disciplines reciprocity. Proposition 3's ordering is stated as partial, with exit prior and consent and reciprocity unordered with respect to each other, and Section 3.7 records that at the collective level the foundation is voice. Section 4.5 states how the floor goods that are not compute are delivered, names the acceptance obligation as a legal-tender rule, and records the circularity that the disagreement payoff is delivered by capacity owners who are also counterparties; Section 4.6 states that demurrage has no distributive effect of its own without the capacity and credit rules; Section 4.4 limits the par obligation to the reference workload and ties the fixity of the share to re-basings. Section 5.3 states what anonymity removes, the organization of a bloc rather than the motive to vote one's interest; Section 5.5 states that strong blindness adds nothing for principals and capacity owners; Section 5.6 no longer claims that the eligibility criterion presupposes none of the contested classification; Section 5.7 states what the symbiosis standard would require of the design and that the design does not yet meet it. Section 6.6 states that a floor financed through existing instruments lacks the properties Chapter 3 argued for, and Section 6.7 that a window opened by correlated failure would favor any design that distributes control. Section 8.3 assigns the donor's withdrawal of consent to clause C(iii) rather than to exit, and Section 8.5 states that Chapter 3's definitions do not yet reach a tissue-based party in its own right. Section 7.7 no longer attributes a dynamic to Propositions 1 to 4. The copyedit removed intensifiers and figurative phrasing, regularized numerals (words below ten, numerals from ten and for decimals and percentages, "percent" spelled out), serial commas (none for short items), quotation punctuation (inside), American spellings, the square-bracket form for EU regulations in parentheticals, and the run-in form of the headings in Section 5.9. Open items are as listed at the end of C.10.
